Let geometry remove a reaction

A cylinder rolls along a horizontal rail while a spring attached at its centre pulls it towards equilibrium. The rail friction is essential for rolling, but an ideal contact point is instantaneously at rest and the friction force does no work. One can write separate translational and rotational equations with an unknown friction force. Alternatively choose a coordinate that satisfies rolling from the beginning. The reduced energy then contains the rotational inertia without introducing a contact reaction.

Take the rightward displacement \(x\) of the cylinder centre as independent coordinate and clockwise angle as positive. On the fixed surface the no-slip condition gives \(x=R\theta\), up to a reference offset. A body of total mass \(m\) and centre inertia \(I_G\) has kinetic energy from both translation and rotation. A centre spring with stiffness \(k\) has elastic potential \(kx^2/2\).

\[T=\tfrac12(m+I_G/R^2)\dot x^2,\]
\[V=\tfrac12kx^2 .\]

Connect energy to a dynamical equation

The virtual work of a horizontal force \(F\) applied at the centre is \(F\delta x\). In this coordinate its generalised force is simply \(F\). A torque \(M\) acting clockwise would do \(M\delta\theta=(M/R)\delta x\) and would enter as a force \(M/R\). With a centre damper \(c\), the dissipative force is \(-c\dot x\). Lagrange’s equation for this system becomes

\[\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\partial T}{\partial\dot x}-\frac{\partial T}{\partial x}+\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}=F-c\dot x,\qquad (m+I_G/R^2)\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=F .\]

For coordinates whose inertia depends on position, retain both derivatives involving \(T\). The differentiation of \(\partial T/\partial\dot x\) then generates a term proportional to \(\dot x^2\), part of which cancels \(\partial T/\partial x\). Simply multiplying a configuration-dependent effective mass by acceleration would miss the remaining term.

Predict a frequency and check rolling

A solid cylinder has \(m=8.0\,\mathrm{kg}\), \(R=0.10\,\mathrm m\), and \(I_G=mR^2/2=0.040\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). The effective mass seen by the spring is

\[8+0.040/0.10^2=12.0\,\mathrm{kg}.\]

With \(k=480\,\mathrm{N/m}\) and negligible damping the natural frequency is

\[\sqrt{480/12}/(2\pi)=1.007\,\mathrm{Hz}.\]

Release it from rest at \(x=+0.060\,\mathrm m\). Its initial acceleration is

\[-480(0.060)/12=-2.40\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

Writing only the centre’s translation and ignoring rotation would overpredict the acceleration magnitude.

Reconstruct the previously eliminated contact force from translation. The spring pulls leftwards with 28.8 N and the centre accelerates leftwards with \(m\ddot x=-19.2\,\mathrm N\). Friction must therefore point rightwards with 9.6 N to supply the appropriate angular acceleration. The coefficient of static friction must be at least \(9.6/(8\times9.81)=0.122\) at release. For a smaller coefficient, the assumed rolling constraint fails and the model needs separate rotational and translational coordinates together with a slipping contact law.

Compare two cylinders with identical mass

A thin-walled hoop of the same mass and rolling radius has \(I_G=mR^2\). With the original spring its effective mass doubles the translational mass, giving natural frequency \(\sqrt{480/16}/(2\pi)=0.872\,\mathrm{Hz}\). A no-slip ring-down can therefore distinguish two bodies whose masses and outer dimensions are identical but whose internal mass distributions differ.

Use a cyclic coordinate to reduce coupled motion

Mount a pendulum on a cart that can roll horizontally on a level frictionless guide. Let cart displacement be \(x\), bob angle \(\theta\) from downward vertical, length \(l\), bob mass \(m\) and remaining cart mass \(M\). The bob position is \((x+l\sin\theta,-l\cos\theta)\). Differentiating and squaring its speed gives a cross term \(2l\dot x\dot\theta\cos\theta\) because cart and pendulum velocities need not be perpendicular. The full energy terms are

\[T=\tfrac12(M+m)\dot x^2+ml\cos\theta\,\dot x\dot\theta+\tfrac12ml^2\dot\theta^2,\]
\[V=mgl(1-\cos\theta).\]

Horizontal position itself does not occur in either expression. It is a cyclic coordinate, so \(\partial T/\partial\dot x=(M+m)\dot x+ml\cos\theta\,\dot\theta\) is conserved if no external horizontal force acts. This is horizontal momentum written in generalised coordinates. The pendulum equation from Lagrange’s equation is

\[l\ddot\theta+\ddot x\cos\theta+g\sin\theta=0.\]

For small swings from rest, differentiate the momentum relation and linearise to obtain

\[\ddot x=-ml\ddot\theta/(M+m).\]

Substituting gives

\[[lM/(M+m)]\ddot\theta+g\theta=0.\]

Free horizontal motion makes the small-swing frequency larger than for a clamped cart, by \(\sqrt{(M+m)/M}\). This result reflects motion about the system’s horizontally constrained centre of mass. The extra degree of freedom also permits uniform horizontal translation at zero frequency. Its presence does not imply an additional oscillatory mode.

Predict opposing cart travel in a release test

Let \(M=3.0\) kg, \(m=1.0\) kg and \(l=0.60\) m. Clamp the cart: the oscillation period would be \(2\pi\sqrt{0.60/9.81}=1.55\,\mathrm s\). Release its horizontal guide and the period becomes

\[2\pi\sqrt{0.60(3)/(9.81\times4)}=1.35\,\mathrm s.\]

At release the bob is at \(\theta_0=10^\circ\) and both bodies are at rest. Because horizontal momentum remains zero, integrate the exact relation to obtain

\[(M+m)x+ml\sin\theta=\text{constant}.\]

As the bob first crosses the vertical, the cart has shifted \(ml\sin10^\circ/(M+m)=0.0260\,\mathrm m\) to the right from its starting point.

At the vertical the potential-energy decrease is

\[mgl(1-\cos10^\circ)=0.0894\,\mathrm J.\]

Substituting \(\dot x=-ml\dot\theta/(M+m)\) into the kinetic energy at this instant gives effective angular inertia \(ml^2M/(M+m)=0.270\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\). Thus the angular speed magnitude is \(\sqrt{2(0.0894)/0.270}=0.814\,\mathrm{rad/s}\), and the cart speed magnitude is \(0.15(0.814)=0.122\,\mathrm{m/s}\). The frequency estimate used a small-angle approximation; the centre-of-mass and energy results at the vertical used the actual release angle. A horizontal cable pulling on the cart or measurable wheel friction would change the conserved momentum and should be measured before attributing deviations solely to pendulum nonlinearity.

The two-sensor experiment can measure conservation without estimating acceleration. Track the cart position and bob angle from video, then plot \(x+[ml/(M+m)]\sin\theta\). For the chosen masses its value should stay constant at the initial offset despite the cart and pendulum both moving. A steadily sloping line indicates nonzero initial horizontal momentum; a curved departure suggests an external force or tracking bias. This separation is useful because rolling resistance can be weak enough to leave the first measured period nearly unchanged while producing visible drift over several cycles. The conserved combination is specific to the inertial laboratory coordinate, so a camera fixed on the moving cart needs a separate record of cart displacement.

Why a radial slide presses sideways on a rotating guide

Machine dynamics

A low-friction carriage moves along a radial slot in a horizontal turntable. It can move at constant speed relative to the guide and still require a changing force. Keeping track of the moving basis exposes both acceleration components.

Differentiate the direction vectors

Let \(r(t)\) be displacement from the turntable centre along the guide and \(\theta(t)\) the angle of the guide. In fixed Cartesian components the radial unit vector is \((\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\) and the tangential vector is \((-\sin\theta,\cos\theta)\). Differentiating shows \(\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta\mathbf e_\theta\) and \(\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\mathbf e_r\). Apply the product rule to the position \(r\mathbf e_r\) to obtain the velocity and then differentiate both terms.

\[\mathbf v=\dot r\mathbf e_r+r\dot\theta\mathbf e_\theta,\]
\[\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)\mathbf e_r+(r\ddot\theta+2\dot r\dot\theta)\mathbf e_\theta .\]

One contribution to \(2\dot r\dot\theta\) comes from differentiating the radially directed velocity as its basis turns. The other comes from differentiating the magnitude of the tangential velocity \(r\dot\theta\). For positive rotation and outward travel, both point in the positive tangential direction. The expression already gives the acceleration in an inertial frame. Multiplying by mass gives the net real force required on the carriage.

Check sideways force with angular momentum and energy

Hold the angular speed at \(\Omega\), and drive the radial position as \(r=r_0+ut\) with constant u. Gravity and support cancel vertically. The net radial drive force is \(-mr\Omega^2\) and the guide must exert a side force \(2mu\Omega\). The resulting torque about the axis is \(2mru\Omega\). This equals the derivative of the angular momentum \(mr^2\Omega\), providing an independent sign and factor-of-two check.

\[P_r+P_\theta=(-mr\Omega^2)u+(2mu\Omega)(r\Omega)=mru\Omega^2={d\over dt}\left[{m\over2}(u^2+r^2\Omega^2)\right].\]

The side force does work in the laboratory since its contact point moves tangentially. Describing a smooth slot as doing zero work without specifying the frame would lose this energy transfer. The radial actuator absorbs power during outward constant-speed travel.

Forces and power on two passes

A 0.40 kg carriage moves from \(r=0.10\) m outwards at 0.20 m/s, with \(\Omega=5\) rad/s. At \(t=0.50\) s it reaches 0.20 m. Its radial force is −2.0 N, tangential force is +0.80 N and net mechanical power is

\[−2(0.20)+0.80(1)=0.40\,\mathrm{W}.\]

Setting u to zero removes the side force, while the guide still requires a radial restraint to maintain circular travel.

On an inward pass at −0.20 m/s, evaluated at the same radius, the radial force remains −2.0 N. The side force reverses and the turntable now receives angular momentum from the carriage. The magnitude of each force depends on a different combination of radius, radial speed and angular speed. Use the trace to distinguish the constant side load from the changing inward force.

Set radial speed to zero before changing angular speed. Restore outward motion and predict how doubling angular speed changes the two force components at the same radius. Use mechanical power as a separate sign check.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · reconstruct angular acceleration from a side load

At one instant the carriage is at 0.20 m, with mass 0.40 kg, \(\dot r=0.10\) m/s and \(\dot\theta=4\) rad/s. A tangential force transducer measures +0.48 N. Estimate \(\ddot\theta\) and explain what a rigidly fixed carriage would read at the same instant.

Show the reconstruction

The tangential acceleration is

\[0.48/0.40=1.20\,\mathrm{m/s^2}.\]

Subtract \(2(0.10)(4)=0.80\) m/s² to isolate \(r\ddot\theta=0.40\) m/s². Thus \(\ddot\theta=2.0\) rad/s². With no radial sliding the second term disappears and the side load falls to \(0.40(0.20)(2.0)=0.16\) N. The radial centripetal component points along the guide and does not enter this reading.

Further reading · MIT 2.003SC · velocity, acceleration and rotational motion

When a suspended capacitor loses its stable equilibrium

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

An electrode suspended on a spring can remain motionless at a bias that makes the calculated electrostatic force grow as it moves. Static force balance alone misses the voltage at which that equilibrium disappears. Deriving the small-signal frequency gives an independent measurement of the same instability.

Start with the electrical and mechanical control variables

A movable conducting plate of area \(A\) faces a fixed electrode. Its unstressed gap is \(g\). Define positive displacement \(x\) toward the fixed plate, with spring force \(-kx\), mass \(m\) and viscous damping coefficient \(b\). For \(0\le x<g\) assume ideal parallel plates, negligible tilt and fringing, constant \(\epsilon\), and a linear elastic spring. The capacitance is \(C(x)=\epsilon A/(g-x)\). The model ends when the plates approach contact or when the suspending structure departs from its specified elastic law. It cannot by itself calculate adhesion, contact resistance or post-contact motion.

Before differentiating electrical energy specify whether the source holds voltage or charge. At fixed charge \(Q\), electrical energy is \(Q^2/[2C(x)]\), and its negative displacement derivative gives the force toward increasing \(x\). At fixed voltage \(V\), a source supplies \(VdQ\) as the plates move. The appropriate potential for mechanical equilibrium is the spring energy plus the field energy minus \(QV\), which here is \(\mathcal U_V=kx^2/2-C(x)V^2/2\). Differentiating gives the outward spring reaction and the attractive electrical force \(F_e=V^2C'(x)/2=\epsilon A V^2/[2(g-x)^2]\). An independent derivation multiplies the conductor pressure \(\epsilon E^2/2\) by area with \(E=V/(g-x)\). Both give a positive force. Replacing the source potential with the stored energy at fixed voltage would reverse its sign.

Locate all equilibria before choosing a branch

Define \(s=x/g\) and \(\Lambda=\epsilon A V^2/(2kg^3)\). The static force equation reduces to

\[\Lambda=s(1-s)^2.\]

At a specified voltage one can find \(s\) by scanning the curve \(s\mapsto s(1-s)^2\) over the physically admissible interval \(0\le s<1\). Its derivative is \((1-s)(1-3s)\), giving an interior maximum \(4/27\) at \(s=1/3\). For \(0<\Lambda<4/27\) there are two physical roots. The smaller is a local minimum of \(\mathcal U_V\) while the larger is a local maximum. Their merger is a fold of the static equilibrium branch:

\[x_{\rm pi}=g/3,\]
\[V_{\rm pi}=\sqrt{\frac{8kg^3}{27\epsilon A}} .\]

The often-quoted one-third displacement is the result for a linear spring, ideal voltage drive and this capacitance geometry. It is not a universal travel limit determined by the gap alone. Additional mechanical restoring forces or a different electrical boundary condition change the fold. Neither positive net electrical force nor the mere existence of a real solution is evidence of stable operation. A root finder started near the almost closed gap can converge to the unstable large-displacement root with an extremely small static residual.

The two branches can be distinguished experimentally without deliberately reaching contact. At static equilibrium \(kx=F_e\). Linearise the total force about \(x_0\) while maintaining voltage at its prescribed value. The incremental restoring stiffness and corresponding weak-damping natural frequency are

\[k_{\rm eff}=k-\frac{\epsilon A V^2}{(g-x_0)^3}=k\,\frac{1-3s_0}{1-s_0},\]
\[f_{\rm small}=\frac1{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{k_{\rm eff}}m}.\]

A small push toward the fixed electrode is restored only when \(k_{\rm eff}>0\). The familiar electrostatic softening is the reason the smaller equilibrium is stable. The small-signal frequency tends to zero as the fold is approached. With appreciable damping the observed amplitude resonance and ringdown oscillation have slightly different frequencies; fit the full mechanical response rather than identifying an arbitrary peak with the undamped \(f_{\rm small}\).

Background · Can a sudden step pull in a statically stable actuator?

An ideal voltage step applied to a plate initially at rest can push it past the unstable equilibrium. During the motion at fixed voltage the effective potential per \(kg^2\) is \(u(s)=s^2/2-b/[2(1-s)]\), where \(s=x/g\) and \(b=\epsilon A V^2/(kg^3)\). Neglect damping and begin at \(s=0\). At the dynamical threshold the initial energy equals the saddle energy and the slope vanishes at that saddle.

\[u(s)=u(0)\ \Longrightarrow\ b=s(1-s),\]
\[u'(s)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ b=2s(1-s)^2.\]

The two equations meet at \(s=1/2\), \(b=1/4\). The quasistatic fold gives \(b=8/27\). Consequently the undamped step threshold is only \(\sqrt{27/32}\simeq0.919\) times the slow-ramp pull-in voltage. A device operated between the two voltages can survive a ramp but collapse after an abrupt step. Dissipation and the actual rise time change this prediction.

Reconstruct the prepared state from a frequency measurement

Take a 100 μm square electrode in vacuum, \(g=2.00\) μm, \(k=2.00\) N/m and \(m=5.00\times10^{-10}\) kg. The predicted pull-in voltage is 7.32 V, the zero-bias capacitance 44.3 fF and the zero-bias natural frequency 10.07 kHz. On the low branch \(s_0=0.20\) gives \(x_0=0.400\) μm and voltage 6.80 V. Then \(k_{\rm eff}=1.00\) N/m and the small-signal frequency is 7.12 kHz. A measured frequency ratio \(f/f_0\) can be inverted via \(s=(1-r)/(3-r)\), where \(r=(f/f_0)^2\). The result is independent of absolute optical displacement calibration if the mass and underlying spring stiffness have not changed during the bias sweep.

At the same voltage the other physical root is \(s=0.48769\). A numerical force balance at this root would give the same supply reading but a negative tangent stiffness. The ideal voltage-controlled potential in units \(kg^2\) is \(u(s)=s^2/2-\Lambda/(1-s)\). The barrier height \(kg^2[u(0.48769)-u(0.20)]\) for this example is \(7.26\times10^{-14}\) J. A sufficiently energetic disturbance can carry the plate out of the stable basin even below static pull-in. This is different from loss of the static equilibrium itself. A quasistatic model predicts the existence and local stiffness of the equilibrium; a transient simulation must additionally specify initial displacement, velocity, drive ramp and damping.

Change the preparation to fixed charge

Disconnect a perfect isolated capacitor after placing a known charge \(Q\) on it, neglecting leakage and parasitic capacitance. Then \(F_Q=Q^2/(2\epsilon A)\) is independent of the remaining gap, because \(D=Q/A\) is fixed. Static equilibrium is \(x_0=Q^2/(2\epsilon A k)\), with incremental stiffness exactly \(k\). At the same initial voltage and gap the instantaneous force agrees with the voltage-controlled value, since \(Q=C(x_0)V\). The derivatives governing subsequent motion differ. At \(x_0=0.400\) μm the required charge in the numerical example is \(0.376\) pC. After ideal electrical isolation the small-signal frequency returns to the underlying elastic value even though the loaded static position stays the same.

Ideal charge isolation is delicate in a small device. A fixed parasitic capacitance \(C_p\) from the isolated node to a reference means the conserved charge belongs to \(C(x)+C_p\). Motion changes the fraction of total charge residing on the actuator. The electrical energy becomes \(Q_{\rm total}^2/[2(C+C_p)]\), so the force is \(Q_{\rm total}^2 C'/[2(C+C_p)^2]\), generally displacement dependent. If \(C_p\gg C\), the node voltage changes little with a small displacement and behavior is closer to voltage control. Interpreting an observed softening after disconnecting the supply as evidence against electromagnetic force balance would overlook this boundary change.

A different practical preparation puts an intentional series capacitor \(C_s\) between a fixed voltage supply and the actuator. Its series combination is \(C_{\rm ser}=\epsilon A/[g-x+\epsilon A/C_s]\). For static mechanical work at fixed supply voltage use \(F=V_s^2 C_{\rm ser}'/2\). Defining an effective electrical spacing \(g_e=g+\epsilon A/C_s\), the static fold occurs at \(x=g_e/3\) if this lies within the open physical gap. If \(\epsilon A/C_s\ge2g\), contact would occur before any such static fold in the ideal model. This calculation shows a direct connection between passive circuit design and allowed mechanical travel, while making clear that the maximum actuator voltage can differ from the voltage printed on the supply.

Why a sudden voltage change can reach contact below static pull-in

For a rapid electrical step the plate has a position and velocity inherited from the previous preparation. Suppose it starts at rest at its zero-bias position, the supply steps to a constant \(V\) much faster than a mechanical period, and mechanical damping is initially negligible. Immediately after the step, energy in the mechanical potential \(u(s)=s^2/2-\Lambda/(1-s)\) is \(u(0)=-\Lambda\) in units of \(kg^2\). Motion under this constant voltage can reach the force barrier if the initial value exceeds the potential at the unstable stationary point. This question can be solved by comparing two energies without integrating an entire time history.

Let \(s_b\) be the unstable root of \(\Lambda=s_b(1-s_b)^2\). At threshold the initial state is on the separatrix, the trajectory separating oscillations in the well from excursions to the closing gap. Set \(u(0)=u(s_b)\), subtract the two expressions and cancel \(s_b\) from the nontrivial solution. The two conditions become

\[\Lambda={s_b(1-s_b)\over2},\]
\[\Lambda=s_b(1-s_b)^2 .\]

Thus \(s_b=1/2\), \(\Lambda=1/8\), and the ideal undamped step threshold is

\[V_{\rm step}/V_{\rm pi}=\sqrt{27/32}=0.919.\]

In our example this is 6.72 V. A slow increase to 6.80 V leads to the observed stable 0.40 μm deflection, whereas a sudden ideal voltage step to the same value from rest at the origin contains enough energy to escape the well. Starting on the biased equilibrium before applying a small perturbation is a different experiment. The direction of the threshold shift makes physical sense: damping removes mechanical energy during the first excursion, and a slow ramp allows the equilibrium to move with the plate. The undamped value specifies a particular ideal initial-value experiment, rather than a voltage rating for an unspecified drive waveform.

One can check a simulated step by monitoring \(m\dot{x}^2/2+\mathcal U_V\) after the supply settles. With viscous damping its derivative is \(-b\dot{x}^2\), so any sustained increase during a constant-voltage segment points to a numerical or modelling error. Before the step ends there is an explicit drive term \(-CV\dot V\) in this energy balance. The mechanical potential already includes the source work. Adding \(V\dot Q\) to the same balance a second time gives an artificial energy imbalance. A transient solver should stop at a physically specified minimum gap instead of marching across the singularity; a statement about contact time depends on what counts as contact.

A finite source resistance changes what a ringdown measures

A dc supply with series resistance \(R\) can maintain the static voltage accurately while failing to hold the actuator voltage fixed over a mechanical cycle. Let its steady node voltage be \(V\), with equilibrium capacitance \(C=C(x_0)\). A small sinusoidal displacement \(\delta x\) causes current \(i\omega(C\,\delta v+VC'\delta x)\) into the actuator. The small-signal supply voltage is zero, so \(\delta v=-R i\). Eliminating current gives

\[\delta v=-\frac{V C'}C\frac{i\omega\tau}{1+i\omega\tau}\delta x,\]
\[\tau=RC .\]

Linearise \(F_e=V^2 C'/2\) allowing both voltage and position to vary. The direct derivative is \(K_{e0}=V^2C''/2\), while the change from node voltage is \(VC'\,\delta v\). For an ideal parallel plate \(C''/2=(C')^2/C\), so the two terms combine to the simple complex relation \(\delta F_e=K_{e0}\delta x/(1+i\omega\tau)\). At low frequency the electrical force follows displacement with its fixed-voltage slope. At high frequency the slope vanishes, as it must for an isolated ideal plate carrying fixed charge over one oscillation. At intermediate frequency a motion-induced current dissipates heat in the resistor.

Bring \(\delta F_e\) to the left side of the mechanical equation. Its real part subtracts \(K_{e0}/[1+(\omega\tau)^2]\) from the mechanical stiffness; its imaginary part adds \(K_{e0}\tau/[1+(\omega\tau)^2]\) to the viscous damping coefficient. This damping is positive for a passive resistor. At \(s=0.20\) the static electrical stiffness is \(1.00\) N/m, the operating capacitance is 55.3 fF and \(R=400\) MΩ gives

\[\tau=22.1\,\mu\mathrm s.\]

A 7.2 kHz interrogation has \(\omega\tau\simeq1\). Only about half the static electrical slope survives and the added damping is roughly \(11\,\mu\mathrm{N\,s/m}\). Treat these as frequency-dependent coefficients in the driven susceptibility. Substituting the low-frequency stiffness into a ringdown fit at this frequency would give the wrong displacement when the fitted frequency is inverted. Fitting the complex response at several frequencies avoids that ambiguity.

An experiment can separate the circuit effect from mechanical nonlinearity. Keep the same static bias and displacement, insert two known series resistances, then measure both in-phase and quadrature response to a small sinusoidal force. The displaced equilibrium should remain essentially unchanged at dc if leakage stays small. A stiffness dispersion and dissipative peak that shift in proportion to \(1/R\) support an electrical cause. Then repeat at a smaller oscillation amplitude. Amplitude-dependent frequency shift that survives the change in resistance can arise from nonlinear spring or force curvature, which the first-order susceptibility leaves out. A simple change to the external circuit has turned the slope of an energy curve into a prediction for a measured phase lag.

Compare force intersections for voltage and charge control

The initial gap is 2.00 μm, the area is a 100 μm square and the mass is 0.50 μg. Use the voltage ratio to move the force curve through the fold. At 93% of pull-in the low-displacement fixed-voltage intersection has a positive restoring slope. The high-displacement intersection has a negative slope. The charge-controlled line is prepared at the selected voltage at the original open gap. It gives the same initial force but a different subsequent curve. The readout reports both open-gap roots even if the upper root lies beyond the plotted interval. At the baseline stiffness, static pull-in is 7.32 V.

Change the spring stiffness while holding the voltage ratio fixed. The dimensionless locations of the roots stay in place, but their dimensional force and small-signal frequency change. This checks the scaling that eliminated the physical dimensions in the fold equation. Then set the ratio between the dynamic step threshold and unity. Use the force intersections to locate the potential maximum and sketch the area between attraction and spring reaction from the initial position. Its signed integral supplies the work which distinguishes a bounded excursion from crossing the maximum. The displayed curves predict initial-value behaviour only when combined with a choice of initial kinetic energy and damping.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · distinguish stability from force balance

Infer a gap fraction and a bias from a resonance

An otherwise identical actuator shows \(f/f_0=0.80\) at a stable fixed voltage. Find the deflection as a fraction of gap, the voltage relative to pull-in, and the independent capacitance change. What could spoil this comparison?

Work through the solution

Here \(r=0.64\) and \(s=(1-0.64)/(3-0.64)=0.1525\). The bias ratio is \(\sqrt{(27/4)s(1-s)^2}=0.860\) approximately. The parallel-plate capacitance should rise by \(1/(1-s)-1=18.0\%\). Measure the change relative to the active capacitance after removing any constant wiring capacitance. Disagreement can come from changing mode shape, a nonlinear spring, a tilted plate, a measurement frequency above the electrical equilibration bandwidth, or an incorrect extraction of mechanical stiffness from a strongly damped resonance. With a measured gap and mode shape these explanations yield different quantitative corrections.

Choose a series capacitor to move the static fold

The original 44.3 fF zero-bias actuator should remain on its stable slope as far as \(x=0.60g\), ignoring contact and other limits. Find the series capacitance whose fold falls exactly at this displacement. How much supply voltage appears across the actuator at that point?

Work through the solution

Set \(g_e/3=0.60g\), giving \(\epsilon A/C_s=0.80g\) and \(C_s=C(0)/0.80=55.4\) fF. At \(0.60g\) the actuator capacitance is \(C(0)/0.40=110.7\) fF. Series charge is identical in both capacitors, so

\[V_{\rm act}/V_{\rm supply}=C_s/(C_s+C_{\rm act})=1/3.\]

Choosing exactly this value places operation at vanishing ideal stiffness, so a strictly positive measured restoring slope at that displacement requires a smaller series capacitance or a different margin. The result is sensitive to additional capacitance connected between the series node and ground.

Further reading

  • Derive capacitor pressure from field stress and work

A moving-coil galvanometer as an electrical and mechanical system

Electromechanics, optics and measurement

A scale division records an angle, yet the quantity marked on the dial is electric current. A spring, a magnetic field and the resistance of the external measuring circuit connect the two. Derive the calibration, predict how a pointer approaches its reading, and find which experiment measures the charge of a short pulse.

Convert the force on two straight sides into a torque

A light rectangular coil has \(N\) turns, active length \(l\), width \(w\), and area per turn \(A=lw\). It pivots about an axis parallel to its long sides. Shaped pole pieces confine an approximately radial magnetic field of magnitude \(B\) to an air gap. Over the working angular range the magnetic field is perpendicular to the active wire and lies approximately in the plane of the coil. An elementary length carrying conventional current \(i\) experiences \(d\mathbf F=i\,d\mathbf l\times\mathbf B\). The two long sides carry current in opposite directions and experience forces of equal magnitude \(N ilB\). Acting with lever arms \(w/2\), the forces make a couple whose torque is \(NiAB\). Their net force is approximately zero. The short ends supply negligible torque about the pivot under the ideal geometry.

Write \(K=NAB\), and choose the positive sense of current to give positive torque. Its units are \({\rm N\,m/A}\). In a uniform Cartesian field an ordinary rigid loop instead gives torque \(NiAB\sin\alpha\), where \(\alpha\) is the angle between its normal and the field. The radial gap maintains the factor equivalent to \(\sin\alpha\) close to unity as the pointer turns. This is a design choice that produces a nearly linear scale. If a measured calibration curve flattens at a large angle, field nonuniformity and a nonlinear torsion spring are candidate causes that can be tested separately.

For a soft torsion spring with stiffness \(\kappa\), the return torque is \(-\kappa\theta\) at small deflections from the zero-current setting. At static equilibrium \(\theta=Ki/\kappa\). With \(N=200\), \(A=1.00\,{\rm cm^2}\), \(B=0.250\,{\rm T}\) and \(\kappa=8.0\times10^{-4}\,{\rm N\,m/rad}\), the torque constant is \(0.0050\,{\rm N\,m/A}\) and the current sensitivity is \(6.25\,{\rm rad/A}\). A \(5.0\,{\rm mA}\) current turns the pointer through \(0.03125\,{\rm rad}\), about \(1.79^\circ\). A mirror mounted on the same axle deflects a reflected laser through twice this angle. At a screen distance of \(0.50\,{\rm m}\) the spot shifts \(0.50\tan(2\theta)=31.3\,{\rm mm}\), making the small mechanical rotation easy to resolve.

Recover the reciprocal voltage from work

Now rotate the coil through a small positive angle while it carries positive current. The electromagnetic torque does mechanical work \(Ki\,d\theta\). The voltage associated with the motion must therefore draw electrical work \(i e_{\rm back}\,dt\) of equal magnitude when the motor torque assists that motion. This gives the back-emf relation \(e_{\rm back}=K\dot\theta\), measured as a positive drop in the motor sign convention. Its coefficient has units \({\rm V\,s/rad}\), numerically the same SI coefficient as the torque constant. One can also derive it from the motional emf on the two active sides. Each moves at speed \((w/2)\dot\theta\), crosses field \(B\), and generates a voltage \(Bl(w/2)\dot\theta\) along its wire. The two contributions add over every turn.

Let \(J\) denote the rotational inertia, \(b\) the mechanical viscous damping coefficient, \(L\) the coil inductance and \(R\) the sum of winding plus external series resistance seen by a voltage source \(v(t)\). The electromechanical equations are

\[J\ddot\theta+b\dot\theta+\kappa\theta=Ki,\]
\[L\dot i+Ri+K\dot\theta=v(t).\]

Multiplying the first equation by \(\dot\theta\) and the second by \(i\) eliminates the coupling power \(Ki\dot\theta\). The result says that source power \(vi\) equals the rate of increase of \(J\dot\theta^2/2+\kappa\theta^2/2+Li^2/2\), plus \(b\dot\theta^2+Ri^2\). This identity fixes the relative signs of the torque and back emf. If a sign mistake turns a passive closed coil into a self-accelerating pointer, the same mistake produces a residual coupling term in this power balance. Mechanical friction and winding heat represent distinct sinks even though both can damp the observed displacement.

Predict overshoot and find a critical external resistance

If the electrical relaxation time \(L/R\) is short compared with the oscillation time \(1/\sqrt{\kappa/J}\), set \(i\simeq(v-K\dot\theta)/R\). Substituting into the mechanical equation increases its effective damping to \(b_{\rm eff}=b+K^2/R\). Set \(v=0\) when discussing free relaxation with the electrical terminals connected through a resistor. A moving shorted coil generates a current that opposes its motion. Opening the circuit instead suppresses that current and leaves the mechanical damping. An ideal current source holding specified current through all pointer motions also changes the back-emf response because its output voltage adjusts to enforce that current.

The undamped angular frequency is \(\omega_0=\sqrt{\kappa/J}\) and the damping ratio is

\[\zeta=b_{\rm eff}/(2\sqrt{\kappa J}).\]

Take \(J=2.0\times10^{-6}\,{\rm kg\,m^2}\), \(b=1.6\times10^{-5}\,{\rm N\,m\,s/rad}\), and the preceding \(K,\kappa\). Then \(\omega_0=20.0\,{\rm rad/s}\), \(2\sqrt{\kappa J}=8.0\times10^{-5}\,{\rm N\,m\,s/rad}\), and an open circuit gives \(\zeta=0.20\). With \(R=1.00\,\Omega\) the electric contribution to damping is \(2.50\times10^{-5}\,{\rm N\,m\,s/rad}\), giving \(\zeta=0.5125\). For a voltage step the ideal fractional overshoot in the reduced model is

\[\exp[-\pi\zeta/\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}]=0.153.\]

The first peak occurs after \(\pi/(\omega_0\sqrt{1-\zeta^2})=0.183\,{\rm s}\).

Critical damping occurs when \(b+K^2/R=2\sqrt{\kappa J}\), which requires a total resistance of about \(0.391\,\Omega\). A winding resistance of \(0.300\,\Omega\) therefore leaves \(0.091\,\Omega\) for external source and leads. If the winding alone exceeds the target resistance, a simple passive external series resistor cannot reach this damping in the reduced model. A finite \(L\) must also be checked: with \(L=5.0\,{\rm mH}\) and \(R=1.0\,\Omega\), \(\omega_0L/R=0.10\), so the approximation provides a useful starting estimate. Reducing \(R\) indefinitely eventually invalidates the elimination of current. The full pair of equations then predicts the damping and frequency dependence.

Distinguish a charge impulse from a slow current reading

Suppose a brief imposed current pulse ends long before the pointer has turned appreciably. Integrating the torque equation over that interval gives

\[J\Delta\dot\theta\simeq K\int i\,dt=Kq.\]

The initial signal is now angular velocity proportional to total transmitted charge. For weak subsequent damping, the first excursion is approximately \(Kq/(J\omega_0)\). A dose of \(20\,\mu{\rm C}\) gives \(2.50\,{\rm mrad}\) with the instrument above, corresponding to about \(2.50\,{\rm mm}\) on the mirror scale. Two current pulses of identical charge but different shapes give nearly the same reading if both are short on the mechanical time scale. Their peak currents can be entirely different.

For appreciable damping, use the impulse solution \(\theta(t)=Kq\,e^{-\zeta\omega_0t}\sin(\omega_dt)/(J\omega_d)\), with \(\omega_d=\omega_0\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\) for an underdamped pointer. Its maximum obeys \(\tan(\omega_dt_p)=\omega_d/(\zeta\omega_0)\) in the first quarter cycle. Substitution corrects the ballistic calibration instead of treating the damped peak as an undamped amplitude. The state of the external circuit after the pulse matters because it determines \(\zeta\) during the throw. A calibration pulse should leave the measuring circuit connected in the same way as the unknown pulse.

Measure the torque constant without assuming the magnetic field

The preceding design calculation multiplies turn count, active area and a nominal air-gap induction. An independent calibration can measure the same constant without knowing the field distribution along every wire. Move the pointer through a small controlled angle while measuring its open-circuit voltage with a high-impedance instrument. In the same working interval the voltage is \(K(\theta)\dot\theta\) up to a polarity choice. Integrating the voltage over the sweep gives \(\int_{\theta_1}^{\theta_2}K(\theta)\,d\theta\). If \(K\) is constant this is simply \(K\Delta\theta\), independent of sweep speed. It is often easier to integrate a recorded voltage pulse accurately than to differentiate the angle trace to obtain instantaneous speed.

For a sweep through \(0.0800\,{\rm rad}\), suppose the measured voltage integral is \(0.401\,{\rm mV\,s}\). The generator constant is \(5.01\times10^{-3}\,{\rm V\,s/rad}\). Independently add a known small torque to the spring and measure its static angle. A torque \(32.0\,\mu{\rm N\,m}\) rotates the pointer by \(0.0400\,{\rm rad}\), giving \(\kappa=8.00\times10^{-4}\,{\rm N\,m/rad}\). Combining the two measurements predicts a current sensitivity of \(6.27\,{\rm rad/A}\). Splitting the sweep into smaller equal angular intervals tests whether the measured \(K\) depends on angle. Sweeping backwards should reverse the integrated voltage. A voltage offset instead contributes its offset times duration, so repeating at two sweep speeds helps separate it.

A dynamic cross-check uses free decay under two load conditions. Measure the exponential decay rate \(\alpha_{\rm open}\) with a high-impedance voltage probe and \(\alpha_R\) with a known total resistance in the closed circuit. In the short-electrical-time limit, subtraction of the two damping laws yields

\[K^2=2JR(\alpha_R-\alpha_{\rm open}).\]

With \(J=2.0\times10^{-6}\,{\rm kg\,m^2}\), \(R=1.00\,\Omega\), and decay rates \(4.0\) and \(10.25\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), this gives

\[K=0.0050\,{\rm N\,m/A}.\]

The method needs two identifiable decay rates and independent inertia. If the loaded response is overdamped, the envelope of two oscillation peaks is no longer available; fit the full two-real-pole response to the measured displacement.

Keep the third dynamical variable in a frequency-response experiment

For a sinusoidal voltage at angular frequency \(\omega\) the exact linear transduction law follows by eliminating the complex current after transforming both differential equations. The mechanical impedance in angular-displacement form is \(\kappa-J\omega^2+i\omega b\); the winding impedance is \(R+i\omega L\). Using phasors with time dependence \(e^{i\omega t}\) gives

\[\frac{\Theta}{V}=\frac{K}{(R+i\omega L)(\kappa-J\omega^2+i\omega b)+i\omega K^2}.\]

At zero frequency the gain is \(K/(R\kappa)\) and the phase tends to zero, as the static calibration requires. At a sufficiently high frequency both the electrical and inertial impedances grow. For \(L>0\) the magnitude approaches \(K/(LJ\omega^3)\). The reduced model with the current eliminated algebraically falls only as \(1/\omega^2\). The change of the asymptotic slope provides an experimental way to distinguish finite inductance from an incorrectly adjusted viscous damping constant. Beyond the working band of a real instrument, elastic modes of the pointer or parasitic capacitance can enter before that asymptote is resolved.

At the nominal undamped frequency \(20\,{\rm rad/s}\), with \(R=1.0\,\Omega\) and \(L=5.0\,{\rm mH}\), the stiffness and inertia terms cancel. The denominator becomes \(-\omega^2 L b+i\omega(Rb+K^2)\), or approximately \(-0.000032+i\,0.000820\) in consistent SI units. Its magnitude gives about \(6.09\,{\rm rad/V}\). A sinusoid of \(1.0\,{\rm mV}\) peak produces about \(6.09\,{\rm mrad}\) peak, whereas the static response to \(1.0\,{\rm mV}\) is \(6.25\,{\rm mrad}\). The phases differ radically despite their similar amplitudes: near this frequency the pointer lags by about \(92.2^\circ\). A gain-only calibration at a single frequency would miss the phase lag.

Change the electrical load and compare two step models

In the live graph the source voltage is chosen anew at each resistance to give a final current of \(5.0\,{\rm mA}\) after the pointer settles. That convention keeps the final angle common to all curves and isolates the transient response of the load. The full model retains \(i,\theta,\dot\theta\); the second curve sets \(L\dot i=0\). Predict first which trace should start flatter. With finite inductance the current initially vanishes and increases at a finite rate. The initial torque and angular acceleration therefore vanish, while the reduced model applies a finite torque immediately. Increasing the inductance makes that distinction easier to see, especially when the electrical response time becomes comparable to the period of mechanical oscillation.

Open the live experiment

How could a student change the static range of the instrument without changing its suspension or its torsional stiffness?

Work through the solution

For a dc ammeter, connect a calibrated shunt resistance in parallel with the coil, allowing only a known fraction of total current into it. With a coil winding resistance \(0.300\,\Omega\) and a \(0.0333\,\Omega\) shunt, the coil carries about ten percent of a steady terminal current and the indicated full-scale terminal current increases about tenfold. The total circuit seen by a motional voltage during a pointer oscillation depends on the shunt and source impedance, so the static range multiplier does not by itself give the new damping. For a voltmeter, a series resistance instead sets the current produced by an applied terminal voltage. In both cases record the resistance network whenever reporting a dynamic response.

Estimate the back action on the signal being measured

A galvanometer becomes part of the circuit it observes. Suppose a source with open-circuit voltage \(10.0\,{\rm mV}\) and internal resistance \(2.00\,\Omega\) drives a coil of resistance \(0.300\,\Omega\). After settling, the current is \(4.35\,{\rm mA}\) and the pointer turns by \(27.2\,{\rm mrad}\). The undisturbed short-circuit current of the source would have been \(5.00\,{\rm mA}\). Interpreting the indicated value as the current that would flow with a perfect zero-resistance meter underestimates that quantity by about thirteen percent. Measuring the open-circuit voltage instead requires a sufficiently large input resistance, another electrical boundary condition.

During pointer motion the disturbance includes the back emf as well as winding resistance. If the same coil is briefly turning at \(0.40\,{\rm rad/s}\) it develops a \(2.0\,{\rm mV}\) back emf. Neglecting inductance for that estimate, the resulting current is

\[(10.0-2.0)\,{\rm mV}/2.30\,\Omega=3.48\,{\rm mA}.\]

The deflection therefore contains information about both the source and the pointer's earlier motion. A static calibration alone cannot recover a rapid source waveform. A known source resistance and the two coupled dynamic equations provide a way to estimate the waveform from synchronised angle and current measurements.

Tutorial on calibration and transient identification

A current source produces a static mirror shift of \(12.5\,{\rm mm}\) on a screen \(0.50\,{\rm m}\) away. Estimate the current and identify a reversible test for an angular zero offset.

Work through the solution

Use \(\theta=\frac12\arctan(0.0125/0.50)=0.01250\,{\rm rad}\) and \(i=\kappa\theta/K=2.00\,{\rm mA}\). Repeat with reversed current of equal magnitude. Half the difference of the two angles cancels a fixed angular zero offset and isolates the response odd in current. A changing temperature during reversal can alter the spring or winding resistance, so alternate the order and record the zero afterwards if the required uncertainty is small.

For an open-circuit free decay the ratio of two successive same-side positive peak angles is 3.60. Their time separation is \(0.321\,{\rm s}\). Recover the mechanical parameters that can be inferred if \(J\) is known.

Work through the solution

The logarithmic decrement is \(\ln 3.60=1.281\). The decay rate is \(\alpha=1.281/0.321=3.99\,{\rm s^{-1}}\), and the damped angular frequency is

\[2\pi/0.321=19.57\,{\rm rad/s}.\]

Then \(\omega_0=\sqrt{\omega_d^2+\alpha^2}=19.97\,{\rm rad/s}\),

\[b=2J\alpha=1.60\times10^{-5}\,{\rm N\,m\,s/rad},\]
\[\kappa=J\omega_0^2=7.98\times10^{-4}\,{\rm N\,m/rad}.\]

This identification assumes viscous damping and a sufficiently high impedance to make the electrical damping negligible.

Explore how motion generates voltage in the induction chapter.

Why an oscillator can move without its packet spreading

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Start with the measured mass and curvature of a potential. Factor its Hamiltonian, prove the energy ladder, construct a freely moving Gaussian from number states and find when squeezing or anharmonicity changes that prediction.

Use the equilibrium length and momentum scales

Near a stable minimum a smooth one-dimensional potential has expansion \(V(x)=V(0)+m\omega^2x^2/2+O(x^3)\) after the origin is shifted to the minimum. Choose the arbitrary zero \(V(0)=0\). The fitted curvature is \(m\omega^2\), not \(\omega\) itself; measuring both the oscillation frequency and restoring slope separately identifies the effective mass. The classical energy ellipse becomes a circle if position and momentum are rescaled consistently. Define \(X=\sqrt{m\omega/\hbar}\,x\) and \(P=p/\sqrt{m\hbar\omega}\), so \([X,P]=i\) and \(H=\hbar\omega(X^2+P^2)/2\). Since position and momentum fail to commute, multiplying two linear factors must retain their order.

Set \(a=(X+iP)/\sqrt2\) and \(a^\dagger=(X-iP)/\sqrt2\). Then

\[a^\dagger a=(X^2+P^2+i[X,P])/2=(X^2+P^2-1)/2.\]

Thus \(H=\hbar\omega(N+1/2)\), \(N=a^\dagger a\) and \([a,a^\dagger]=1\). For every normalised state in the relevant domain \(\langle N\rangle=\|a|\psi\rangle\|^2\ge0\). The additive half quantum is an energy contribution that survives in a state with vanishing classical means of \(x,p\); discarding the commutator would lose it. Transition frequencies within an ideal ladder measure energy differences, so those frequencies alone cannot determine an absolute zero of energy.

Prove discreteness and construct the ground state

Using the product rule for commutators gives

\[[N,a]=-a,\]
\[[N,a^\dagger]=a^\dagger.\]

If a normalisable energy eigenstate has \(N\) eigenvalue \(\nu\), application of \(a\) either annihilates it or yields an eigenstate of eigenvalue \(\nu-1\) and norm squared \(\nu\). Repeated lowering cannot yield a state of negative norm squared. For noninteger nonnegative \(\nu\) the chain would reach \(0<\nu'<1\) with nonzero image at a negative eigenvalue. Therefore the chain must terminate at an integer step in an annihilated ground state. This argument uses the bound oscillator's spectrum and states in the ladder operators' domains. Existence and completeness can also be checked by solving the one-dimensional differential equation with decaying boundary conditions.

In coordinate representation \(P=-i\,d/dX\), so \(a\psi_0=0\) reduces to

\[d\psi_0/dX=-X\psi_0.\]

Its square-integrable solution is

\[\psi_0(X)=\pi^{-1/4} e^{-X^2/2}.\]

Normalised excited states follow by \(|n\rangle=(a^\dagger)^n|0\rangle/\sqrt{n!}\). Orthogonality follows from the self-adjoint number operator with distinct eigenvalues. The coordinates in metres have ground variance \(\sigma_x^2=\hbar/(2m\omega)\), momentum variance \(\sigma_p^2=m\hbar\omega/2\), and zero symmetrised covariance. The geometric ground-state width is thus fixed by mass and trap stiffness. A semiclassical estimate minimising \(\hbar^2/(8m\sigma_x^2)+m\omega^2\sigma_x^2/2\) gives \(E_{\min}=\hbar\omega/2\), because the Gaussian saturates the uncertainty inequality used in that estimate.

In a number state both \(\langle x\rangle\) and \(\langle p\rangle\) vanish. Their fluctuations do not. Substitute \(x=\sigma_x(a+a^\dagger)\). Terms \(a^2,(a^\dagger)^2\) change the occupation by two and have zero diagonal element, whereas \(aa^\dagger\) and \(a^\dagger a\) contribute \(n+1\) and \(n\). Hence \(\langle x^2\rangle_n=(2n+1)\sigma_x^2\); similarly \(\langle p^2\rangle_n=(2n+1)\sigma_p^2\). The mean kinetic and potential energies each equal half the total. Measuring a nonzero rms displacement does not imply that the oscillator centre is undergoing a coherent motion.

Find a state whose centre follows the classical trajectory

Search for a normalised eigenstate of \(a\) with complex eigenvalue \(\alpha\). It is permissible for this non-Hermitian operator to have complex eigenvalues; they label states rather than directly observable outcomes. Expand \(|\alpha\rangle=\sum_n c_n|n\rangle\). Matching every number-state coefficient yields

\[c_{n+1}=\alpha c_n/\sqrt{n+1}.\]

Using the exponential series to normalise gives

\[|\alpha\rangle=e^{-|\alpha|^2/2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^n}{\sqrt{n!}}|n\rangle,\]
\[P(n)=e^{-|\alpha|^2}\frac{|\alpha|^{2n}}{n!}.\]

The mean and variance of occupation are both \(|\alpha|^2\). Different coherent states are nonorthogonal: summing the overlap yields

\[|\langle\beta|\alpha\rangle|^2=e^{-|\alpha-\beta|^2}.\]

At displacement difference one in this dimensionless complex plane the overlap probability is still \(e^{-1}=0.368\). A measurement that distinguishes all such nearby coherent preparations perfectly from one copy would contradict this overlap.

Each coefficient acquires \(e^{-i(n+1/2)\omega t}\). Take the common zero-point phase outside the sum, leaving exactly the same expansion with \(\alpha(t)=\alpha e^{-i\omega t}\). Hence \(\langle x(t)\rangle=2\sigma_x\operatorname{Re}\alpha(t)\) and \(\langle p(t)\rangle=2\sigma_p\operatorname{Im}\alpha(t)\). The centred operator \(a-\alpha\) annihilates the state, so its centred quadrature variances match those of the ground state. The packet moves on the classical phase-space ellipse without spreading. In a free particle the quadratic momentum phase causes spreading; in the bound oscillator equally spaced energy levels preserve this particular relative-phase structure.

For a vibrational mode with \(m=2.00\times10^{-25}\) kg and \(\omega=2\pi\times100\) kHz, \(\sigma_x=20.5\) nm. Prepare \(\alpha=1.5i\). Initially the average position is zero and the momentum is positive; a quarter period later the centre reaches \(3\sigma_x=61.5\) nm. The mean occupation is 2.25 and the ground probability is 0.1054. This ensemble has a nonzero ground-state probability even though its position oscillation is clearly resolved by repeated measurements. A pure number state of average energy near this value could give similar rms positions but would show no time-dependent centre.

Background · Which measurement distinguishes two oscillators of equal mean energy?

Compare the first excited number state with a coherent state of amplitude \(\alpha=1\). Both have mean occupation one and hence the same mean energy \(3\hbar\omega/2\). For a number state the expectation of the lowering operator vanishes because it connects orthogonal levels. For a coherent state its expectation equals \(\alpha\) and evolves with the oscillator phase.

\[\langle x(t)\rangle_{\rm coh}=\sqrt{\frac{2\hbar}{m\omega}}\,\mathrm{Re}(\alpha e^{-i\omega t}),\]
\[\langle x(t)\rangle_{n=1}=0.\]

Synchronous position measurements on freshly prepared states distinguish the moving centroid from the stationary one. A repeated energy measurement also separates the preparations. The number state always gives one quantum, whereas the coherent state gives a Poisson distribution with mean and variance both equal to one. A mean energy by itself omits both distinctions.

Rotate an uncertainty ellipse and test a nonideal trap

A sudden frequency change provides an experimentally meaningful way to generate a state with unequal quadrature fluctuations. Prepare the ground state at frequency \(\omega_i\), then instantaneously change to \(\omega_f\). The state in the position representation is continuous through an ideal sudden change. Relative to the final oscillator,

\[\langle X_f^2\rangle=\omega_f/(2\omega_i),\]
\[\langle P_f^2\rangle=\omega_i/(2\omega_f);\]

their product remains \(1/4\). Their sum exceeds one unless the two frequencies coincide. Thus the state is a squeezed vacuum for the new trap, with mean occupation \(\tfrac14(\omega_f/\omega_i+\omega_i/\omega_f-2)\). Unlike a coherent displacement its centre remains zero. Measuring a variance as the ellipse rotates distinguishes these preparations even if their average energies match.

For \(\omega_f=2\omega_i\) the mean occupation is 0.125. Since \(X_f(t)=X_f(0)\cos\omega_ft+P_f(0)\sin\omega_ft\), variance oscillates between 1 and 0.25 in these units with angular frequency \(2\omega_f\). The ground variance for the final trap would be 0.5, so part of the cycle lies below it. Momentum variance rises correspondingly, and at intermediate angles a nonzero covariance is needed in the uncertainty determinant. Abrupt changes also inject work via the time-dependent Hamiltonian. Energy measured before and after the sudden change need not agree even though the state vector at the switching instant is unchanged.

Real traps eventually depart from exact harmonic confinement. Write a model diagonal spectrum \(E_n/\hbar=\omega(n+1/2)+\chi n(n-1)/2\) for weak amplitude-dependent spacings over the occupied range. Coherent-state sums give

\[\langle a(t)\rangle=\alpha e^{-i\omega t}\exp[|\alpha|^2(e^{-i\chi t}-1)].\]

Its magnitude is \(|\alpha|\exp[-|\alpha|^2(1-\cos\chi t)]\). The collapse of the centre can occur under strictly unitary evolution while the number probabilities stay fixed. Their phases reassemble at \(2\pi/|\chi|\) in this ideal spectrum. Irreversible noise and uncontrolled fluctuations can suppress a measured revival; loss of the mean alone does not diagnose dissipation. A position trace and a number-resolved distribution probe complementary parts of the dynamics.

Factor a measured displacement into force history and oscillator response

A linear force couples to position,

\[H(t)=\hbar\omega(N+1/2)-F(t)x.\]

Even if the force time series is irregular, this driven oscillator is exactly soluble within the harmonic approximation. In the Heisenberg picture use \(\dot a=(i/\hbar)[H,a]\). Since \([x,a]=-\sigma_x\), the equation is

\[\dot a=-i\omega a+i\sigma_x F(t)/\hbar.\]

Multiply by \(e^{i\omega t}\) and integrate:

\[a(t)=e^{-i\omega t}\left[a(0)+\frac{i\sigma_x}{\hbar}\int_0^t F(t')e^{i\omega t'}dt'\right].\]

A force therefore displaces the rotating oscillator according to one complex Fourier component of its history. Its phase determines which final quadrature changes. The centred operator still evolves as in free oscillation. Thus a known linear drive displaces a ground state into a coherent state without squeezing it. The same formula shifts a thermal state without removing its thermal fluctuations. The statement about pure coherence requires knowing the initial state as well as measuring the final displacement.

Take a constant force \(F_0\) switched on for time \(T\) then off. For an initially stationary ground state the final amplitude, measured at switch-off in the original oscillator basis, is \(\alpha_F=F_0\sigma_x[1-e^{-i\omega T}]/(\hbar\omega)\). The corresponding mean position is \(F_0(1-\cos\omega T)/(m\omega^2)\), agreeing with the classical step response of a mass on a spring. Its excitation number is \(4(F_0\sigma_x/\hbar\omega)^2\sin^2(\omega T/2)\). A constant force applied for exactly a full period creates zero residual excitation at switch-off even though the oscillator moved substantially during the pulse. Motion of the trap centre and persistent excitation after the trap returns must be experimentally distinguished.

For \(m=2.00\times10^{-25}\) kg and \(100\) kHz as above, let \(F_0=2.00\times10^{-21}\) N. Its static equilibrium displacement is

\[F_0/(m\omega^2)=25.3\,\mathrm{nm}.\]

A half-period pulse leaves the mean at 50.7 nm with zero mean momentum and \(|\alpha|=50.7/(2\times20.5)\simeq1.24\). Its mean added occupation is roughly 1.53. The elastic energy \(m\omega^2\langle x\rangle^2/2\) at switch-off matches \(\hbar\omega|\alpha|^2\), independently of any counting argument. This check subtracts the unchanged zero-point energy. Using the total energy including zero point in the comparison would incorrectly attribute a half quantum to the drive even when the applied force vanishes.

The sudden impulse limit provides another independent test. Let a short pulse deliver total impulse \(J=\int Fdt\) over a time much shorter than \(1/\omega\). The amplitude increment is approximately \(i\sigma_xJ/\hbar\) in the appropriate rotating phase. Since \(2\sigma_p\sigma_x=\hbar\), the mean momentum changes by \(J\). For a smooth pulse spread around one period the same total impulse can produce a much smaller residual excitation because the force acts at different phases of the oscillator. A force sensor based on final occupation measures the squared magnitude of a windowed resonant integral. Reconstructing a complete time-dependent force from that single energy measurement is generally impossible. Varying the drive start time or measuring both final quadratures gives additional information.

Use operator ordering to predict a phase with zero final displacement

Define the displacement operator \(D(\alpha)=\exp(\alpha a^\dagger-\alpha^* a)\). Its generator is anti-Hermitian, so it is unitary. The commutator of \(\alpha a^\dagger-\alpha^*a\) with \(a\) is a constant. Differentiating \(D^\dagger(s\alpha)aD(s\alpha)\) with respect to \(s\) and integrating gives

\[D^\dagger(\alpha)aD(\alpha)=a+\alpha.\]

Consequently \(D(\alpha)|0\rangle\) is an annihilation eigenstate of eigenvalue \(\alpha\), giving an independent physical construction of the number expansion. The normal-order factorisation \(D=e^{-|\alpha|^2/2}e^{\alpha a^\dagger}e^{-\alpha^*a}\) follows from the Baker–Campbell–Hausdorff formula because the relevant commutator is a scalar. Acting on the ground state recovers the Poisson coefficients directly. Omitting the scalar prefactor gives a state with the wrong norm.

Two displacements do not commute as operators despite their effects on the mean phase-space position adding in either order. Their scalar commutator gives

\[D(\alpha)D(\beta)=e^{(\alpha\beta^*-\alpha^*\beta)/2}D(\alpha+\beta).\]

The product around a closed displacement loop is

\[D(\alpha)D(\beta)D(-\alpha)D(-\beta)=e^{\alpha\beta^*-\alpha^*\beta}I.\]

For \(\alpha=1,\ \beta=i/2\) the phase is \(-1\) rad in this written operator order. Reversing the physical order conjugates the phase. The oscillator returns to its initial phase-space position and, apart from this phase, to its initial state. In a single isolated preparation the common phase is invisible. To observe it, place the oscillator interaction under coherent control of one branch of a separate two-level reference, recombine the reference and compare the branch that went around the loop with the branch that did not. Surviving coherence of the reference is essential. The same result can be described as an oriented area in the \(\operatorname{Re}\alpha,\operatorname{Im}\alpha\) plane and ultimately follows from \([x,p]=i\hbar\).

It is useful to trace the actual motion before interpreting a claimed geometric phase. Operators act from right to left on kets, so plotting a particular rectangular loop in time can correspond to the conjugate of a product written left to right. A lab comparison should specify the four pulse phases in chronological order and the ordinary free rotation occurring between them. In the rotating frame the displacement vectors are simple complex increments; in the laboratory frame the oscillator itself continually advances in phase. An uncompensated delay can leave a residual displacement rather than a closed loop. The overlap of that residual displaced ground state with the reference branch is \(e^{-|\Delta\alpha|^2/2}\) in amplitude, reducing observed fringe contrast as well as shifting its phase.

Measure displaced parity to distinguish a number state from a mixture

The number parity operator \(\Pi=(-1)^N\) gives \(+1\) for even and \(-1\) for odd occupation. It implements the coordinate reflection \(x\to-x\) for the harmonic oscillator. A position-space Wigner distribution may be sampled by displacing the state and measuring its parity. In the complex phase-space convention used here define \(W(\beta)=(2/\pi)\operatorname{tr}[D(-\beta)\rho D^\dagger(-\beta)\Pi]\); it integrates to one in \(d^2\beta\). At \(\beta=0\) it is just twice the even-minus-odd probability divided by \(\pi\). Its sign is determined by observable population statistics, but its value at a point is not the probability that a particle occupies a sharp simultaneous position and momentum.

For a coherent input \(|\alpha\rangle\), displacement produces \(|\alpha-\beta\rangle\) up to a phase. Summing the even-minus-odd Poisson occupations gives \(e^{-2|\alpha-\beta|^2}\). Hence \(W(\beta)=2e^{-2|\alpha-\beta|^2}/\pi\), a positive Gaussian. A first excited number state instead has \(W(0)=-2/\pi\). Mix that state with ground-state population,

\[\rho=p|1\rangle\langle1|+(1-p)|0\rangle\langle0|.\]

Then \(W(0)=(2/\pi)(1-2p)\), negative only when \(p>1/2\). An observed lack of negativity at one displacement does not imply a Gaussian state or a globally classical description. It may simply mean that the probe is examining a positive region or that losses have mixed the odd preparation with the ground state.

Suppose an experimental detector distinguishes only “zero excitations” from “one or more”. A measured nonzero fraction of 0.80 does not determine parity without further information: all those events might contain one excitation or a substantial even population might be present. Number resolution or a separate calibrated parity mapping is needed. If only ground and first excited population are independently established, an observed \(p_1=0.70\pm0.02\) gives \(W(0)=-0.255\pm0.025\) in these dimensionless units. Propagating that uncertainty requires the assumption about higher occupations. Combining a displacement scan with number-resolved or parity-sensitive data therefore tests both the preparation and the adequacy of a finite oscillator basis.

Recover a thermal occupation from two independent observables

For an oscillator in thermal equilibrium at inverse temperature \(\beta_T\), the occupation probabilities are \(P_n=(1-q)q^n\) with \(q=e^{-\beta_T\hbar\omega}\). The geometric series gives \(\bar n=q/(1-q)\) and variance \(\bar n(\bar n+1)\). Unlike a coherent state with the same mean occupation, the number distribution is broader and the free mean displacement is zero. The symmetrised position variance is \((2\bar n+1)\sigma_x^2\). A variance measurement alone cannot distinguish a thermal distribution from an incoherent nonthermal mixture with the same mean energy. Resolving more than the variance probes the full probability law.

For example, at \(\bar n=2\) thermal equilibrium predicts ground occupation \(1/3\), number variance 6 and parity \(1/(2\bar n+1)=1/5\). A coherent state with \(|\alpha|^2=2\) gives ground probability 0.1353, variance 2 and parity \(e^{-4}=0.0183\). A uniform randomisation of the coherent phase removes the mean displacement but leaves these Poisson number statistics unchanged. It can therefore resemble the thermal state on a quadrature average while differing sharply in ground population and parity. A displaced thermal preparation adds a classical centre motion on top of the thermal fluctuations; fitting only its energy would confuse displacement with temperature. Centre, centred variance and at least one independent number statistic make a coherent experimental set of observables for these physically different preparations.

Follow a coherent oscillator state in phase space

Change the magnitude and phase of a coherent amplitude independently. Predict which control changes the mean occupation and which shifts the timing of the position measurement.

Open this experiment online

Recover individual transition probabilities after a sudden trap change

The sudden-frequency experiment predicted mean occupation from the second moments. Number-resolved probabilities provide a stronger test. The initial ground-state wavefunction is even, and changing the trap curvature about the same centre preserves parity. The initial state therefore overlaps only even eigenstates of the final trap. A measured odd population beyond readout uncertainty diagnoses a displaced minimum, parity-breaking drive or imperfect initial preparation. The vanishing of odd occupation is stronger than knowing only the mean and variance of a quadrature.

Write the old annihilator in terms of the new operators,

\[a_i=\cosh r\,a_f-\sinh r\,a_f^\dagger,\]

with \(r=\frac12\ln(\omega_f/\omega_i)\) for this choice of sign. This follows directly by expressing both in the same \(x,p\) and comparing coefficients. The relative sign can change under a different convention for defining the squeeze parameter but all probabilities below are invariant. The old vacuum obeys \((a_f-\tanh r\,a_f^\dagger)|0_i\rangle=0\). In the final number basis \(c_n=\langle n_f|0_i\rangle\), the coefficient of \(|n\rangle\) gives

\[\sqrt{n+1}c_{n+1}=\tanh r\sqrt n\,c_{n-1}.\]

The \(n=0\) equation forces \(c_1=0\), and iteration gives

\[c_{2j}=c_0\frac{\sqrt{(2j)!}}{2^j j!}(\tanh r)^j,\]
\[c_{2j+1}=0.\]

To normalise, use the central-binomial series \(\sum_j\binom{2j}{j}q^j/4^j=(1-q)^{-1/2}\) at \(q=\tanh^2r\). Thus

\[|c_0|^2=1/\cosh r.\]

A separate coordinate-space check follows by integrating the overlap of two Gaussian ground states, giving \(P_0=2\sqrt{\omega_i\omega_f}/(\omega_i+\omega_f)\). This agrees with \(1/\cosh r\), independently of the operator recursion. Differentiating the normalisation-generating series gives \(\bar n=\sinh^2r\), agreeing with the energy inferred from the sudden change of the Hamiltonian.

For a doubling of frequency, \(\tanh r=1/3,\ \cosh r=3/(2\sqrt2)\), so

\[P_0=0.9428,\ P_2=P_0/18=0.05238,\]
\[P_4=P_0(3/8)(1/81)=0.00436.\]

Higher even levels contain the remaining probability. At a mean excitation of 0.125, a coherent state would instead have \(P_1=0.1103\). Even imperfect number resolution discriminating odd versus even can strongly distinguish a pure curvature quench from a coherent displacement with equal final energy. A finite switching time can reduce excitation if the oscillator tracks the moving ground state adiabatically. A shift of trap centre during a nominal curvature change adds a displacement and can populate odd levels. Measure the mean and parity along with the energy estimate before inferring a squeeze strength solely from a single occupation number.

Separate temperature from a parity-preserving quench

Before a sudden centred frequency doubling, a calibrated trap has thermal mean occupation 0.10. Predict the fraction of odd-number results after the quench and its final mean occupation. How might an experiment mistakenly interpret the odd results?

Derive and check the result

In the initial thermal ensemble parity expectation is

\[1/(2\bar n+1)=1/1.2=0.8333.\]

Parity commutes with both centred trap Hamiltonians, and measuring even or odd final occupation measures that same parity operator. The odds fraction is therefore \((1-0.8333)/2=0.0833\), unchanged by the quench. The final energy fluctuations scale with the initial quadrature variances multiplied by \(2\bar n+1\), giving \(\bar n_f+1/2=(\bar n_i+1/2)(\omega_f/\omega_i+\omega_i/\omega_f)/2=0.75\). Thus \(\bar n_f=0.25\). Assuming a perfectly cold initial state might turn the observed odd fraction into a false diagnosis of trap displacement. Measure initial parity and post-quench parity independently to isolate processes that actually break the symmetry. The increase of mean occupation alongside unchanged parity is the predicted signature of a centred quench.

Tutorial · analyse a state from amplitude and fluctuation data

Test two preparations with the same mean energy

Take a coherent state with \(|\alpha|^2=3\) and number state \(|3\rangle\). Compare energies, number variance, ground probability and the largest possible mean displacement.

Work through the solution

Both have \(\langle H\rangle=3.5\hbar\omega\). The coherent number variance is 3 while the number-state variance is zero. Ground probabilities are \(e^{-3}=0.0498\) and zero. By selecting a suitable oscillator phase, the coherent state reaches \(2\sqrt3\sigma_x\); the number state has zero centre at every time. An energy average alone leaves these preparations indistinguishable. The event of measuring exactly three quanta has probability \(e^{-3}3^3/3!=0.224\) in the coherent case and unity in the number case. Repeated shots allow this comparison without assuming a single shot reveals the input state.

Estimate numerical truncation before trusting a simulated coherent state

Expand a coherent state with \(\alpha=2\) in number states \(0,\ldots,8\). Is the missing probability negligible at a desired accuracy of one part in a thousand?

Work through the solution

Generate probabilities successively from \(p_0=e^{-4}\), \(p_{n+1}=4p_n/(n+1)\); summing through 8 gives 0.97864, leaving 0.02136 above the cutoff. Renormalising the retained vector hides this discarded probability from a norm check and distorts quantities weighted by \(n\). Through \(n=11\) the tail is about 0.00092, just below the specified probability tolerance. Energy moments require a separate tail weighted by \(n\); a probability bound alone can be inadequate for them. Vary the cutoff while monitoring both number statistics and the centre motion to separate truncation artefacts from real dephasing.

Infer the coherent displacement and a squeezing test

At a known phase, measurements give

\[\langle x\rangle=1.2\sigma_x,\ \langle p\rangle=-1.6\sigma_p.\]

Infer the coherent amplitude consistent with those means, then say what must still be measured to confirm a coherent preparation.

Work through the solution

The means imply \(\operatorname{Re}\alpha=0.60,\ \operatorname{Im}\alpha=-0.80\), so \(|\alpha|=1\) and the putative coherent mean occupation is one. They do not fix the variances. A displaced thermal or squeezed state can produce the same means with different fluctuations. Measure the variance along independent quadratures, including a rotated axis to infer covariance. In an ideal normalised state the expectation of the positive operator \((a-\alpha)^\dagger(a-\alpha)\) equals \(\langle N\rangle-|\alpha|^2\). If it vanishes exactly, the support is in the kernel of \(a-\alpha\), giving the coherent state. With finite uncertainty a near-zero value instead bounds extra excitation in the displaced frame.

Rotation and finite-amplitude dynamics

Translate an angular-momentum balance between laboratory and body frames. Follow it from impact to gyroscopic motion and distinguish driven nonlinear response from a change of stability.
