Why two finite-element wave solvers propagate the same mode at different speeds

Numerical partial differential equations

Build the element mass matrix by integrating shape functions and track the effect of diagonal lumping on the wave spectrum.

From a continuum wave to nodal masses

For \(u_{tt}=c^2u_{xx}\), multiply by a test function and integrate by parts. On a periodic line the boundary contributions cancel. A piecewise-linear element of width \(h\) uses shape functions \(1-\xi,\xi\), with \(\xi\in[0,1]\). Integrating products of shape functions gives

\[M_e=(h/6)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Integrating derivative products gives

\[K_e=(c^2/h)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right).\]

Row-sum lumping replaces each mass row by its sum on the corresponding diagonal, \(h/2\). This preserves the mass associated with a uniform velocity but changes the response to a velocity that varies within an element.

Background · Numerical dispersion
\[c_{\rm ph,h}=\omega_h(k)/k\]

A discretised wave can retain its amplitude and still propagate at the wrong speed. Compare phase and group errors over the actual resolved wavelengths.

Diagonalise the assembled periodic problem

A periodic nodal mode has \(u_j=e^{ij\theta}\). Each neighbour contributes \(e^{\pm i\theta}\) relative to node \(j\). Thus \(K\) multiplies by \(2c^2(1-\cos\theta)/h\), consistent \(M\) by \(h(2+\cos\theta)/3\), and lumped \(M\) by \(h\). Dividing stiffness by mass gives the discrete squared frequency. Relative to the exact frequency \(c\theta/h\),

\[\frac{\omega_L}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta},\]
\[\frac{\omega_C}{c\theta/h}=\frac{2\sin(\theta/2)}{\theta}\sqrt{\frac{3}{2+\cos\theta}}.\]

Both tend to one for long waves; the leading quadratic errors have opposite signs. This is a spatial effect, present even with exact time integration.

Two wavelengths in one mesh

At four cells per wavelength \(\theta=\pi/2\). The lumped ratio is \(2\sqrt2/\pi\approx0.9003\); the consistent ratio is about 1.1027. One wave solver lags by roughly ten per cent and the other leads by roughly ten per cent. At eight cells per wavelength the ratios move much closer to one, but repeated propagation over many wavelengths can still accumulate visible phase error.

At the shortest grid mode \(\theta=\pi\), the consistent frequency is \(\sqrt{12}c/h\) and the lumped frequency \(2c/h\). A central-difference time integrator stable for \(h_t\omega\le2\) therefore requires a smaller step for the consistent mass. Accuracy at one wavelength and stability at the grid scale are different design criteria.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on interpreting a convergence test

Which mass gives the exact kinetic energy for a linear velocity varying from \(v\) to \(-v\) on one element?

Worked solution

The continuum energy with unit density is \(hv^2/6\). The consistent matrix reproduces it by exact integration. The lumped kinetic energy is \(hv^2/2\), because it assigns both end masses the full endpoint speed.

How would you isolate spatial dispersion from time stepping in a measurement of a cavity eigenfrequency?

Worked solution

Solve the generalised eigenproblem \(Kq=\omega^2Mq\) directly for increasingly fine meshes or converge time steps on each fixed mesh. Then compare with the continuum frequency and report both \(h\) and the time tolerance.

Vibrations of monoatomic and diatomic chains

Condensed matter and band theory

Derive normal modes from a local force law and identify the physical motion behind the acoustic and optical branches.

From first principles

Turn a local spring law into a phonon dispersion relation

Consider identical atoms of mass \(m\) spaced by \(a\) along a chain. Write \(u_j\) for the displacement from the equilibrium position of atom \(j\). For small changes of bond length, expand the bond energy to second order about its minimum. Its first derivative vanishes and its positive second derivative \(K\) is the measured bond stiffness. The bond on the right pulls with force \(K(u_{j+1}-u_j)\); the left bond contributes \(K(u_{j-1}-u_j)\). Newton’s law gives

\[m\ddot u_j=K(u_{j+1}-2u_j+u_{j-1}) .\]

Translation of a normal mode by one site can change its phase but not its shape. Insert \(u_j=Ue^{i(kja-\omega t)}\), divide by the common amplitude, and simplify:

\[-m\omega^2=K(e^{ika}+e^{-ika}-2)=-4K\sin^2(ka/2),\]
\[\omega=2\sqrt{K/m}\,|\sin(ka/2)|.\]

For wavelengths much larger than \(a\),

\[\sin(ka/2)\simeq ka/2.\]

The slope gives the sound speed \(a\sqrt{K/m}\). At \(k=0\) all atoms move together and no bond stretches, which explains the zero frequency. More than one atom per primitive cell permits internal relative motion even at \(k=0\), the ingredient needed for an optical branch.

Derivation and applications

Count the modes that crowd against a chain’s band edge

Why can a narrow frequency bin near the top of a vibration band contain a substantial share of all modes? Take a monoatomic chain of \(N\) equal masses \(m\) with one longitudinal displacement each. Impose periodic ends and use identical harmonic nearest-neighbour springs of stiffness \(K\) with spacing \(a\). The frequency on the positive half of the first zone rises monotonically to \(\omega_m=2\sqrt{K/m}\). Inverting that branch gives the wavevector bounding the states below a trial frequency,

\[k(\omega)=\frac{2}{a}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\omega}{\omega_m}\right),\qquad 0\leq\omega\leq\omega_m .\]

Allowed wavevectors are spaced by \(2\pi/(Na)\). Counting the interval of width \(2k(\omega)\) includes both propagation directions. In a large chain the fractional count and its derivative per atom are therefore

\[F(\omega)=\frac{2k(\omega)}{2\pi/(Na)}\frac{1}{N}=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin\!\left(\frac{\omega}{\omega_m}\right),\]
\[g(\omega)=\frac{dF}{d\omega}=\frac{2}{\pi\omega_m\sqrt{1-(\omega/\omega_m)^2}}.\]

Here \(g\,d\omega\) is a fraction of the modes. The normalization follows from \(F(\omega_m)=1\), as required by the count of independent displacements. Near the edge the slope of the dispersion tends to zero, so evenly spaced wavevectors map into progressively smaller frequency intervals. This causes an integrable singularity in the continuum density. A finite spectrometer bin always contains a finite count. A measured spectrum can also weight modes differently through a probe’s coupling to atomic motion.

Worked example

Count modes within the upper tenth of the band

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

For \(N=10{,}000\), the interval between \(0.9\omega_m\) and the upper edge contains \(N[1-(2/\pi)\arcsin(0.9)]\simeq2871\) modes, allowing a mode or two for endpoints. A uniform distribution in frequency would give 1000. A nearly stationary wave packet near the upper edge and a large number of modes per unit frequency have the same origin in the small group velocity.

Second application

Use the spectral median to infer a spring stiffness

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

An ideal chain with atomic mass \(5.00\times10^{-26}\,\mathrm{kg}\) has half of its longitudinal modes below an ordinary frequency of \(4.00\,\mathrm{THz}\). Setting \(F=1/2\) gives

\[\omega_{1/2}/\omega_m=\sin(\pi/4).\]

The upper frequency is \(5.66\,\mathrm{THz}\), and \(K=m(2\pi f_m)^2/4=15.8\,\mathrm{N/m}\). The frequency ratio is unchanged by conversion to angular frequency, but the final stiffness requires that conversion.

Try it · Chain count

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

From bond extension to a travelling displacement

Let atoms of mass \(m\) on a line be coupled by springs of stiffness \(K\), spaced \(a\) apart. The spring to the right pulls site \(n\) by \(K(u_{n+1}-u_n)\); the left contributes \(K(u_{n-1}-u_n)\). A uniform shift produces no force. Translation symmetry suggests \(u_n=u e^{i(kna-\omega t)}\). Substitution gives

\[\omega^2(k)=\frac{2K}{m}(1-\cos ka)=\frac{4K}{m}\sin^2(ka/2).\]

The sound speed at long wavelength is \(a\sqrt{K/m}\), consistent with a continuum rod whose one-dimensional stiffness is \(Ka\) and whose mass per length is \(m/a\). At \(k=\pi/a\) neighbouring atoms move in opposition and the slope of the dispersion vanishes. A wave packet made of nearby \(k\)'s moves with the group velocity \(d\omega/dk\), which can differ greatly from the phase velocity \(\omega/k\) near the boundary.

Background · Why must one vibration have exactly zero frequency?

Give every atom the same displacement. If the potential energy depends only on separations, no bond changes length and no atom experiences a restoring force. Differentiate the force with respect to this uniform translation. For one displacement direction the resulting row-sum condition on the force constants is

\[\sum_j K_{ij}=0,\]
\[D\,(\sqrt{m_1},\ldots,\sqrt{m_N})^T=0,\]
\[D=M^{-1/2}KM^{-1/2}.\]

With unequal masses the translational eigenvector is uniform in physical displacement, while its mass-weighted components vary. A nonzero frequency at zero wavevector may reveal missing neighbour interactions in a numerical model. A real spring to a substrate also breaks translation symmetry and produces a physical gap. The boundary conditions decide which explanation is plausible.

Introduce an internal coordinate

Place masses \(m,M\) alternately, with spacing \(a/2\) between neighbours and cell period \(a\). Let their displacements in cell \(n\) be \(Ue^{ikna}\) and \(Ve^{ikna}\) times the same time factor. The two force balances contain sums of neighbouring amplitudes. For example the A mass sees B in cell \(n\) and in cell \(n-1\), giving \(m\omega^2 U=2KU-K(1+e^{-ika})V\). Exchanging the masses and conjugating the phase gives the second row. Requiring a nonzero solution yields

\[mM\omega^4-2K(m+M)\omega^2+4K^2\sin^2(ka/2)=0 .\]

There are two nonnegative solutions for \(\omega^2\) per \(k\). With \(N\) cells under periodic boundary conditions this gives \(2N\) longitudinal modes, exactly matching the number of independent displacements. At \(k=0\) the lower branch has \(U=V\) and zero frequency. For the upper branch the centre of mass stays fixed, \(mU+MV=0\), with \(\omega^2=2K(1/m+1/M)\). An electric field can excite some optical modes in polar crystals; the branch name alone does not guarantee optical activity.

A gap and a sound speed answer different questions

At the zone boundary \(1+e^{ika}=0\), so the sublattices decouple. If \(M=4m\), the two edge frequencies are \(\sqrt{K/(2m)}\) and \(\sqrt{2K/m}\), a factor of two apart. When the masses become identical the two folded branches meet, recovering the original monoatomic chain in a doubled cell. An apparent branch boundary created by plotting in a smaller zone need not be a physical frequency gap.

Expand the determinant for small \(k\) on the lower branch. Since \(\omega^2\) scales as \(k^2\), discard the term of order \(k^4\). This gives

\[v_s=a\sqrt{K/[2(m+M)]}.\]

The two springs in a cell act in series for long waves, while both masses contribute to inertia. The sound speed alone determines a stiffness-to-mass ratio; measuring the upper branch supplies independent information about the internal motion.

Try it · Diatomic

Change the inputs and compare the recalculated curves with the preceding derivation and baseline figure. The axes give the units; the numerical readout states the model assumptions and any limiting cases. Reset the inputs to return to the reference setting.

Open this live calculation

Convert a mode count into thermal predictions

Quantisation assigns a harmonic mode levels \(\hbar\omega(j+1/2)\). The thermal part of its mean energy is \(\hbar\omega/[\exp(\hbar\omega/k_BT)-1]\). Low-temperature excitations thus select long-wavelength acoustic modes while high-frequency optical modes remain scarcely populated. Counting states in a three-dimensional sphere gives a low-frequency density proportional to \(\omega^2\) for linear branches. Rescaling the energy integral by \(\hbar\omega/k_BT\) gives thermal energy proportional to \(T^4\) and heat capacity proportional to \(T^3\). Dimensionality and linear dispersion are essential assumptions behind that power.

Count thermally generated vacancies

A mode calculation for a perfect crystal starts with one atom on every site. Heating a real sample can leave some atoms at a surface or reservoir and some lattice sites empty. How large should that defect population be even at thermal equilibrium? Consider \(N\) equivalent sites and \(n_v\) independent vacancies. At fixed temperature and pressure let \(g_f\) be the Gibbs cost of making one vacancy and transferring its atom to the reservoir. It may contain local vibrational contributions. We count the separate entropy of choosing the missing sites explicitly.

There are \(W=N!/[n_v!(N-n_v)!]\) such arrangements. With \(x=n_v/N\) and macroscopic occupations, Stirling’s formula gives

\[\frac{G-G_0}{N}=xg_f+k_BT\left[x\ln x+(1-x)\ln(1-x)\right].\]

Minimizing with respect to \(x\) gives

\[g_f+k_BT\ln[x/(1-x)]=0.\]

The second derivative is positive. Thus \(x=1/(1+e^{g_f/(k_BT)})\). For positive \(g_f\gg k_BT\) there are few vacancies and \(x\simeq e^{-g_f/(k_BT)}\). Each vacancy costs energy, but at tiny \(x\) the number of possible positions grows fast enough to favour a nonzero population. A finite microscopic specimen can still contain zero vacancies at a given instant.

Take \(g_f=0.800\,\mathrm{eV}\) as constant over the chosen range and \(k_B=8.6173\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{eV/K}\). At \(900\,\mathrm K\), \(k_BT=0.07756\,\mathrm{eV}\) and \(x=3.31\times10^{-5}\). A region with \(10^{15}\) sites contains an expected \(3.31\times10^{10}\) empty sites. At \(600\,\mathrm K\) the equilibrium fraction drops to \(1.91\times10^{-7}\). Rapid cooling may leave an excess because vacancies must migrate to sinks before the concentration relaxes. The formation free energy in this calculation sets an equilibrium population. A migration barrier would enter the time to attain it.

A light substitution can localize a vibration

An infinite monatomic chain has a continuous vibration band, while a single altered atom can support a vibration spatially concentrated near one site. Let identical springs of stiffness \(K\) connect masses \(m\) on a chain of spacing \(a\), except the central mass at site zero is \(M\). Seek a symmetric localized solution with alternating neighbouring amplitudes \(u_n=A(-q)^{|n|}e^{-i\omega t}\) for \(0<q<1\). Alternation is motivated by the top of the perfect-chain band where adjacent atoms oscillate out of phase. Exponential decay supplies the localization.

At any ordinary site away from the impurity, the equation \(-m\omega^2u_n=K(u_{n+1}+u_{n-1}-2u_n)\) gives

\[m\omega^2=K(2+q+q^{-1}).\]

Since \(q+q^{-1}>2\), this frequency lies above the perfect-chain band edge \(\omega_{\max}=2\sqrt{K/m}\). At site zero both neighbours have displacement \(-q u_0\), giving \(M\omega^2=2K(1+q)\). Eliminating \(\omega\) and cancelling \(1+q\) yields

\[q=\frac{M}{2m-M},\]
\[\omega^2=\frac Km(2+q+q^{-1}).\]

The physical condition \(0<q<1\) requires a lighter central mass \(0<M<m\). A heavy isotope can still scatter travelling waves or produce resonant response within the band. This ansatz predicts no exponentially localized symmetric mode above the band for the heavier substitution. Its absence follows from the admissible decay factor rather than an assumption that a heavy impurity cannot move.

Predict a localized spectral peak

Take host atomic mass \(m=4.00\times10^{-26}\) kg, spring stiffness \(K=40.0\) N/m, lattice spacing \(a=0.300\) nm and impurity \(M=m/2\). The host upper angular frequency is

\[2\sqrt{40.0/(4.00\times10^{-26})}=6.32\times10^{13}\,\mathrm{rad/s}.\]

For the impurity \(q=1/3\), so \(\omega^2=(16/3)K/m\), giving \(\omega=7.30\times10^{13}\) rad/s or \(f=11.6\) THz. The ordinary chain ends near \(10.1\) THz.

The neighbour amplitude is minus one third of the central amplitude, and the second neighbour has plus one ninth. Expressing the absolute envelope as \(e^{-|n|a/\xi}\) gives localization length \(\xi=a/\ln3=0.273\) nm. As \(M\) approaches \(m\) from below, \(q\) approaches one, localization length diverges and the extra frequency merges with the band edge. A finite-chain calculation should therefore vary chain length before claiming that a very weak impurity mode is spatially localized: a box shorter than its decay length cannot separate that broad tail from a standing host mode.

Spatially resolved displacement data can provide two independent estimates of impurity mass. Invert the decay-factor relation to obtain \(M/m=2q/(1+q)\) from neighbouring displacement amplitudes. Separately insert the measured localized frequency and the same host force constant in the central equation of motion. Agreement tests the assumption that the impurity changes only inertia. A substituted atom may also change the two neighbouring springs: in that case the observed frequency and decay factor can no longer be fitted by altering mass alone. This inversion distinguishes an isotope-like substitution from a stronger local bonding perturbation.

A weak second impurity far away perturbs the localized mode only through its exponentially small tail. Bringing two such impurities within a few decay lengths allows the modes to mix and split in frequency, offering another test of their spatial localization.

Rotational and vibrational populations in a molecular gas

Statistical mechanics from microscopic counting to material response

A pressure thermometer and a calorimeter do not count molecular degrees of freedom in the same way. A dilute diatomic gas can retain nearly ideal pressure while its heat capacity changes appreciably as discrete internal levels become populated. Build the molecular partition function, derive the rotor and oscillator contributions, and infer temperatures from line populations without treating spectral intensity as a direct state count.

When a molecule's partition function factorises

Assume a dilute gas of stable diatomic molecules with weak interparticle interactions. Approximate one molecule's Hamiltonian by independent translational, rotational, vibrational and electronic parts. This requires a bond sufficiently stiff that rotation does not greatly alter vibrational energy and a temperature well below electronic excitations or dissociation when the electronic factor is treated as constant. If energies add and allowed states form a product, Boltzmann factors multiply. Thus \(q=q_{\rm tr}q_{\rm rot}q_{\rm vib}q_{\rm el}\), \(Z_N\simeq q^N/N!\) in the dilute classical translational regime. The logarithm converts this product into additive free energies, energies and heat capacities.

The translational factor \(V/\lambda_T^3\) provides the volume derivative and hence ideal pressure \(Nk_BT/V\). A volume-independent internal factor changes neither that derivative nor the associated translational equation of state. It does change entropy, chemical potential and calorimetry. The molar heat capacities satisfy \(C_p-C_V=R\) for a fixed-composition ideal gas, even when both vary strongly with temperature. Dissociation, chemical equilibration or significant intermolecular interactions can change the conclusion for the measured sample.

One danger is double-counting states. Angular momentum orientations belong to rotational degeneracy, oscillator quanta to vibration, electronic spin and nuclear exchange symmetry may constrain one another. A constant electronic degeneracy contributes an additive entropy \(Nk_B\log g\) but no internal energy or heat capacity at fixed level energies. A large heat capacity is evidence for energetically separated states becoming populated, not just for a large number of exactly degenerate states.

Derive the quantum rigid-rotor contribution

For a molecule of moment of inertia \(I\) about its centre of mass, rotational Hamiltonian is \(\hat L^2/(2I)\). Angular momentum eigenvalues are \(\hbar^2J(J+1)\), with \(2J+1\) projections at fixed \(J=0,1,\ldots\). Define rotational temperature \(\theta_r=\hbar^2/(2Ik_B)\). For distinguishable nuclei and a common nuclear spin factor omitted, the rotational sum and populations are

\[q_r=\sum_{J=0}^\infty(2J+1)e^{-\theta_r J(J+1)/T},\]
\[p_J=(2J+1)e^{-\theta_r J(J+1)/T}/q_r .\]

At high temperature the populated range contains many adjacent \(J\) values. Replacing the sum with an integral and substituting \(u=J(J+1)\) gives

\[q_r\simeq T/\theta_r.\]

Hence \(U_r\simeq Nk_BT\), \(C_{V,r}\simeq Nk_B\), the classical result for two independent rotational kinetic energies of a linear object. At low temperature only \(J=0\) and \(J=1\) matter. Their degeneracies and gap \(2k_B\theta_r\) give \(q_r\simeq1+3e^{-2\theta_r/T}\) and an exponentially vanishing heat capacity. Keeping the degeneracy three is essential for both its magnitude and the temperature of its turn-on.

For an illustrative heteronuclear rotor with \(\theta_r=85\) K, direct summation at 20 K gives \(q_r=1.00061\) and \(C_{V,r}=0.0440k_B\) per molecule. At 90 K the corresponding values are 1.471 and 1.063. At 300 K the heat capacity is 1.002. A small overshoot above the classical rotational value is possible during the crossover. A model that simply switches from zero to one classical degree contribution at \(T=\theta_r\) misses the entire smooth population redistribution and cannot predict line ratios.

The most populated \(J\) need not be zero, despite increasing energies, because the projection multiplicity increases with \(J\). Viewing the numerator as a smooth function of \(J\) gives \(J_{\rm peak}\simeq[\sqrt{2T/\theta_r}-1]/2\), to be checked against neighbouring integer values. The estimate is unreliable if it predicts only one or two populated levels. Computing a short discrete sum costs little and reveals its own truncation error through the final omitted weights.

Derive oscillator energy and heat capacity from a geometric series

Near its bond-length minimum approximate the interaction by a spring. With reduced mass \(m_r\) and angular vibration frequency \(\omega_v\), quantum energies are \(E_n=\hbar\omega_v(n+1/2)\). Define \(\theta_v=\hbar\omega_v/k_B\). Summing the resulting geometric series and differentiating gives

\[q_v=\frac{e^{-\theta_v/(2T)}}{1-e^{-\theta_v/T}},\]
\[\frac{U_v}N=k_B\theta_v\left(\frac12+\frac1{e^{\theta_v/T}-1}\right),\]
\[\frac{C_{V,v}}{Nk_B}=\frac{x^2e^{-x}}{(1-e^{-x})^2},\]
\[x=\theta_v/T .\]

The zero-point term contributes energy but no heat capacity at fixed frequency. For \(x\ll1\) the heat capacity approaches \(k_B\) per independent vibrational mode, comprising kinetic and potential contributions. For \(x\gg1\) excitation is too costly and the heat capacity behaves as \(k_Bx^2e^{-x}\). A molecule can still vibrate quantum mechanically in its ground state while contributing virtually no vibrational heat capacity. A calorimeter measures sensitivity of mean energy to temperature and need not see the ground-state motion.

Take \(\theta_v=3000\) K. At \(T=300\) K the vibrational contribution is only \(0.00454k_B\), while at 1200 K it reaches \(0.609k_B\). Combining with the earlier rotor gives molar \(C_V\) approximately \(2.507R\) at 300 K and \(3.109R\) at 1200 K, assuming stable composition and frozen electronic structure. For acoustic measurements the ideal adiabatic ratio \(\gamma=(C_V+R)/C_V\) then decreases from about 1.399 to 1.322. An acoustic drive faster than vibrational relaxation can measure a frequency-dependent effective ratio because internal populations fail to remain equilibrated through a compression cycle.

Compare this single oscillator with the frequency distribution in the lattice heat-capacity plot

Nuclear exchange and the symmetry number

For a homonuclear molecule, rotating its classical orientation by half a turn end for end does not create a second distinct arrangement. In a high-temperature rotational count a symmetry number two therefore divides the distinguishable-nuclei rotor partition function once the compatible spin states are accounted for. Since this factor is constant with temperature it shifts entropy and chemical potential, but not the classical rotational energy or heat capacity. At low temperature it is important to treat actual quantum parity, not divide every level by two.

A homonuclear diatomic rotational wavefunction acquires a factor \((-1)^J\) under exchange of nuclei. The complete state also includes nuclear spin, electronic and vibrational parts. For ordinary molecular hydrogen in its electronic and vibrational ground state the protons are spin-half fermions. Antisymmetry pairs even \(J\) with the single antisymmetric spin singlet and odd \(J\) with the three symmetric triplet spin states. In true spin-conversion equilibrium the rotor sum uses weights one or three in addition to \(2J+1\). At temperatures large compared with rotational spacing, even and odd \(J\) sectors have nearly equal rotational sums and the equilibrium odd-to-even population ratio approaches three.

Conversion between spin species can be slow. A rapidly cooled sample may preserve its prepared fractions rather than attain spin equilibrium. Then compute separate parity-restricted partition functions for fixed species numbers, and average their thermal observables with the frozen proportions. Allowing the total odd fraction to relax yields a different heat capacity, including the energy liberated by conversion. Two experiments on chemically identical hydrogen can consequently report different heating curves because their preparation constrains different internal sectors.

Use intensities only after accounting for the instrument

An absorption line originating from level \(J\) has a strength depending on more than its population. Transition moment, polarisation, transition frequency, stimulated emission correction, instrument response and path length can all enter. Suppose line strengths and detector factors have been divided out to yield quantities proportional to level populations \(N_J\). Then a straight-line fit of \(\log[N_J/(2J+1)]\) against \(J(J+1)\) has slope \(-\theta_r/T\) for the simple heteronuclear model. Its intercept depends on total particle number, partition function and residual instrumental gain.

For \(\theta_r=10\) K, a measured corrected ratio \(N_2/N_0=2.00\) implies \(2=5e^{-60/T}\) and \(T=65.5\) K. Omitting the factor of five would make an upper population larger than the lower one appear incompatible with positive temperature. The inferred temperature assumes the rotational populations are equilibrated at one temperature; an optically pumped or rapidly expanding sample may have different rotational and translational distributions.

A plot over several \(J\) values can test whether one slope is adequate. Curvature may come from centrifugal distortion, line saturation, unresolved overlap, multiple temperatures or a wrong nuclear-spin degeneracy. Alternating residuals for even and odd \(J\) indicate a different physical problem from a smooth quadratic residual against \(J(J+1)\). Repeat with lower optical depth to isolate saturation, and use independently measured transition frequencies to fix level energies before fitting populations.

Recover molecular translation separately from internal structure

In a low-density molecular beam, the translational speed distribution is often easier to measure than the internal populations. For an equilibrium gas leaving a small opening into vacuum, the molecular flux is weighted by the normal velocity. Fast molecules reach and cross the aperture more frequently, so the distribution of speeds in the beam differs from the Maxwell speed distribution inside the reservoir. The mean translational energy of molecules in an effusive beam is \(2k_BT\), whereas the bulk mean is \(3k_BT/2\). This selectivity occurs even when rotational and vibrational populations remain canonical.

To derive the difference, start with the bulk velocity density proportional to \(e^{-mv^2/(2k_BT)}\). Write the outward normal speed as \(v\cos\theta\), integrate over the outward hemisphere and include the velocity-space shell factor \(v^2dv\). The resulting beam speed density is proportional to \(v^3e^{-mv^2/(2k_BT)}dv\), while the original bulk speed density has \(v^2\). Taking a ratio of Gaussian moments gives the stated energy. Comparing a beam energy to the wrong bulk prefactor would yield a temperature one third too high.

If the aperture is small compared with the mean free path and transition-detection efficiency is independent of speed after correction, internal state probabilities factor out of this flux weighting. A rotational population ratio can then report the reservoir temperature while the observed mean translational energy follows the effusive weighting. In a dense nozzle undergoing many collisions, expansion can cool translation and rotation differently. An inferred rotational and translational temperature disagreement is therefore not automatically bad calibration; first identify whether the beam is effusive, collisionally expanding or selectively detected.

As a bench example, a beam mean translational energy of 0.060 eV corresponds to approximately 348 K under effusion, using \(k_B=8.6173\times10^{-5}\) eV/K. Treating it as the bulk mean would give about 464 K. Compare against a population ratio from two corrected lines spanning \(80k_B\) kelvin and equal degeneracies. At the two temperatures the predicted ratios are about 0.795 and 0.842. Resolving this difference demands a relative line calibration appreciably better than six percent even though the two erroneous temperature interpretations differ by more than one hundred kelvin.

An isotope mixture reveals which spectral changes affect entropy

Compare two isotopic forms with the same approximate electronic potential surface. Bond force constant and equilibrium bond length then remain nearly unchanged while reduced nuclear mass changes. The harmonic vibrational frequency scales as \(m_r^{-1/2}\), while the rotational temperature scales as \(m_r^{-1}\). Consequently vibration and rotation respond differently to the same isotope substitution. Their shifts can jointly test whether two sets of spectral features genuinely belong to the same molecular carrier.

Suppose the reduced mass rises by a factor 1.21. A vibrational line at 2000 inverse centimetres moves approximately to 1818 inverse centimetres, and a rotational constant initially 2.00 inverse centimetres moves to 1.65 inverse centimetres. The vibrational characteristic temperature follows from \(hc\widetilde\nu/k_B\), about 1.4388 K per inverse centimetre. At room temperature the two vibrational gaps still greatly exceed thermal energy, so the vibrational heat capacity can remain small even though the spectral isotope shift is easy to resolve.

The zero-point energies nevertheless differ. For this vibrational example their difference is \(hc(2000-1818)/2\) per molecule, corresponding to about 131 kelvin in thermal units or 1.09 kJ/mol. Such offsets cancel within a fixed-species heat-capacity derivative but can matter to comparisons of chemical equilibrium or isotopic exchange. An analysis that discards all constant energy offsets independently in each molecular species can corrupt a reaction free energy unless it restores a consistent reference across reactants and products.

For a thermal entropy contribution from this one oscillator use \(S_v=k_B[(n+1)\log(n+1)-n\log n]\) with \(n=1/(e^x-1)\). This follows by inserting \(U_v\) and \(\log q_v\) into \(S=k_B\log q+U/T\); the zero-point contributions cancel exactly. Thus the measured isotope shift can change vibrational entropy via occupancy even while the zero-point offset itself contributes no entropy. It is helpful to evaluate all three quantities—energy difference, entropy difference and heat-capacity difference—rather than presume that a line shift must have the same thermodynamic role in each.

An internal relaxation time appears in frequency-dependent calorimetry

The equilibrium partition function assumes internal mode populations re-equilibrate on times short compared with the measurement. Model one slower internal degree by an internal energy \(U_s\) relaxing toward its equilibrium value at the instantaneous translational temperature \(T\), with \(\dot U_s=[U_{s,\rm eq}(T)-U_s]/\tau_s\). For a small oscillation around \(T_0\),

\[\delta U_{s,\rm eq}=C_s\delta T.\]

In the \(e^{-i\omega t}\) convention, the complex internal heat capacity and the total measured capacity including an instantaneous background \(C_f\) are

\[C^*(\omega)=C_f+\frac{C_s}{1-i\omega\tau_s}.\]

At low frequency the capacity is \(C_f+C_s\). At high frequency the slow internal energy cannot follow and the apparent in-phase capacity approaches \(C_f\). Its out-of-phase component peaks at \(\omega\tau_s=1\), where real and imaginary increments equal \(C_s/2\). The structure resembles dielectric relaxation because both arise from one relaxing state variable driven by a controlled sinusoid. It follows here from an explicit energy-exchange model and its assumptions.

Take a molar fast contribution \(2.50R\), slow equilibrium contribution \(0.60R\) and relaxation time 2 ms. The full equilibrium value is \(3.10R\). At about 79.6 Hz the real measured capacity is \(2.80R\) and the imaginary increment magnitude \(0.30R\). A heat pulse much shorter than two milliseconds initially raises the temperature as if the capacity were close to \(2.50R\), then energy flows into the internal mode. A slow calorimetric ramp can be perfectly consistent with a faster acoustic measurement that infers a smaller active capacity.

A sample container connected to a thermal bath produces its own complex temperature response even if molecular populations are always equilibrated. Disentangle those effects by calibrating the empty cell or an inert reference gas across the same frequency range, changing pressure to alter collision-mediated internal-relaxation time, and checking the predicted equilibrium limit from independently known level spacings. A mechanical resonance in the apparatus can be separated by varying cell geometry while keeping gas composition fixed. Fitting one imaginary peak to a molecular relaxation model without these controls does not uniquely determine the microscopic relaxation time.

How a line ratio and a calorimeter constrain degeneracies differently

Two groups report a low-energy excited manifold with dimensionless population ratio \(r=N_1/N_0\) at two temperatures. A single ratio determines only \(r=(g_1/g_0)e^{-\Delta/(k_BT)}\), so changing the assigned degeneracy changes the inferred gap. Taking the log ratio at two known temperatures removes a common degeneracy factor. The slope of \(\log r\) against \(1/T\) is minus the gap in kelvin, and the intercept is the log degeneracy ratio if the line-strength calibration and equilibrium assumptions hold.

For illustrative values \(r=0.300\) at 100 K and \(r=0.949\) at 200 K, the log change is approximately 1.152. Dividing by the inverse-temperature change magnitude 0.005 per kelvin gives \(\Delta/k_B\simeq230\) K. Inserting the first ratio gives \(g_1/g_0\simeq3.00\). The same two-manifold model predicts a dimensionless heat capacity \(x^2r/(1+r)^2\) per molecule. At 100 K it is about 0.94, a separate calorimetric prediction independent of the absolute number of detected photons.

A third temperature materially improves the test. An unseen nearby manifold or a line strength that changes with instrumental settings may fit two exact population ratios but produce curvature and excess heat capacity elsewhere. Spectroscopy resolves ordered energy differences while calorimetry sums energy variances over all accessible states. Their combination offers more information than adding further precision to one population measurement under the same ambiguous degeneracy assumption.

Compress a gas without pretending its heat capacity stays constant

A reversible adiabatic compression is often summarised by a fixed exponent \(\gamma\). That power law assumes temperature-independent heat capacities along the path. Molecular populations give a direct case where the exponent changes. For an ideal gas at fixed composition,

\[dS=C_V(T)dT/T+Nk_B\,dV/V.\]

Along a reversible adiabatic path set the sum to zero and integrate. The volume ratio is determined by an integral of measured or calculated \(C_V(T)/T\), not by a universal constant exponent across every temperature.

As an illustrative calculation approximate translation and fully active rotation by \(C_V=2.5Nk_B\) plus one harmonic vibrational mode with \(\theta_v=3000\) K. Heating from 300 to 1200 K at constant entropy gives a volume contraction factor from the fast degrees alone of \(4^{2.5}=32\). The vibrational entropy difference adds another factor \(e^{\Delta S_v/(Nk_B)}\). At 300 K the oscillator entropy is only about \(0.00050k_B\), while at 1200 K it is about \(0.309k_B\). The combined estimate requires \(V_i/V_f\simeq43.6\), a substantially larger compression than the frozen-vibration approximation predicts.

This comparison assumes ideal gas throughout and no dissociation, conditions to be checked in a real large compression. It nevertheless identifies the mechanism: part of the compression work goes into levels that were effectively inactive initially. Using the final high-temperature heat capacity across the whole path would err in the opposite direction by assigning that energy storage to the low-temperature portion as well. The entropy integral correctly weights each temperature interval by its actual internal population response.

A numerically stable solution can avoid differentiating noisy calorimetry. Integrate the entropy increment from tabulated heat capacities in the log-temperature variable. Compare an adaptive integral with a refined straightforward grid, paying particular attention to the rotational or vibrational crossover scale. Alternatively compute endpoint entropies from partition functions when an independently justified spectral model exists. Agreement between calorimetric integration and spectroscopy-derived endpoint entropy checks both assignments without demanding exact agreement of noisy pointwise derivatives.

Experimental pressure also checks the endpoint calculation independently. After equilibration, the ideal equation of state predicts \(p_f/p_i=(T_f/T_i)(V_i/V_f)\), approximately 174 for this idealised path. Finite heat leakage or a pressure-dependent nonideality can change that value. A rapid adiabatic compression can be irreversible even if little heat leaves the chamber, and then the measured entropy can increase. Distinguish a reversible reference calculation from an actual rapid piston protocol before using a mismatch to infer missing molecular levels.

An independent isotropic acoustic test at a fixed initial temperature infers \(\gamma\) from the small-amplitude sound speed \(c_s^2=\gamma RT/M\), where \(M\) is molar mass. At fixed molecular mass a decrease of \(\gamma\) from 1.40 to 1.32 at the same temperature would lower sound speed by the square root of their ratio, roughly three percent. That comparison must truly hold temperature fixed: the large direct thermal dependence of sound speed can easily exceed the internal-mode effect. Frequency-resolved acoustics can see part of a vibrational mode freeze dynamically while equilibrium spectroscopy still predicts its population. Record both phase velocity and acoustic attenuation if the aim is to identify a relaxation time. Across the relaxation band, dispersion without the corresponding dissipative response should prompt a check of transducer phase calibration and acoustic boundary conditions.

Read the rotational level spacing from thermal energy storage

Sum the discrete levels of a linear rotor and compare the heat capacity with equipartition as temperature rises.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial · Identify a rotor inertia from spectral spacing

For a heteronuclear linear rotor the lowest pure rotational transition has ordinary frequency 120 GHz. Infer \(\theta_r\), the moment of inertia and the expected \(J=1\) to \(J=0\) population ratio at 10 K. How could an isotope substitution test the interpretation?

Use the transition gap and keep angular and ordinary frequency distinct

The gap is \(2k_B\theta_r=h\nu\), so \(\theta_r=2.88\) K and \(I=\hbar^2/(2k_B\theta_r)=1.40\times10^{-46}\) kg square metres. The population ratio is

\[3e^{-5.76/10}=1.69.\]

The upper manifold contains more molecules even though each of its levels has lower individual Boltzmann weight. Increasing reduced mass at nearly fixed bond length increases \(I\), reducing the transition frequency approximately inversely with reduced mass. A vibrational frequency from the same spring would scale instead with inverse square root of reduced mass.

Tutorial · Infer an excitation temperature from calorimetry

A gas of linear molecules has molar \(C_V=2.91R\) at a temperature where translation and rotation contribute \(2.50R\). Neglect electronic excitation and estimate the characteristic temperature of a single harmonic vibration in units of measurement temperature. Would a measured \(C_p\) be usable unchanged?

Invert the oscillator contribution and verify its feasible range

The remaining \(0.41R\) lies between zero and the one-mode classical limit \(R\). Solve \(x^2e^{-x}/(1-e^{-x})^2=0.41\) on positive \(x\). At 3.0 it is 0.496 and at 3.5 it is 0.393, placing the root near 3.41. Thus \(\theta_v\) is about 3.41 times measurement temperature. Use several temperatures to verify the characteristic value and frozen-composition model. For ideal gas at fixed composition, subtract \(R\) from \(C_p\) before applying this constant-volume balance. Latent processes, reaction and internal relaxation would require an extended model.

Tutorial · Distinguish equilibrium hydrogen from a frozen spin mixture

At low temperature only \(J=0\) in the even sector and \(J=1\) in the odd sector contribute. Use \(\theta_r=85\) K and \(T=30\) K. Estimate the equilibrium odd fraction and compare the energy stored in a sample frozen at a three-quarter odd fraction, measured relative to the common lowest even state.

Normalise both sectors and identify the energy released on conversion

The relative weight of \(J=1\) is \(3(2J+1)e^{-170/30}=0.0311\); the even ground state has weight one. The equilibrium odd fraction is approximately 0.0302. A frozen sample at odd fraction 0.75 stores about \(0.75(170)k_B=127.5k_B\) kelvin of rotational energy per molecule, versus about \(5.1k_B\) kelvin for equilibrium. The excess expressed per mole is roughly \(R(122.4\,{\rm K})=1.02\) kJ/mol.

The frozen low-temperature heat capacity can be small even while substantial energy remains trapped in the odd sector. A calorimeter monitoring a slowly converting sample measures time-dependent heat release, not simply the derivative of a stationary restricted partition function. Measure the spin fraction independently or vary equilibration time before comparing its heat curve with an equilibrium prediction.

Energy and geometric integration

Numerical ordinary differential equations

Compare three time updates on a conservative oscillator and explain why a method can have small short-term state error yet distort an orbit over many periods.

From first principles

Build an oscillator update out of two area-preserving shears

Take one undamped oscillator of mass \(m>0\) and stiffness \(k>0\). Its canonical position \(q\) and momentum \(p\) satisfy

\[\dot q=p/m,\]
\[\dot p=-kq.\]

Exact trajectories run along energy ellipses and preserve phase-plane area. If both slopes are taken at the left endpoint, the update and its area factor are

\[\begin{pmatrix}q_{n+1}\\p_{n+1}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&h/m\\-hk&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}q_n\\p_n\end{pmatrix},\qquad \det=1+h^2k/m .\]

A positive factor greater than one at each step makes the numerical orbit spiral outward. Instead update the momentum at fixed position, then move the position using that new momentum. Both operations are shears:

\[p_{n+1}=p_n-hkq_n,\]
\[q_{n+1}=q_n+h p_{n+1}/m.\]

The product matrix has diagonal entries \(1-h^2k/m\) and \(1\), with off-diagonal product \(-h^2k/m\), giving determinant one. Its eigenvalues form a conjugate pair on the unit circle when \(|2-h^2k/m|<2\). This calculation explains the bounded oscillatory behaviour for \(0<h\sqrt{k/m}<2\). Preserving area alone would also allow one real mode to grow while another shrinks. It does not establish either stability or exact conservation of the original energy.

Background · Symplectic map
\[(D\Phi)^\mathsf T J(D\Phi)=J\]

This is preservation of the canonical symplectic form. It provides control of long-time structure without guaranteeing that every trajectory has the exact phase.

Derivation and applications

Which quadratic energy does symplectic Euler preserve?

An undamped linear oscillator conserves the physical sum of kinetic and potential energy. A geometric time integrator may conserve a nearby quadratic form instead. Take \(q''=-\omega^2q\) with constant \(\omega\) and unit mass. Scale velocity by setting \(u=\dot q/\omega\) so that both variables have units of length, and write \(z=h\omega\). Use the kick-then-drift version of symplectic Euler. Its step is two triangular shears:

\[u'=u-zq,\]
\[q'=q+zu'=(1-z^2)q+zu .\]

To find a preserved quantity, test a quadratic form \(I=q^2+u^2+\alpha qu\). The changes of the ordinary sum and the product follow directly by multiplication and collection:

\[q'^2+u'^2-q^2-u^2=z(q'u'-qu),\]
\[I'-I=(z+\alpha)(q'u'-qu).\]

Choosing \(\alpha=-z\) cancels the entire change at every step. Completing the square identifies the range where the invariant confines trajectories to closed ellipses:

\[I=q^2+u^2-zqu=(u-zq/2)^2+(1-z^2/4)q^2.\]

For \(|z|<2\) the form is positive definite. The physical energy is proportional to \(q^2+u^2\) and can oscillate, but the invariant bounds it. As \(h\to0\) the tilted ellipse approaches the exact energy circle. At the endpoint \(|z|=2\) the quadratic form ceases to bound both variables; preservation of an indefinite form alone does not guarantee stable trajectories.

Worked example

Follow a moderate step exactly

Take \(\omega=2.00\) s⁻¹, \(h=0.250\) s, \(q=1.00\) m and \(u=0\). Then \(z=0.500\), \(u'=-0.500\) m and \(q'=0.750\) m. The ordinary sum falls from \(1.00\) to \(0.8125\) m². But \(I'=0.8125-0.500(0.750)(-0.500)=1.00\) m². A plot restricted to physical energy at one time could mistake the discrepancy for damping.

Second application

Diagnose a seemingly exact conservation test outside stability

Choose \(z=2.50\) and the same initial \((q,u)=(1,0)\). One step gives \((-5.25,-2.50)\). The ordinary sum is \(33.8125\), whereas the cross term is \(2.50(13.125)=32.8125\), so \(I'=1\). The invariant remains exact while the amplitude grows. Testing positivity of the conserved quadratic form therefore supplies information absent from a conservation-only check.

Choose the diagnostic before choosing the method

A mass on an ideal spring with unit mass and stiffness obeys \(\dot q=p,\ \dot p=-q\). The state moves on circles with conserved energy \(H=(q^2+p^2)/2\). At short times a state norm measures trajectory error, but over many periods there are independent questions about amplitude, phase and energy. A time-stepping method that systematically increases the orbit radius can produce an artificial runaway even on this simplest stable physical system.

Forward Euler updates \(q_{n+1}=q_n+hp_n,\ p_{n+1}=p_n-hq_n\). Square and add: the cross terms cancel, leaving \(H_{n+1}=(1+h^2)H_n\). The phase space area factor of the update matrix is its determinant \(1+h^2\), also greater than one. Backward Euler instead contracts energy by \(1/(1+h^2)\). The second method is stable in the sense of bounded states, but it removes mechanical energy from an undamped model. The sign of drift explains much more here than a solitary endpoint comparison.

Update momentum before position

The momentum-first symplectic Euler scheme sets \(p_{n+1}=p_n-hq_n\) and \(q_{n+1}=q_n+hp_{n+1}\). Its update matrix in \((q,p)\) has rows \((1-h^2,h)\) and \((-h,1)\). Its determinant is one and trace is \(2-h^2\). For \(0<h<2\), the two eigenvalues are a complex conjugate pair of unit magnitude because their product is one and the trace lies strictly between −2 and 2. The resulting numerical frequency satisfies

\[2\cos\theta=2-h^2.\]

Thus phase error still accumulates even if the amplitudes remain bounded.

Direct substitution also verifies an exact discrete invariant \(\tilde H=(q^2+p^2-hqp)/2\). For \(h<2\) the corresponding quadratic form is positive definite, so constant \(\tilde H\) confines the state to an ellipse. The exact physical energy can fluctuate around its initial level as the point moves around that ellipse. Area preservation alone would not guarantee this behaviour: for \(h>2\) the determinant remains one but real reciprocal eigenvalues permit growth. The eigenvalue and invariant calculations supply the missing qualification.

Check a long-time forecast with numbers

Start at \((q,p)=(1,0)\) with \(h=0.1\). Forward Euler gives \((1,-0.1)\) and \(H=0.505\) after one step. After 100 steps the exact multiplicative law predicts

\[H=0.5(1.01)^{100}=1.3524.\]

Symplectic Euler instead gives \((0.99,-0.1)\), \(H=0.49505\), but \(\tilde H=0.5\) again. The first step alone might suggest that the second scheme dissipates energy. The invariant predicts that the decrease cannot continue monotonically over many periods.

Implicit midpoint, \((y_{n+1}-y_n)/h=J(y_n+y_{n+1})/2\) with the skew-symmetric oscillator matrix \(J\), preserves the exact quadratic energy. Dot both sides with the midpoint state. The left side is proportional to \(H_{n+1}-H_n\) and the right side is zero by skew symmetry. Its rotations need not accumulate the exact physical phase, since the rotation angle per step is \(2\arctan(h/2)\). A meaningful long-time study therefore records measured period, energy range and reversibility alongside any state error after a specified number of cycles.

Test an algorithm by reversing the clock

A method is time symmetric if taking a step of length \(h\) and then a step of length \(-h\) restores the starting state, up to arithmetic and solve tolerance. For the midpoint update, exchanging the old and new values leaves their average unchanged and changes both sides of the difference equation consistently. This gives exact reversibility for an accurately solved implicit step. For forward Euler on the oscillator, the product of the forward and backward matrices is \((1+h^2)I\), so reversal actually scales the state.

At \(h=0.1\), the simple reversal from \((1,0)\) therefore returns \((1.01,0)\) for forward Euler, making the failure visible in one pair of steps. This diagnostic is useful only for a reversible underlying evolution paired with its time-reversed integration law. A dissipative or dissipatively constrained physical system has different requirements. Reversibility, energy preservation and phase accuracy probe separate mathematical properties, each with a different operational test.

Test a flow map on a small cell of phase space

For canonical coordinates \(z=(q,p)\), a differentiable Hamiltonian update \(z_+=\Psi(z)\) is symplectic when its Jacobian \(D\Psi\) preserves the matrix \(J=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\) through \(D\Psi^T J D\Psi=J\). Direct multiplication shows that for any real two-by-two matrix \(M\), \(M^TJM=(\det M)J\). Thus in one degree of freedom symplecticity is equivalent to preserving oriented area. A small rectangle of nearby initial conditions should map to a region of the same signed area even when its sides stretch or tilt.

For more than one degree of freedom, preserving ordinary volume is necessary but no longer sufficient. A map may stretch one canonical \((q,p)\) plane and compress another by the reciprocal factor. Its full determinant remains one while the area pairing in each plane changes. Energy is a further, independent diagnostic: an area-preserving map can move individual points among nearby energy contours. The determinant calculation tests a geometric property of the map across neighbouring trajectories, rather than just the energy at one chosen state.

Compare the phase volume of an oscillator ensemble

For \(q'=p,\ p'=-q\) with unit frequency, forward Euler gives \(M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&h\\-h&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), of determinant \(1+h^2\). Backward Euler gives \((I-hA)^{-1}\), where \(A\) has off-diagonal entries \(1,-1\), so its determinant is \(1/(1+h^2)\). A kick followed by a drift gives the symplectic Euler matrix with rows \((1-h^2,h)\) and \((-h,1)\), whose determinant is exactly one. These facts hold for every input rectangle, so they can be checked by integrating its corners rather than relying on a single solution.

Choose \(h=0.2\) and an initial ensemble occupying a rectangle of area 0.01 in normalised phase coordinates. After 25 steps forward Euler predicts area \(0.01(1.04)^{25}\approx0.02666\). Backward Euler gives \(0.01/(1.04)^{25}\approx0.00375\). Symplectic Euler preserves 0.01. Repeated steps compound even a four percent per-step area change into a substantial distortion. For the oscillator all three approximate individual paths can initially look plausible, so this ensemble diagnostic reveals information inaccessible in a short plot of one coordinate.

As a counterexample in four dimensions, scale the first pair of coordinates by \((2,1)\) and the second pair by \((1/2,1)\). The volume multiplier is one, but the first canonical area doubles while the second halves. A full symplecticity test must check \(M^TJM-J\) using the correctly ordered canonical coordinate pairs. An algorithm preserving volume in an arbitrary collection of engineering state units is not automatically qualified for long-time Hamiltonian integration.

Area preservation also survives composing individual canonical shears: the determinants multiply and every shear has determinant one. This explains why reversing the order of a kick and a drift changes the phase error but preserves the two-dimensional area test. The two compositions may therefore produce different numeric positions while agreeing precisely on one geometric invariant. A useful verification suite includes both ensemble area and a trajectory observable with a known time dependence.

Störmer–Verlet and a discrete invariant

Numerical ordinary differential equations

Derive the leapfrog update, identify its oscillatory stability interval and quantify the difference between bounded energy and correct phase.

From first principles

Derive a reversible position update from Newton’s law

For a force that depends only on position, \(m\ddot q=F(q)\), the acceleration can be evaluated at a stored position without an implicit solve. Expand the exact position one step forward and one step backward about the same time \(t_n\). The odd derivatives reverse sign and cancel when the expansions are added:

\[q(t_n+h)+q(t_n-h)=2q(t_n)+h^2\ddot q(t_n)+O(h^4).\]

Replace acceleration by force divided by mass and solve for the forward position. The resulting relation is unchanged if the labels \(n+1\) and \(n-1\) are exchanged:

\[q_{n+1}=2q_n-q_{n-1}+\frac{h^2}{m}F(q_n).\]

Introduce the staggered momentum \(p_{n-1/2}=m(q_n-q_{n-1})/h\). The same algebra becomes an impulse followed by a drift,

\[p_{n+1/2}=p_{n-1/2}+hF(q_n),\]
\[q_{n+1}=q_n+h p_{n+1/2}/m .\]

A specified momentum at \(t=0\) starts the first half step with \(p_{1/2}=p_0+h F(q_0)/2\). For smooth forces the method has second-order position accuracy. It is important to distinguish the staggered momentum from momentum at the stored position time when checking energy.

Derivation and applications

Why a stable Verlet trajectory can arrive at the wrong time

Long-term energy behaviour is important in reversible mechanics, but phase error can dominate a timing measurement. Consider the velocity-independent linear oscillator \(q''=-\omega^2q\). Use a uniform step \(h\), an accurately initialized first point and the position recurrence obtained by eliminating the half-step momenta in Verlet. Write \(z=h\omega\):

\[q_{n+1}-2q_n+q_{n-1}=-z^2q_n .\]

Assume a discrete mode \(q_n=e^{in\vartheta}\). Divide by the mode value at \(n\) and combine the two exponentials. The angle advanced per step obeys

\[2\cos\vartheta-2=-z^2,\]
\[\sin(\vartheta/2)=z/2 .\]

For \(0<z<2\) choose the branch continuous with the exact oscillation at small \(h\). The discrete angular frequency and its expansion are

\[\widetilde\omega=\frac2h\arcsin(z/2)=\omega\left[1+\frac{z^2}{24}+O(z^4)\right].\]

The numerical oscillator runs slightly fast even though its amplitude can remain bounded indefinitely. Its phase lead grows approximately linearly with elapsed time at fixed step. Above \(z=2\) the dispersion relation permits no real phase angle, signaling a separate stability failure. The formula concerns time discretization alone; incorrect initial half-step velocities can add an initial phase offset.

Worked example

Estimate a ten-period timing error

For \(\omega=5.00\) rad/s and \(h=0.100\) s, \(z=0.500\) and \(\widetilde\omega=20\arcsin(0.250)=5.054\) rad/s. Ten true periods last \(12.57\) s. Over that duration the numerical phase gains about \((5.054-5.00)(12.57)\simeq0.674\) rad. The estimated time at which the numerical oscillator completes its tenth period is \(20\pi/5.054=12.43\) s, about \(0.13\) s too soon.

Second application

Select a step from a phase requirement

Suppose 100 periods should accumulate less than \(0.100\) rad of phase error. The leading estimate gives \(2\pi(100)z^2/24<0.100\), so \(z<0.0618\). For \(\omega=5.00\) rad/s this means \(h<0.0124\) s. A choice based only on the stability requirement \(h<0.400\) s would be far too coarse. Halving a small step should reduce the accumulated phase error by approximately four at fixed physical duration.

Position and momentum at staggered times

For unit mass and a time-independent potential \(V(q)\), the equations are

\[q'=p,\ p'=-V'(q).\]

Treat the potential force over a half step, drift with the updated momentum for a full step, then use the new position for the last half kick. This yields

\[p_{n+1/2}=p_n-\tfrac h2V'(q_n),\]
\[q_{n+1}=q_n+hp_{n+1/2},\]
\[p_{n+1}=p_{n+1/2}-\tfrac h2V'(q_{n+1}).\]

Successive half kicks at the same position share a force evaluation. Reversing the sign of \(h\) exactly undoes the steps in reverse order, in exact arithmetic. Subtracting consecutive position increments gives

\[q_{n+1}-2q_n+q_{n-1}=-h^2V'(q_n).\]

The starting position and velocity must initialise the very first half step correctly; using the full velocity as the half-step velocity injects an initial phase error.

The oscillator has an exactly computable modified energy

For \(V=\omega^2q^2/2\) define \(z=h\omega\). The position coefficient of \(q_n\) after one step is

\[a=1-z^2/2;\]

the momentum coefficient of \(q_n\) is \(-h\omega^2(1-z^2/4)\). The full map has equal diagonal entries \(a\) and off-diagonal entries \(h,-hc\) with \(c=\omega^2(1-z^2/4)\). Since \(a^2+h^2c=1\), expanding squares shows \(p_{n+1}^2+cq_{n+1}^2=p_n^2+cq_n^2\).

When \(0<z<2\), \(c>0\) and level sets of this invariant are bounded ellipses. The eigenvalues are \(e^{\pm i\theta}\) with \(\cos\theta=a\). Beyond two, one real eigenvalue has magnitude greater than one and trajectories generically grow. At exactly two the invariant loses control of position and a repeated eigenvalue can cause linear growth. An apparently bounded run starting from specially chosen data at the threshold cannot certify stability for arbitrary perturbations.

Follow a step and its timing

For \(\omega=1,h=1,q_0=1,p_0=0\), the half kick gives \(-0.5\), the drift gives \(q_1=0.5\), and the final half kick gives \(p_1=-0.75\). The physical energy decreases from 0.5 to 0.40625 in this first step. The modified energy \((p^2+0.75q^2)/2\) equals 0.375 at both points. The discrete phase is \(\theta=\pi/3\), so the computed trajectory returns after six steps. The true oscillator returns after \(2\pi\approx6.283\) seconds. A stable trajectory with a bounded energy error can therefore accumulate a large timing error over many cycles.

For small \(z\), expanding \(\theta=2\arcsin(z/2)\) gives

\[\theta=z+z^3/24+O(z^5).\]

At a fixed final time the accumulated phase error is therefore second order in \(h\). A period estimate and an energy trace probe different aspects of long-time integration and should be reported separately.

Why the initial velocity test matters

A test with only the initial position nonzero may conceal the borderline instability at \(h\omega=2\). At that boundary the update is

\[q_{n+1}=-q_n+hp_n,\ p_{n+1}=-p_n.\]

For \(p_0=0\) the positions merely alternate sign forever. With \(q_0=0\) and \(p_0=1\), however, explicit iteration gives

\[p_n=(-1)^n,\]
\[q_n=(-1)^{n-1}nh.\]

The position magnitude grows linearly. A stability test must account for all independent starting directions or small perturbations of the chosen one, especially when an eigenvalue of modulus one is repeated.

Prove conservation of angular momentum by individual substeps

For a particle of constant mass in a radial potential \(V(\|q\|)\), the force is parallel to the position vector. Its angular momentum is \(L=q\times p\). During a kick of duration \(\tau\), momentum changes by \(\tau F(q)\) at fixed position, so the change in \(L\) is

\[\tau q\times F(q)=0.\]

During a drift, position changes by \(\tau p/m\) at fixed momentum, giving change \((\tau/m)p\times p=0\). The full kick–drift–kick map therefore preserves angular momentum exactly in arithmetic without rounding, for any step length for which these evaluations are defined.

The proof uses radial force and the relationship between velocity and momentum. An external noncentral force exerts a physical torque, while a coordinate-dependent mass matrix changes the drift subflow. Under either modification the two zero cross products need rechecking. The argument also gives no bound on energy or phase. Exact conservation of angular momentum constrains orbital geometry but leaves freedom to move from the correct energy level onto a nearby one or to return to a point on the orbit at the wrong time.

Compare three diagnostics on an elliptical oscillator trajectory

Use unit mass and two-dimensional potential \(V=(x^2+y^2)/2\). Start at \(q=(1,0)\), \(p=(0,2)\), so \(L=2\) and physical energy is

\[E=(1+4)/2=2.5.\]

At step \(h=0.5\), the first half kick gives

\[p_{1/2}=(-0.25,2).\]

The drift leads to \(q_1=(0.875,1)\). The last half kick subtracts \(0.25q_1\), giving \(p_1=(-0.46875,1.75)\). The resulting planar angular momentum is \(0.875(1.75)-1(-0.46875)=2\), exactly as proved.

Calculate energy separately. The squared position is \(0.875^2+1=1.765625\), and the squared momentum is

\[0.46875^2+1.75^2=3.2822265625.\]

Hence the physical energy after this step is 2.52392578125, about 0.96 percent above the initial value. The exact continuous position at this time is \((\cos0.5,2\sin0.5)\approx(0.87758,0.95885)\), so the position error is concentrated in the second coordinate. Angular momentum, physical energy and the individual trajectory coordinates expose three distinct properties of the same update.

Because the harmonic force is separable by coordinate, the modified invariant from the scalar oscillator adds across the two directions: \([\|p\|^2+(1-h^2/4)\|q\|^2]/2\) is constant. Substitution gives 2.46875 at both steps, independently checking the arithmetic. For a general radial potential this particular quadratic modified invariant no longer follows, while the angular momentum proof remains valid. A conservation claim should identify the symmetry responsible rather than assuming every observed invariant survives a change of potential.

If there were a small constant transverse force, the physical change in angular momentum would be the time integral of \(q\times F_{\rm ext}\). A better diagnostic would subtract the accumulated applied torque from the measured change in \(L\), rather than demand that \(L\) remain fixed. This distinction also guards against a subtle implementation error: zeroing a real torque to improve a conservation plot would change the physical problem and could hide a significant trajectory bias.

Measuring the signed torque makes its direction as well as its magnitude available for this balance check.

Estimate the phase error of a stable Verlet orbit

Eliminate momentum from velocity Verlet for the harmonic oscillator. A discrete sinusoid obeys \(\cos\theta=1-(h\omega)^2/2\). Its angular frequency is \(\theta/h\); divide by \(\omega\) before comparing steps.

Implicit midpoint, reversibility and the phase of an oscillator

Numerical ordinary differential equations

Imagine integrating a mechanical trajectory forward, saving only its end state and then running the same method backward. Recovering the start is a stronger demand than obtaining a plausibly small one-step error. Even exact recovery does not guarantee that an oscillation peaked at the correct time.

Build a method from a centred difference

Integrate \(y'=f(t,y)\) over an interval of length \(h\). Midpoint quadrature approximates the integral by \(h\) times its integrand at \(t_n+h/2\). The arithmetic mean of the unknown endpoint states approximates the exact midpoint state up to order \(h^2\) on a smooth trajectory. Using this mean as the derivative's state argument gives

\[y_{n+1}-y_n=h f\!\left(t_n+h/2,(y_n+y_{n+1})/2\right).\]

Errors from midpoint quadrature and from evaluating \(f\) at the approximate midpoint both contribute \(O(h^3)\) locally, giving global order two on a fixed interval under the usual regularity and stability conditions. In general the unknown appears inside \(f\), so an implicit equation must be solved. If \(f\) is Lipschitz with constant \(L_f\), direct fixed-point iteration contracts when \(|h|L_f/2<1\). Newton iteration can converge over a wider range. Neither a failed nonlinear solve nor loose tolerances inherit the exact algebraic properties of the method.

Exchange \(y_n,y_{n+1}\) and replace \(h\) by \(-h\). The left side changes sign, the arithmetic midpoint is identical and the midpoint time is identical if the reverse step starts at \(t_{n+1}\). Thus, on the same locally unique solution branch,

\[\Phi_{-h}=\Phi_h^{-1}.\]

A round trip using variable steps must repeat the accepted steps in reverse order. Independently choosing a new adaptive mesh on the way back need not retrace the states.

Solve the step for a harmonic oscillator

Write \(q'=\omega p,\ p'=-\omega q\), with scaled momentum \(p\) in the same units as \(q\), and \(z=q+ip\). The ODE becomes

\[z'=-i\omega z.\]

Solving the linear midpoint equation gives the scalar multiplier

\[M(u)=\frac{1-iu/2}{1+iu/2}=\exp[-2i\arctan(u/2)],\]
\[u=\omega h.\]

Its numerator and denominator are complex conjugates. Hence it preserves \(q^2+p^2\), proportional to the oscillator energy, exactly in real arithmetic. For any radius or step size \(M(-u)M(u)=1\). The rotation per step is

\[\theta=2\arctan(u/2)=u-u^3/12+O(u^5).\]

The numerical phase lags by \(u-\theta\) per step. After \(N\) steps the accumulated lag is \(N(u-\theta)\), increasing linearly in elapsed time even while the energy remains exactly constant.

Compare explicit midpoint Runge–Kutta. A half Euler predictor leads to

\[M_{\rm RK2}=1-iu-u^2/2.\]

It also has order two, but \(|M_{\rm RK2}|^2=1+u^4/4\). The amplitude grows slowly at every nonzero step. A forward and reverse pair multiplies the starting amplitude by \(1+u^4/4\). In a conservative problem these two second-order procedures can produce visibly different long records.

Inspect the return and the lag separately

With \(\omega=1\), \(h=0.5\), \(N=20\), exact accumulation is 10 radians. Midpoint gives \(40\arctan(0.25)=9.7991\) radians, a lag of \(0.2009\) radians. The explicit round trip multiplies the radius by \((1+0.5^4/4)^{20}\simeq1.364\). The implicit round trip returns the original radius and phase to roundoff. Run both updates below. Compare results at equal elapsed time by halving \(h\) and doubling \(N\). The phase error should drop by about a factor of four. The graph uses computed steps for the methods and a more finely sampled exact reference.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on designing an accuracy criterion

A solver preserves the harmonic energy exactly over a simulation of 100 time units with \(\omega=1\). A measurement also requires phase error below 0.01 radians. Use the leading midpoint error to estimate an admissible step. How would you verify it?

Work through the solution

The accumulated phase error is approximately \(T\omega^3h^2/12\). Solving \(100h^2/12<0.01\) gives \(h<0.0346\). Verify at a chosen integer step count using \(T-2N\arctan[T/(2N)]\), or by convergence against a refined trajectory that measures the phase continuously. Energy alone has zero error for all these steps, so it cannot certify the phase target.

Further reading · Hairer's TUM geometric numerical integration course, symmetric methods.

What different observers measure

Waves, radiation and relativity

A moving observer carries clocks, rulers and a calibrated test charge. A statement about the electric field must specify which observer held that test charge at rest. In this chapter we derive how different measurements can agree on the force law and yet assign different electric fields, magnetic fields and charge densities to the same physical apparatus. A current-carrying wire and a light pulse will provide independent checks.

Events, synchronisation and directed boosts

Use two inertial coordinate systems with aligned axes. The primed origin moves at speed \(v\) in the positive laboratory \(x\) direction. Choose coincident origins when both clocks read zero and synchronise distant clocks in each frame by light signals. Homogeneity makes the transformation linear. The worldline of the primed origin requires

\[x'=A(x-vt).\]

A light ray moving along either axial direction must still obey \(x'=\pm ct'\). Substituting its two possible worldlines into the linear map fixes the relative coefficients of \(x\) and \(t\) in \(t'\). Reversing the transformation by changing the sign of \(v\) fixes the remaining scale.

\[x'=\gamma(x-vt),\]
\[t'=\gamma(t-vx/c^2),\]
\[y'=y,\]
\[z'=z,\]
\[\gamma=(1-v^2/c^2)^{-1/2}.\]

The squared interval \(c^2\Delta t^2-|\Delta\mathbf r|^2\) is unchanged, a direct algebra check that detects a surprising number of sign choices. Two sparks separated by 3.0 m along a laboratory bench and simultaneous on laboratory clocks have \(\Delta t'=-7.51\) ns for \(v=0.60c\), if the second event is at larger \(x\). Their ordering in this frame is consistent with their spacelike separation. Events joined by a light signal are null-separated and retain their order under this boost.

Length measurements need two simultaneous endpoint readings in the chosen frame. A bar at rest in the lab and parallel to the axis has length \(L/\gamma\) in the primed frame. Substituting the simultaneous laboratory endpoint events into the boost instead gives \(\gamma L\), but their primed times differ. This distinction reappears when we count charge per unit length in a moving wire. The result of that count depends on the spacetime slice used to perform it.

A current is a rate of charge crossing a surface

Begin with a single population of particles, each carrying charge \(q\), whose proper number density is \(n_0\). Their counting volume is Lorentz-contracted only along their motion. The lab assigns density \(n=\gamma_u n_0\) and current density \(q n\mathbf u\). Both are components of \(j^\mu=q n_0 u^\mu\), where \(u^\mu=\gamma_u(c,\mathbf u)\) is the four-velocity. Independent species can be superposed. A total four-current can consequently be spacelike even though every material particle moves more slowly than light.

\[\rho'=\gamma(\rho-vJ_x/c^2),\]
\[J'_x=\gamma(J_x-v\rho),\]
\[J'_y=J_y,\]
\[J'_z=J_z .\]

The usual continuity law becomes \(\partial_\mu j^\mu=0\) with \(\partial_0=c^{-1}\partial_t\). Transformation of the derivative and of the current cancels exactly in this contraction. An isolated finite charge distribution has an observer-independent total charge if each observer integrates through a complete spacelike slice. One way to see this is to connect the two slices by a large timelike enclosing wall. Four-dimensional flux conservation sets the difference between the two charge integrals equal to the current crossing the wall. For a wall outside all the charge trajectories that last contribution vanishes.

For a charge-neutral straight wire carrying conventional current \(I=2.0\) A along \(+x\), a frame moving parallel to the wire at \(0.20c\) assigns line density \(\lambda'=-\gamma vI/c^2=-1.36\) nC/m. Transverse cross-section is unchanged. There is no requirement that electrons themselves travel at \(0.20c\). Both the mobile and immobile species contribute to the transformed density. The ideal wire is infinitely long, so one cannot integrate its transformed charge density out to infinity and apply the finite isolated system argument.

Obtaining field components from measured forces

Place a test charge at rest at an event in the primed frame. In the lab it has velocity \(v\mathbf e_x\) and force \(q(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\). To transform the force, transform the corresponding momentum change. For a small transverse kick, \(dp'_y=dp_y\) while \(dt'=dt/\gamma\) on the test charge's path. Thus \(F'_y=\gamma F_y\) and similarly for \(z\). For the parallel component use \(dp'_x=\gamma(dp_x-v\,d\mathcal E/c^2)\) and \(d\mathcal E/dt=vF_x\). The result is

\[F'_x=F_x.\]

Dividing by \(q\) gives the electric field. Requiring the same force law for charges moving transversely, or transforming the magnetic flux measured by a moving loop, gives the magnetic field.

\[\mathbf E'_\parallel=\mathbf E_\parallel,\]
\[\mathbf B'_\parallel=\mathbf B_\parallel,\]
\[\mathbf E'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)_\perp,\]
\[\mathbf B'_\perp=\gamma(\mathbf B-\mathbf v\times\mathbf E/c^2)_\perp .\]

Imagine a uniform \(+y\) electric field of 90.0 kV/m and a \(+z\) magnetic field with \(cB=150\) kV/m. For a boost along \(+x\), the \(y\) component of \(\mathbf v\times\mathbf B\) is negative. At \(\beta=0.60\) the electric field vanishes. The remaining magnetic field has \(cB'=1.25(150-54)=120\) kV/m or 0.400 mT. A particle comoving with that frame feels no force at that event. A different particle moving across the magnetic field can still curve.

Vary the observer speed and compare the electric and magnetic field invariants

Compare two force measurements near an ideal wire

Return to the 2.0 A wire and put the observation point 1.0 cm along \(+y\). Ampère's law gives \(B_z=\mu_0I/(2\pi r)=40.0\) microtesla. The lab radial electric field is zero in this idealised symmetric model. A positive test charge moving along \(+x\) at \(0.20c\) experiences an inward magnetic force per unit charge of 2.40 kV/m. In its momentary rest frame the wire's negative line charge produces an inward radial electric field. The field from Gauss's law is \(|\lambda'|/(2\pi\epsilon_0r)=2.45\) kV/m. The difference in the reported transverse forces is precisely the factor \(\gamma=1.0206\) derived from the momentum kick.

There is no contradiction between a field with zero laboratory divergence outside the wire and a different electric field in the primed frame. The charge density at the wire itself also transforms. If the laboratory wire carries a real driving surface charge and a return conductor, add their fields and charges before transforming. A claimed measurement of this ideal result without specifying the return path and electrostatic shielding needs a separate model for those additional fields.

When can a field be made purely electric or magnetic?

Direct substitution in the transverse transformation leaves the scalars \(B^2-E^2/c^2\) and \(\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B\) unchanged. The first is a difference, so retain its sign. If the second is nonzero, no observer sees either field vanish. If the fields are orthogonal and \(E<cB\), choose \(\mathbf v=\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\). The vector identity for \((\mathbf E\times\mathbf B)\times\mathbf B\) cancels \(\mathbf E\), and the required speed is below \(c\). With \(E>cB\), choosing \(c^2\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/E^2\) instead cancels \(\mathbf B\).

When both invariants vanish one must still distinguish a field identically zero from a nonzero null field. A vacuum travelling plane wave satisfies \(E=cB\) and orthogonality everywhere. The advertised boost would then require speed \(c\). An observer can lower its measured frequency and amplitude continuously by following the wave at increasing speed but can never transform away its nonzero field. A superposition of waves need not be null. A standing wave has positions and times where only one of the fields is present.

Phase matching across inertial frames

Every observer agrees on whether a particular spacetime event is a crest. The phase \(\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf r-\omega t\) therefore labels the same event in each frame. Substituting the inverse coordinate transformation gives the transformation of frequency and wavevector. For vacuum propagation at laboratory angle \(\theta\) to the boost, divide the transformed longitudinal wavenumber by the transformed magnitude.

\[\omega'=\gamma\omega(1-\beta\cos\theta),\]
\[\cos\theta'=\frac{\cos\theta-\beta}{1-\beta\cos\theta}.\]

An observer chasing a collinear 600 nm light wave at \(0.60c\) sees half its frequency and a wavelength of 1200 nm. A beam entering at \(90^\circ\) to the boost appears at \(\cos\theta'=-0.60\) and frequency \(1.25\omega\). Asking for a transverse Doppler shift without saying in whose frame the angle is a right angle leaves the problem under-specified. A detector counts phase fronts crossing its own trajectory and its own clock sets the elapsed time.

A boosted light beam also transports momentum

Frequency alone does not specify the energy measured by a moving detector. Its collecting surface and clock must be assigned to an observer. For a monochromatic collimated beam in vacuum, one route is to transform both fields and calculate their new Poynting vector. Another route is to transform the stress-energy tensor, whose energy density, momentum density and momentum flux describe the same field together. This second route shows explicitly which measured quantity is being transformed and extends to superposed beams with zero mean momentum in one frame.

For a right-moving plane wave along \(x\), the energy density is \(u=S_x/c\), momentum density is \(u/c\) and normal momentum flux is \(u\). In coordinates with \(x^0=ct\), the relevant block has \(T^{00}=T^{01}=T^{10}=T^{11}=u\). Boost both indices using the same Lorentz matrix as for coordinates. The transformed energy density follows directly:

\[T'^{00}=\gamma^2(T^{00}-2\beta T^{01}+\beta^2T^{11})=\gamma^2(1-\beta)^2u .\]

The frequency Doppler factor enters twice. One factor corresponds to lower energy per photon and one to the changed number per unit volume. For a finite pulse, its length at a common primed time grows for an observer following it, so the total energy changes by only one Doppler factor.
