Volumes measured between equal-time spatial endpoints must be computed on the appropriate observer's simultaneity slice, just as for a moving rod.

Now superpose two equal-intensity mutually incoherent plane waves in opposite axial directions. In the laboratory \(T^{01}=0\), while \(T^{11}=T^{00}=u\) for the combined radiation. An observer moving at \(0.60c\) obtains \(u'=\gamma^2(1+\beta^2)u=2.125u\), with net flux \(S'_x=-2\gamma^2\beta\,cu=-1.875\,cu\). The counterpropagating beam that the observer meets head-on is blueshifted, while the other is redshifted. Energy density remains greater than the magnitude of momentum density times \(c\), as required for a superposition of distinct propagation directions. This check fails if one transforms a zero Poynting vector as though it were the spatial component of a four-vector with no stress information.

The direction-independent radiation field in an ideal cavity has \(T^{ii}=u/3\) for each spatial axis in its rest frame rather than \(T^{11}=u\). The one-third comes from averaging \(n_i^2\) over a sphere. The moving observer then sees \(u'=\gamma^2u(1+\beta^2/3)\) and a net longitudinal flux. At \(\beta=0.60\) the energy-density factor is 1.75. An isotropic thermal field becomes directionally distorted; assigning one Doppler factor to the whole sky would give neither the correct integrated energy density nor its directional spectrum.

A moving parallel-plate capacitor tests surface sources

Choose large parallel conducting plates at \(z=\pm d/2\) carrying opposite uniform surface densities \(\pm\sigma\). Ignore edge effects in the central region. In the lab the field between them has magnitude \(E_0=\sigma/\epsilon_0\) and there is no magnetic field. A frame boosted along \(+x\) sees both plates move at \(-v\). Their areas contract along \(x\), so their surface charge magnitudes become \(\gamma\sigma\); their equal and opposite surface currents point in opposite senses. Use Gauss's law and the magnetic jump condition on each sheet to reconstruct the transformed fields. The current sheets reinforce magnetically between the plates and cancel outside, just as electric sheets of opposite charge do electrostatically.

For laboratory electric field along \(+z\), the moving observer has \(E'_z=\gamma E_0\) and \(B'_y=+\gamma vE_0/c^2\). Cross the primed plate velocity \(-v\mathbf e_x\) with this magnetic field to find a force along \(-z\) on a positive surface charge, partly cancelling the enhanced electric attraction towards the opposite plate. When finding the force on a sheet, use the fields of the other sheet or the arithmetic mean of the two limiting fields. Substituting the total interior field without removing the self contribution would double the result.

At \(E_0=2.00\) MV/m and \(\beta=0.60\), the primed interior field is 2.50 MV/m and its magnetic field 5.00 mT. Per positive surface charge the transverse attraction from the opposite sheet has magnitude \(\gamma E_0(1-\beta^2)/2=E_0/(2\gamma)\). This is 0.800 MV/m, compared with 1.00 MV/m in the laboratory. The transformation factor is inverse \(\gamma\) because the charge in this example is at rest in the original laboratory; the earlier force derivation placed the test charge at rest in the primed frame. Interchanging those initial conditions without changing the time conversion reverses the factor.

Multiplying the primed force per charge by the larger surface density \(\gamma\sigma\) restores the same force per transverse plate area in this tangential boost. The cancellation supplies a local cross-check among length contraction, field transformation and mechanical force. A complete calculation of the total energy or momentum of an accelerated finite capacitor must also account for stresses in its supports and for the fields near its edges. Taking the uniform central field times a transformed volume is adequate for a local comparison, but does not by itself describe that whole closed mechanical system.

Test a field transformation with an invariant

Change observer speed, compare the separately transformed fields and inspect the invariant combination. Agree on the event before comparing measurements.

Open this experiment online

Worked observer problem · a null of one component does not imply a vanishing field

At one event a laboratory finds \(\mathbf E=(30,40,0)\) kV/m and \(c\mathbf B=(0,0,50)\) kV/m. Another laboratory reports that it can remove the magnetic field by boosting along the direction of \(\mathbf E\times\mathbf B\). Test the claim and predict the fields for a boost of speed \(0.80c\) along this direction. Does the local measurement establish that this is an infinite plane wave?

Evaluate the invariants and transform the transverse field

The electric and scaled magnetic fields both have magnitude 50 kV/m and are perpendicular. Both quadratic Lorentz invariants vanish, while the fields themselves are nonzero. With \(\mathbf v\) along \(\mathbf E\times\mathbf B\), both field vectors are perpendicular to the boost. The cross terms reduce their magnitudes by a common factor \(\gamma(1-\beta)=1/3\) for this speed, so \(E'=cB'=16.7\) kV/m. Their directions in this transverse configuration are unchanged. No subluminal boost removes either nonzero field, as follows also by applying the inverse transformation to an alleged zero field.

A pulse at one point can have these local field values, as can the field near a suitable superposition at a particular event. A local tensor and its invariants contain no information about all the field derivatives elsewhere. To establish a plane-wave model measure spatial phase across transverse probes, verify the propagation relation at separated stations and bound amplitude variation over the required observation region. Infinity is an idealisation established only to a scale specified by the experiment.

As an error check, compare the measured difference \(E^2-c^2B^2\) with the propagated uncertainties of both meters. Small nonzero estimated differences near a null may be statistically insignificant, while a systematic alignment error in \(\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf B\) can obscure orthogonality. Classifying a field as electric-dominated or magnetic-dominated from a difference of nearly equal large squares requires an uncertainty budget. The exact mathematical classification and the experimental confidence in assigning it answer different questions.

Recover a source direction from two clocks and a known speed

Two perfectly synchronised receivers travel with equal speed in opposite directions along the laboratory axis. They sample the same monochromatic plane wave. Their measured frequencies are proportional to \(1-\beta\cos\theta\) and \(1+\beta\cos\theta\), with a common Lorentz factor. Taking their difference divided by their sum determines \(\beta\cos\theta\) without prior knowledge of emitted frequency. Their sum then fixes that frequency once the speed is calibrated. The construction combines two observer assignments rather than treating a transverse Doppler correction as an unknown empirical shift.

For speeds of half the speed of light and a measured frequency ratio of three to two, with the receiver travelling toward negative \(x\) recording the higher value, the inferred cosine is 0.40. If their average measured frequency is 600 THz, the emitted laboratory frequency is about 520 THz. A second direction on the opposite side of the boost axis has the same cosine, so the two receivers alone cannot determine the full incoming unit vector.

A third receiver with a nonparallel velocity supplies another directional projection. Detector clocks must be calibrated in their respective rest frames and each must see the same stable source frequency. If the source itself chirps between noncoincident measurements, that chirp can imitate an angular shift. Using overlapping signals and comparing phase histories addresses this limitation more directly than quoting only two peak frequencies.

Tutorial · Infer charge and current from two observers

A uniform channel carries an unknown lab line charge \(\lambda\) and current \(I\) along its axis. Observers moving at \(+0.30c\) and \(-0.30c\) measure line densities 1.0 and 3.0 nC/m respectively. Recover the laboratory quantities. Can a third observer travel alongside this current so that the measured current vanishes?

Recover the two independent quantities and classify the current

Adding the readings gives \(2\gamma\lambda=4.0\) nC/m, so \(\lambda=1.908\) nC/m. Subtraction gives \(2\gamma\beta I/c=2.0\) nC/m and hence \(I=0.953\) A. Reinsert both signs in the original equations as a check. The speed needed to cancel the current would be \(I/\lambda=5.00\times10^8\) m/s. It exceeds the speed of light. The current four-vector is spacelike since \(I^2>(c\lambda)^2\).

A frame with zero line charge can instead move at \(v=c^2\lambda/I=0.600c\). A spacelike total current is possible for superposed charge carriers of opposite sign. The calculation would rule out a model consisting solely of one sign of carriers all propagating with a common subluminal drift velocity. It does not rule out the measured pair of densities.

Tutorial · What does a moving pulse detector count?

A 10.0 ns rectangular-envelope light pulse moves in \(+x\) in vacuum. A detector moves along the same direction at \(0.60c\). Compute the interval between its encounters with the leading and trailing edges using laboratory kinematics, then its own clock. Predict its measured peak intensity and fluence relative to a stationary detector for a broad collimated beam.

Track the two crossing events and transform the flux

The pulse is \(c(10.0\,\mathrm{ns})\) long in the lab. Its back approaches the moving detector at coordinate closing speed \(c-v=0.40c\), so the two events occur 25.0 ns apart in laboratory time. Both events lie on the detector worldline, so its proper interval is 20.0 ns. This agrees with the inverse Doppler factor of two. A pulse of enough cycles to define a carrier now has half the frequency and twice its envelope duration.

For a transverse plane wave the field amplitude transforms as \(\gamma(1-\beta)=0.50\) times the laboratory value. The primed Poynting flux is therefore one quarter as large. Integrating over twice the proper duration gives one half the original energy per unit transverse area. The cross section perpendicular to the boost is unchanged. Photons are registered with half their laboratory energy and the same total number if the beam is entirely collected, an independent check on the fluence result.

Cambridge Electromagnetism · electromagnetism and relativity

Relativistic particle dynamics and the field tensor

Waves, radiation and relativity

A circular track measures momentum per charge. A clock following the particle registers proper time, and a detector records how much kinetic energy it has gained. We will put these observations in one dynamical equation, solve that equation in uniform fields and exhibit a numerical pusher that can get the energy right while its orbit phase slowly slips.

What the track, clock and calorimeter measure

Consider a particle of invariant mass \(m\), signed charge \(q\) and ordinary velocity \(\mathbf v\). Inertial observers agree on the proper time between neighbouring events on its timelike worldline,

\[d\tau=dt\sqrt{1-v^2/c^2}.\]

Adopt coordinates \(x^\mu=(ct,x,y,z)\) and metric \(g=\mathrm{diag}(1,-1,-1,-1)\). Dividing the displacement by proper time produces a vector of fixed Minkowski norm. Multiplying it by invariant mass gives four-momentum.

\[u^\mu=\gamma(c,\mathbf v),\]
\[p^\mu=mu^\mu=(\mathcal E/c,\mathbf p),\]
\[\mathcal E^2-c^2|\mathbf p|^2=m^2c^4 .\]

Energy includes the rest term. Momentum is \(\gamma m\mathbf v\). Differentiating the last equation on a trajectory gives

\[d\mathcal E/dt=\mathbf v\cdot d\mathbf p/dt.\]

This identity replaces the nonrelativistic step of dotting \(m\mathbf a\) with velocity. A magnetic field can rotate momentum with fixed magnitude and energy, whereas an electric field can change energy by doing work.

Suppose an electron begins at rest and gains 10.0 keV of kinetic energy across an electrostatic potential difference in vacuum. Its rest energy is 511 keV, giving \(\gamma=1.01957\) and \(v/c=\sqrt{1-1/\gamma^2}=0.1950\). The nonrelativistic estimate is 0.1978. In a modest energy measurement the discrepancy could lie below the experimental spread. For a precise orbit calculation the 1.96 percent change in relativistic cyclotron period is measurable independently of that discrepancy in speed.

A linear map that produces the Lorentz force

Seek a local equation linear in field and four-velocity and covariant under boosts. For the energy component to give \(q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v\), and for the spatial components to give the electric force and the oriented cross product, construct the antisymmetric field tensor. With the stated metric and \(\epsilon^{123}=1\), set

\[F^{0i}=-E_i/c,\]
\[F^{i0}=E_i/c,\]
\[F^{ij}=-\epsilon^{ijk}B_k,\]
\[\frac{dp^\mu}{d\tau}=qF^{\mu\nu}u_\nu .\]

Lowering the index on \(u\) reverses the signs of its spatial components. The spatial product becomes \(q\gamma(\mathbf E+\mathbf v\times\mathbf B)\), and division by \(\gamma\) converts the proper-time derivative to \(d\mathbf p/dt\). The time component similarly yields

\[d\mathcal E/dt=q\mathbf E\cdot\mathbf v.\]

Contract the force with \(u_\mu\). The symmetric product \(u_\mu u_\nu\) annihilates the antisymmetric tensor, which proves that the evolution preserves the mass shell.

To recover Maxwell's kinematic constraints from potentials, put \(A^\mu=(\phi/c,\mathbf A)\) and \(F^{\mu\nu}=\partial^\mu A^\nu-\partial^\nu A^\mu\). Then \(F^{0i}=(\partial_t A_i+\partial_i\phi)/c=-E_i/c\), while the spatial-spatial components are the magnetic curl. Adding a four-gradient to \(A^\mu\) leaves the tensor unchanged because ordinary derivatives commute. The particle action \(S=-mc^2\int d\tau-q\int A_\mu dx^\mu\) gives the same force after integrating variations by parts with fixed endpoints. Gauge changes add only an endpoint term to this action. A trajectory measured with local forces cannot distinguish such potential representatives.

Compare the potentials and field-based description in the field-tensor chapter

Constant force, variable coordinate acceleration

Put a particle initially at rest in a uniform static electric field along \(x\). Let \(a_0=qE/m\) be signed. Integration of \(dp_x/dt=qE\) gives \(p_x=ma_0t\), so the speed tends to \(c\) even while momentum increases linearly. Proper-time evolution is simplest in terms of rapidity \(\eta\), where \(u^0=c\cosh\eta\), \(u^x=c\sinh\eta\). The two tensor equations imply \(d\eta/d\tau=a_0/c\).

\[t=\frac{c}{a_0}\sinh(a_0\tau/c),\]
\[x=\frac{c^2}{a_0}\{\cosh(a_0\tau/c)-1\},\]
\[\frac{dv_x}{dt}=\frac{a_0}{\gamma^3}.\]

The initial time and position are zero. The work \(qEx=mc^2(\cosh\eta-1)\) equals kinetic energy, which checks both the integration constant and the dimensional factor in the displacement. For an electron use the direction of the electric force as positive to simplify signs. At 20.0 kV/m it gains 1.0 keV in 5.0 cm. The energy gain follows from the static potential even though the speed does not grow exactly as the square root of distance. The field region must last long enough for the particle to cross it and fringe fields can alter the entry trajectory.

Compare momentum, coordinate time and proper time in the charged-worldline experiment

Helical motion, radius and period

A uniform magnetic field along \(z\) keeps \(p_z\), energy and \(|\mathbf p_\perp|\) fixed. Write \(p_x+ip_y\) as a complex number. The Lorentz force reduces to a first-order rotation equation and a single time integration gives the corresponding position. With positive \(B\), positive charges starting along \(+x\) initially turn towards \(-y\).

\[\frac{d}{dt}(p_x+ip_y)=-i\frac{qB}{\gamma m}(p_x+ip_y),\]
\[R=\frac{p_\perp}{|q|B},\]
\[T=\frac{2\pi\gamma m}{|q|B}.\]

The helical pitch is \(v_zT\). Radius gives transverse momentum and a calibrated period gives total energy through \(\gamma\). A track seen only in projection along the field gives no direct evidence that longitudinal momentum vanishes. A second view or a timing measurement can resolve this ambiguity.

At 10.0 keV the electron has \(pc=\sqrt{K(K+2mc^2)}=101.6\) keV. With entry perpendicular to a 2.0 mT field its radius is 0.169 m and its period 18.2 ns. Doubling the field halves both radius and period at fixed injection energy. Doubling momentum at fixed field doubles radius but changes period by the ratio of Lorentz factors. These are different tests of a track calibration. A beam with a spread of energies will dephase relative to a narrow timing gate even when every single particle remains in a perfectly uniform field.

A drift frame gives an exact crossed-field solution

For constant crossed fields with \(E<cB\), transform to the frame moving at \(\mathbf v_d=\mathbf E\times\mathbf B/B^2\). There \(E'=0\) and \(B'=\sqrt{B^2-E^2/c^2}\); calculate the transformed initial momentum and integrate a helix. Transform two successive equal-phase events back to the lab. Their perpendicular displacement in the electric-free frame is zero. If the motion has no component parallel to \(\mathbf B\), the entire displacement there is zero, and the average lab cycle velocity is exactly \(\mathbf v_d\). The detailed lab trace need not be circular and the instantaneous lab energy may oscillate.

Take \(E=30.0\) kV/m along \(y\) and \(B=0.50\) mT along \(z\). The drift is along \(+x\) at \(6.00\times10^7\) m/s. A particle injected at that velocity crosses the uniform fields on a straight line because the forces cancel. An initially stationary positive particle instead begins by accelerating along \(+y\). These observations can use the same field with different injected particles. If \(E\) exceeds \(cB\), this electric-free frame is unavailable. Transform to a magnetic-free frame and integrate electric acceleration instead.

Continuous rotation and a discrete experiment

A naive forward step \(\mathbf p_{n+1}=\mathbf p_n+q\Delta t\,\mathbf v_n\times\mathbf B\) adds a vector perpendicular to the previous momentum. Its squared norm increases by the squared magnitude of that kick. The apparent heating will persist in perfectly static uniform magnetic fields. For a purely magnetic update, evaluate the direction using the mean of old and new momenta. At fixed \(\gamma\), the resulting implicit two-dimensional linear map can be solved algebraically and is an exact rotation by a modified angle.

\[\delta=2\arctan\!\left(\frac{\omega_c\Delta t}{2}\right),\]
\[\omega_c=\frac{qB}{\gamma m}.\]

The sign of rotation follows the earlier complex equation. Dotting the update with the mean momentum gives zero change in squared norm, which verifies the energy property even for relatively large time steps. A common electromagnetic pusher surrounds the magnetic rotation by two electric half-kicks. In spatially varying fields, specify at what time and position each field is sampled, and check spatial interpolation and time convergence. For ultrarelativistic nearly cancelling electric and magnetic fields, preserving rotation in a pure field alone does not ensure cancellation is represented accurately.

Solve the constant tensor without transforming every time step

When uniform electric and magnetic fields are constant in spacetime, the Lorentz equation is a linear constant-coefficient equation for four-velocity as a function of proper time. Nonlinearity in ordinary time hides that simple structure, because the rate at which proper time accumulates changes along the orbit. Define the mixed tensor \(M^\mu{}_\nu=(q/m)F^{\mu\alpha}g_{\alpha\nu}\). In a uniform field it does not vary along the trajectory. A formal solution is then

\[u(\tau)=e^{M\tau}u(0),\]
\[x(\tau)-x(0)=\int_0^\tau e^{Ms}u(0)\,ds .\]

The coordinate formula is written as an integral since \(M\) can be singular, as it is in a purely magnetic field. Blindly replacing this integral by \(M^{-1}(e^{M\tau}-I)\) fails in exactly such simple cases. A power series for the integral or a block matrix exponential treats zero eigenvalues without an artificial inverse. Antisymmetry of the tensor implies \(M^{\mathsf T}g+gM=0\). Differentiating \(e^{M^{\mathsf T}\tau}ge^{M\tau}\) shows that it stays equal to \(g\), making preservation of \(u^\mu u_\mu=c^2\) a structural property of the exact mapping.

For parallel constant fields along \(z\), the matrix decomposes into a boost in the \(ct,z\) subspace and a rotation in \(x,y\). Write \(\alpha=qE/(mc)\), \(\Omega=qB/m\), and denote the initial four-velocity components by \(u_0^\mu\). The proper-time solution is

\[u^0=u_0^0\cosh\alpha\tau+u_0^z\sinh\alpha\tau,\]
\[u^z=u_0^z\cosh\alpha\tau+u_0^0\sinh\alpha\tau,\]
\[u^x+i u^y=(u_0^x+i u_0^y)e^{-i\Omega\tau}.\]

The longitudinal and transverse operations commute because they act in different subspaces. In lab time the transverse rotation slows as the electric field increases energy, although the proper-time rotation frequency stays constant. A particle therefore traces a helix whose transverse radius \(p_\perp/(|q|B)\) is fixed while the pitch evolves. A single measured turn time without a tagged position in the accelerating region is insufficient for an energy reconstruction.

As a numerical test use an electron, quoting magnitudes, with \(B=1.0\) mT, \(E=10.0\) kV/m and initial purely transverse speed \(0.10c\). One magnetic turn takes 35.72 ns in proper time and accumulates electric rapidity magnitude \(|\alpha|\tau\simeq0.2096\). The final Lorentz factor is \(\gamma_i\cosh(0.2096)=1.0272\), giving final kinetic energy about 13.9 keV, compared with 2.57 keV initially. The lab-time turn duration is \(\gamma_i\sinh(|\alpha|\tau)/|\alpha|\simeq36.2\) ns. Reversing the electric field changes the direction of the axial displacement but leaves final energy and turn duration unchanged for this initially transverse particle. Reversing the initial longitudinal velocity would generally break that energy symmetry.

Infer uncertainty and test units in a momentum reconstruction

A track fitter commonly reports field, radius, pitch, time and a fitted covariance matrix. The dynamical relation turns these observed quantities into momentum. Let \(L_z\) denote a signed pitch per turn. With known signed direction of circulation and calibrated absolute field magnitude,

\[|\mathbf p|=|q|B\sqrt{R^2+(L_z/2\pi)^2}.\]

For small errors, logarithmic differentiation gives weights for fractional radius and pitch errors:

\[d\log p=d\log B+w_R\,d\log R+w_L\,d\log |L_z|,\]
\[w_R=\frac{R^2}{R^2+(L_z/2\pi)^2},\]
\[w_L=1-w_R .\]

For \(R=0.20\) m and \(L_z=0.30\) m the radius accounts for 94.6 percent of the squared transverse-plus-longitudinal geometric factor. Independent relative uncertainties of 0.20 percent in field, 0.50 percent in radius and 2.0 percent in pitch yield about 0.525 percent uncertainty in total momentum. A common detector scale controlling both radius and pitch would enter with correlated signs and should not be counted as two independent errors. When the pitch tends to zero, use the original derivatives with respect to \(L_z\); a reported fractional error on a quantity consistent with zero is ill defined.

Differentiating \(K=\sqrt{m^2c^4+p^2c^2}-mc^2\) gives

\[d\log K=(\gamma+1)d\log p/\gamma.\]

The coefficient approaches two at low energy and one at very high energy. At the approximately 3.70 keV energy of the tutorial the above momentum error would imply about a 1.05 percent energy error. A kinetic-energy estimate from a small difference of nearly equal numbers can also lose numerical precision at much lower energies. Rationalise it as \(K=p^2c^2/(\sqrt{m^2c^4+p^2c^2}+mc^2)\) when working near rest.

Several independent checks use this reconstruction without assuming the track fitter is correct. The turn time inferred from the fitted \(\gamma\) can be compared with a separate timing channel. Reverse the magnetic field to reverse the direction of curvature while keeping the speed and energy approximately fixed. Translate the beam within a mapped uniform field and look for changing fitted radius that correlates with position, an indication of geometric or field-map bias. Finally plot the residual of the magnetic force law separately for transverse magnitude and angle. A time integrator may conserve momentum magnitude for symmetry reasons even when its field interpolation biases the phase or shifts the guiding centre systematically.

Advance a charge using relativistic momentum

Increase the impulse while following speed, energy and proper time. Identify the range where a Newtonian momentum update becomes inaccurate.

Open this experiment online

Worked timing problem · infer an accelerating voltage without a radius

A tagged electron bunch completes ten nearly circular turns in a calibrated 5.0 mT magnetic field. Two time stamps indicate an elapsed 73.0 ns. The field is perpendicular to the orbit. Assuming complete turns and negligible electrical work during the timed interval, estimate the injection energy. What timing precision would distinguish a one-percent change in that energy?

Use the relativistic period sensitivity rather than the energy fraction directly

The nonrelativistic turn period is

\[2\pi m/(eB)=7.145\,\mathrm{ns}.\]

The measured period is 7.30 ns, giving \(\gamma\simeq1.0217\) and \(K=(\gamma-1)mc^2\simeq11.1\) keV. The relative energy sensitivity is much larger than the relative period change when \(\gamma\) is close to one. A one-percent change in \(K\) corresponds to \(\Delta\gamma\simeq2.17\times10^{-4}\), or relative period change \(2.12\times10^{-4}\). Over the ten turns this gives approximately 15.5 ps.

To resolve that change spectroscopically, uncertainties in the elapsed time and field scale must together be significantly smaller than the equivalent change. A constant pickup cable delay cancels if the same electronics register both ends of the interval, while a drift of its timebase scale does not. Timing over more turns improves the total accumulated signal but also increases exposure to field inhomogeneity, energy loss and dephasing among particles with different injection energies. A narrower timing histogram does not automatically eliminate a systematic field calibration.

Finally verify the integer turn count independently. Missing a pickup crossing would cause a much larger change in inferred energy than the desired resolution. The measured wave packet may broaden so strongly that later crossings overlap. Recording the full pickup waveform and tracking ordered crossings, along with an independent rough radius estimate, can constrain the turn assignment before fitting the small relativistic shift. A perfect circular image with no time labels cannot supply that missing count.

Why momentum and kinetic energy do not gain fixed fractions together

In an electrostatic accelerating gap, the kinetic energy increment for a particle passing between two equipotential surfaces is its charge times the potential drop, regardless of injection energy. This statement follows by integrating the Lorentz power law over the actual path. A magnetic guiding field can alter the path and transit time while doing no direct work. A time-dependent electric field, a missed equipotential endpoint or appreciable radiation changes the simple inference and must be addressed separately.

For an electron entering with 10 keV and acquiring an additional 10 keV, kinetic energy doubles. Its initial momentum times \(c\) is about 101.6 keV and its final value about 144.4 keV. The radius in the same subsequent perpendicular magnetic field increases by a factor approximately 1.42. Doubling the radius would instead require doubling momentum, which demands a substantially greater energy increment at this low initial energy. Comparing radii before and after a known voltage supplies an independent check on the sign and magnitude of a particle's charge-to-mass model.

A measured energy gain below the nominal voltage does not by itself diagnose radiation. The potential actually traversed, finite angular acceptance, entrance or exit fringe fields, collision losses and biases in reconstructing longitudinal momentum can all matter. Repeat with reversed field polarity or several injection energies and compare the kinetic-energy difference rather than the two radii directly.

Tutorial · Reconstruct a track with two measurements

A particle known to be an electron or positron crosses a uniform \(+z\) field of 1.0 mT. The projected radius is 0.200 m and the pitch magnitude is 0.300 m. Recover the transverse and longitudinal momentum, energy and period. What else must the detector record to determine the sign of charge?

Reconstruct both momentum components

The transverse momentum is \(|q|BR=3.20\times10^{-23}\) kg m/s. During a cycle the parallel displacement is \(p_zT/(\gamma m)=2\pi p_z/(|q|B)\), so \(|p_z|=7.65\times10^{-24}\) kg m/s. The quadrature total is \(3.29\times10^{-23}\) kg m/s, yielding \(p/(mc)=0.1206\), \(\gamma=1.00724\), and approximately 3.70 keV kinetic energy. The period is about 36.0 ns.

Neither radius nor pitch magnitude carries the signed circulation. Record the direction of traversal relative to the calibrated direction of magnetic field. Reversing both charge and the direction of traversal yields the same unlabelled transverse ring. If timing or two ordered detectors supply a direction, the sign follows from the first Lorentz kick.

Tutorial · Can a constant-energy circle be trusted?

A pusher uses 20 uniform steps per exact cyclotron period in a uniform magnetic field. It reproduces initial kinetic energy to machine precision for 50 turns. Estimate the phase drift from the midpoint rotation formula. Design a refinement diagnostic that distinguishes a time-discretisation bias from a field calibration error.

Compare the exact and discrete angles

The exact unsigned angle per step is

\[h=2\pi/20=0.31416.\]

The discrete one is

\[2\arctan(h/2)=0.31161.\]

The accumulated deficit over 1,000 steps is about 2.55 rad, roughly 0.41 turn. The method can keep every track point on a closed momentum circle yet miss the pickup electrode by a large fraction of a revolution. Coordinate radius may depend on how positions were staggered, so perform the angular comparison on momentum first.

Double the number of steps at fixed physical end time and take the difference in unwrapped phases. The leading angular error \(h^3/12\) per step becomes a quadratic global error, so halving the step should reduce it by about four. A calibration error in \(qB/(\gamma m)\) persists under time refinement. Track both the expected frequency and the convergence of the time series; comparing the coordinates at a single late time can confuse complete turns with near agreement.

Cambridge Electromagnetism · four-vectors, fields and charged matter

Semiclassical quantization

Quantum mechanics

Use a local wavelength to construct the quantization rule for a smooth well and recognize where that construction breaks down.

From first principles

Build the semiclassical phase and the turning-point condition

In a classically allowed interval define \(p(x)=\sqrt{2m[E-V(x)]}>0\) and seek a one-dimensional stationary wave \(\psi=A(x)e^{iS(x)/\hbar}\). Assume \(p\) varies little over a local wavelength. Substitute into the Schrödinger equation and separate terms by powers of \(\hbar\). The leading real and next imaginary balances give

\[(S')^2=p^2,\]
\[2A'S'+AS''=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad A^2 S'=\text{constant}.\]

Thus the phase increment is \(\int p\,dx/\hbar\) and the travelling-wave amplitude varies as \(p^{-1/2}\). This approximation fails where \(p=0\). Near each smooth simple turning point the potential is locally linear; the decaying exterior Airy solution matches an interior sine with phase shift \(\pi/4\). The waves entering from the left and right must describe the same standing wave. Combining the two end phases gives

\[\int_{x_-}^{x_+}p(x)\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+\tfrac12),\]
\[n=0,1,\ldots .\]

The offset comes from the two soft turning points. A hard wall imposes its own node condition and changes the quantization offset. The construction becomes more reliable when the state samples many local wavelengths inside the well.

Derivation and applications

Read a quantum level spacing from a classical clock

The semiclassical quantization rule uses the area swept in phase space. Its change with energy also carries information about time. Consider a smooth one-dimensional binding potential with two simple turning points \(x_\pm(E)\), mass \(m\), and many accessible levels. Write the half-cycle action using the positive momentum branch \(p(x,E)=\sqrt{2m[E-V(x)]}\):

\[I(E)=\int_{x_-}^{x_+}p(x,E)\,dx,\]
\[I(E_n)\simeq\pi\hbar(n+\tfrac12).\]

The endpoints move with energy, but their boundary contributions when differentiating the integral contain \(p(x_\pm)=0\). Inside the well \(\partial_Ep=m/p\). Its divergence near a simple turning point behaves as an inverse square root and is integrable. Using \(p/m=\dot x\) on the forward leg now gives

\[\frac{dI}{dE}=\int_{x_-}^{x_+}\frac{m}{p(x,E)}\,dx=\int_{x_-}^{x_+}\frac{dx}{\dot x}=\frac{T_{\rm cl}(E)}{2}.\]

Two neighbouring quantized actions differ by \(\pi\hbar\). If the classical period varies little over a level spacing, linearizing the action about one level leads to

\[E_{n+1}-E_n\simeq\frac{2\pi\hbar}{T_{\rm cl}(E_n)}=\frac{h}{T_{\rm cl}(E_n)} .\]

Thus a densely spaced part of the spectrum is associated with a long classical excursion. For a harmonic well the period is constant and the result is exactly the known spacing; near a separatrix the rapidly varying period makes a one-step linearization less reliable. Only the period of a complete return to the starting phase-space point belongs in this relation.

Worked example

Estimate a frequency from adjacent lines

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

Consider an approximately harmonic vibrational well with nearby levels separated by \(2.50\) meV. With \(h=4.136\) meV ps, the inferred classical period is \(1.65\) ps and the ordinary oscillation frequency is \(0.604\) THz. A transit from the left turning point to the right should take about \(0.827\) ps. Equating this one-way transit with the period would overestimate the energy spacing by two.

Second application

Predict the scaling change for a quartic well

Change the inputs and investigate this derivation.

In \(V(x)=\lambda x^4\) the turning point is \(x_t=(E/\lambda)^{1/4}\). Substituting \(x=x_tu\) into \(T_{\rm cl}=2\int_{-x_t}^{x_t}m\,dx/p\) leaves an energy-independent integral and gives

\[T_{\rm cl}\propto E^{-1/4}.\]

Therefore local level spacing grows as \(E^{1/4}\). At sixteen times the energy a spacing of \(0.180\) meV should become about \(0.360\) meV, assuming both regions contain enough levels for semiclassical counting. The quadratic trap would show no comparable growth.

Try it · Power law spacing

Vary the physical parameters in the derived relation. Use the units and assumptions in the readout to connect the curves to the two worked examples. A parameter study can test the forward prediction; the same relation can then be inverted to infer a parameter from a measurement.

Open this live calculation

A locally oscillating solution

Consider one-dimensional bound motion of mass \(m\) in a smoothly varying potential. In allowed regions the stationary equation is \(\psi''+p(x)^2\psi/\hbar^2=0\) with \(p=\sqrt{2m(E-V)}\). If the potential changes little over a local wavelength, a small interval resembles a free-particle region. Accumulating its local phase produces the action integral \(\int p\,dx/\hbar\). Yet a wave with changing local momentum must also change amplitude in order to conserve current.

Substitute \(\psi=A(x)\exp[iS(x)/\hbar]\). The leading real terms give

\[S'^2=p^2.\]

The next imaginary terms give \(2A'S'+AS''=0\), or \((A^2S')'=0\). Therefore each travelling contribution has amplitude proportional to \(1/\sqrt p\). Equivalently a classical particle dwells longer where it is slow, and the smoothed semiclassical probability mirrors this dwell time. The neglected derivative terms are small away from turning points when \(\hbar|p'|\ll p^2\).

Match through a turning point

At an ordinary turning point \(V(x_t)=E\) with nonzero slope, the local wavelength diverges and the previous criterion fails. Linearizing \(V\) there gives the Airy equation. Its decaying forbidden-side solution continues to an oscillatory allowed-side standing wave with a phase offset of \(\pi/4\). Matching decaying exterior tails on both sides of a well bounded by two such turning points imposes

\[\int_{x_-}^{x_+}p(x)\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+\tfrac12),\]
\[n=0,1,2,\ldots .\]

The offset encodes the two smooth reflections. An infinite hard wall instead imposes a node directly and has a different reflection phase. Applying this rule unchanged to a hard box would count its levels incorrectly. For slowly varying barriers the forbidden action also controls exponential attenuation, but the two-turning-point integral above determines the energies of a classically allowed well.

A trap with measurable spacings

For \(V=m\omega^2x^2/2\) let \(x_t=\sqrt{2E/(m\omega^2)}\). Put \(x=x_t\sin u\) in the action integral. The integrand becomes \((2E/\omega)\cos^2u\,du\) between \(-\pi/2\) and \(\pi/2\), yielding \(\pi E/\omega\). The rule gives \(E_n=\hbar\omega(n+1/2)\), exceptionally exact even for the oscillator’s lowest levels.

For a mechanical trap at frequency 100 kHz, \(E_4=4.5hf=2.98\times10^{-28}\) J, equivalent to \(21.6\) μK on division by \(k_B\). The spacing is \(hf\) or \(4.80\) μK. These temperatures express energy units; no equilibrium ensemble was assumed. As a transfer test consider \(V=gx^4\). Rescaling \(x=(E/g)^{1/4}y\) makes the action proportional to \(E^{3/4}\). Quantization gives \(E_n\propto(n+1/2)^{4/3}\), so successive spacings grow with excitation. At sufficiently high level number the action changes slowly over a wavelength, making the predicted trend more trustworthy than a low-level numerical prefactor.

An independent check on the energy spacing follows by differentiating the action with respect to energy. The integrand vanishes at the turning points, so their moving endpoints add no first-order term. Inside the integral \(\partial p/\partial E=m/p=1/v\). Consequently the derivative of the one-way action is the time for the classical particle to travel from one turning point to the other. Adjacent quantum levels at high excitation then have separation approximately \(h\) divided by the full classical period. Measuring a variation of level spacing with excitation is therefore also probing the dependence of the classical orbit period on energy.

Estimate tunnelling between two bound wells

A nearly symmetric double well can have pairs of quantum states concentrated mainly in classically separate regions. For one such pair take approximately orthogonal localized states \(|L\rangle,|R\rangle\) with equal isolated energy \(E_0\) and a small real tunnelling matrix element \(-J\). The sign of either localized basis state can be chosen so \(J>0\). The two-level Hamiltonian has symmetric and antisymmetric eigenstates with energies \(E_0-J\) and \(E_0+J\), respectively. A local preparation \(|L\rangle\) is a superposition of both and acquires a relative phase at rate \(2J/\hbar\).

On evolution the probability to be in the other well is \(P_R(t)=\sin^2(Jt/\hbar)\). The first complete transfer occurs at \(t_{\rm tr}=\pi\hbar/(2J)=h/(2\Delta E)\) with \(\Delta E=2J\). In a high smooth barrier the localized wave tails overlap weakly. A semiclassical estimate gives

\[J\simeq J_0 e^{-\mathcal S/\hbar},\]

with \(\mathcal S=\int_{x_1}^{x_2}\sqrt{2m[V(x)-E_0]}\,dx\) between inner turning points. This is an amplitude action. The through-barrier transmission probability of a scattering experiment instead contains twice that exponent. Confusing the two quantities gives an incorrect predicted change in the oscillation time.

Predict how a gate changes a tunnelling period

Suppose a symmetric electron double well shows a spectroscopic splitting \(\Delta E=40.0\) μeV. Its predicted first full transfer time is

\[h/(2\Delta E)=51.7\,\mathrm{ps}.\]

Use the measured splitting to eliminate the unknown semiclassical prefactor. A gate modification adds a nearly flat forbidden segment of width \(0.300\) nm where \(V-E_0=0.150\) eV and otherwise changes the wells negligibly. Using \(\hbar^2/(2m_e)=0.0381\) eV nm² gives decay wavenumber \(\kappa=\sqrt{0.150/0.0381}=1.98\) nm⁻¹. The added dimensionless action is 0.595.

If the prefactor and individual well frequencies remain similar, the new splitting is \(40.0e^{-0.595}=22.1\) μeV, and transfer time rises to \(93.6\) ps. Doubling the width change multiplies the splitting by the square of the original ratio, not by twice the original ratio. These estimates concern coherent dynamics before energy relaxation or phase noise destroys the relative phase.

A gate can also shift the two well bottoms differently. With detuning \(\delta\) the splitting is \(\sqrt{\delta^2+4J^2}\), while maximum transfer falls to \(4J^2/(\delta^2+4J^2)\). A larger measured splitting can therefore accompany poorer population transfer if symmetry is lost. Comparing spectroscopy and time-resolved populations distinguishes a changed tunnel coupling from an unintended potential offset.

The small tunnel coupling also sets a time criterion for disorder: a fluctuating left–right offset comparable with the splitting can produce substantial changes in both transfer amplitude and oscillation frequency. A static distribution of offsets across repeated preparations can wash out ensemble oscillations even when each individual preparation evolves coherently. Repeating the experiment after compensating the measured detuning separates this ensemble averaging from intrinsic energy relaxation. The semiclassical action comparison is most reliable when the two localized levels remain nearly resonant throughout the modification and no higher intrawell level enters the same small energy window.

Independent measurements of the individual well minima supply a practical detuning calibration.

A phase accumulated by slowly turning a magnetic field

Quantum mechanics

A spin aligned with a slowly rotating field returns to its original measurement probabilities after one loop. Derive the additional phase that becomes observable when that path interferes with a reference path.

Separate following the state from acquiring a phase

Let a spin one-half have \(H=-(\hbar\omega_0/2)\,\mathbf n(t)\cdot\boldsymbol\sigma\), with \(\mathbf n\) a unit vector and constant \(\omega_0>0\). The two instantaneous energies are separated by \(\hbar\omega_0\). Prepare the lower-energy state and rotate \(\mathbf n\) on a smooth path. If the rate of change of its eigenvector is small compared with the gap in angular-frequency units, transitions to the upper state are small. Following one eigenstate still allows a time-dependent complex phase.

Write \(|\psi(t)\rangle=e^{i\alpha(t)}|n(t)\rangle\), substitute in the time-dependent Schrödinger equation and project onto \(\langle n|\). Normalization makes \(\langle n|\dot n\rangle\) imaginary. Consequently

\[\dot\alpha=-E_n/\hbar+i\langle n|\dot n\rangle .\]

The first contribution accumulates in proportion to elapsed time. The integral of the second depends on the path of the instantaneous eigenvector. The splitting into pieces changes with the arbitrary phase used to define \(|n(t)\rangle\); after a closed loop the geometric phase in a single-valued gauge is invariant modulo \(2\pi\).

Evaluate a circle on the sphere of directions

Represent the aligned spin by the column \((\cos(\theta/2),e^{i\phi}\sin(\theta/2))^T\). Its mean spin points along the polar angles \(\theta,\phi\). Hold \(\theta\) fixed and increase \(\phi\) by one revolution. Only the lower component contributes to the derivative. Its coefficient is its occupation weight:

\[i\langle n|\partial_\phi n\rangle=-\sin^2(\theta/2),\]
\[\gamma=-2\pi\sin^2(\theta/2)=-\pi(1-\cos\theta).\]

The solid angle enclosed by the latitude is \(2\pi(1-\cos\theta)\), found by integrating \(\sin\theta'\,d\theta'\,d\phi\) over the cap. The aligned spin accumulates minus half this angle. Reversing traversal changes its sign. As the cone closes to a point, the result vanishes continuously. Around the equator the result is \(-\pi\).

Predict an interference fringe

Split a coherent preparation into two equal-amplitude paths and arrange the reference so the dynamical phases match. If only the signal path gains \(\gamma\), an ideal balanced recombiner gives the bright-port probability \(P=(1+\cos\gamma)/2\) for zero additional reference phase. The overall phase of one isolated spin would never produce this signal. The reference path supplies a phase comparison.

For \(\theta=60^\circ\), the solid angle is \(\pi\), the geometric phase is \(-\pi/2\), and \(P=1/2\). For a circuit of the equator the same port becomes dark. Reversing the \(60^\circ\) loop still gives one-half at zero reference phase. Introduce a known reference shift \(\chi\): the probability becomes \([1+\cos(\chi+\gamma)]/2\) in the chosen convention. Choosing \(\chi=\pi/2\) changes the two directions of traversal into full and zero probability, revealing the sign.

Scan the reference phase

Set the cone angle, then vary the reference phase. The horizontal axis scans the cone angle and the marker value is returned in the readout. A source with finite path coherence would reduce the modulation depth, whereas unmatched dynamical phases would shift the fringe.

Open this experiment online

Tutorial on the following condition

A cone has angle \(60^\circ\). At what rate scale does uniform azimuthal rotation stop being reliably adiabatic, and why can a change in loop duration alter a real experiment?

Work through the solution

The off-diagonal eigenstate derivative has magnitude \(|\dot\phi|\sin\theta/2\), so one requires \(|\dot\phi|\sin\theta/(2\omega_0)\ll1\). Increasing duration reduces that coupling but also changes the dynamical phase and can permit decoherence. An interferometer must control both even when the ideal geometric phase has no dependence on duration.

Keep the cone angle at \(60^\circ\) but make two complete positive circuits before recombination. What probability appears at the bright port with zero reference phase? Would performing one forward and one reverse circuit give the same result?

Work through the solution

Each positive traversal contributes \(-\pi/2\). Two give \(-\pi\), hence zero bright-port probability. A positive and negative traversal instead add to zero, giving unit probability when dynamical phases are matched. These outcomes test orientation and addition of the geometric phase over consecutive cycles.

Estimate the sensitivity to a cone-angle calibration error at \(60^\circ\). Express the first-order phase error for one angular degree.

Work through the solution

Differentiating in radians gives

\[d\gamma/d\theta=-\pi\sin\theta.\]

Multiplication by \(\pi/180\) yields \(-0.0475\) radians of phase error per added degree at \(60^\circ\). At a port fringe extremum the intensity change from this small phase error begins quadratically. Near a half-bright fringe it appears linearly and is easier to detect.

Further reading · Tong, Topics in Quantum Mechanics, adiabatic theorem and Berry phase.

A magnetic orbit that spirals outward in a perfect vacuum

Numerical field laboratory

Track a charged particle in a prescribed uniform field, derive a rotation update and separate numerical energy growth from a real magnetic force.

Use the work law before solving the trajectory

A nonrelativistic charged particle of mass \(m\) and charge \(q\) moves perpendicular to a uniform, time-independent magnetic field \(B\hat{\mathbf z}\). There is no electric field and the dilute test particles do not affect the applied field. The Lorentz force gives \(\dot v_x=\omega v_y,\ \dot v_y=-\omega v_x\) for \(\omega=qB/m\). Dotting with \(\mathbf v\) gives zero, so the speed should stay constant. This property holds for either sign of the charge; the direction of rotation changes. It is a better initial test of a trajectory solver than how circular a short segment looks on screen.

Define \(J(v_x,v_y)=(v_y,-v_x)\), so \(J^T=-J\) and \(J^2=-I\). The exact velocity over a time step \(h\) is \([\cos(\omega h)I+\sin(\omega h)J]\mathbf v\). Integration in time gives a circular orbit of radius \(v_\perp/|\omega|\) once the guiding centre is chosen. A magnetic field alone bends a trajectory without changing its kinetic energy; a slowly growing orbit radius at fixed \(B\) and fixed initial speed demands an explanation.

Why forward Euler adds energy at every step

Euler gives \(\mathbf v_+=(I+dJ)\mathbf v\) with \(d=\omega h\). Squaring the norm eliminates the cross term because \(v^TJv=0\), leaving \(\|\mathbf v_+\|^2=(1+d^2)\|\mathbf v\|^2\). No nonzero step has exact unit gain. A geometric picture makes the bias tangible: a tangent step leaves the initial velocity circle on its outside, whatever the point of departure.

After \(N\) steps the speed factor is \((1+d^2)^{N/2}\). With 24 steps per physical orbit, \(d=2\pi/24\simeq0.2618\), so a full orbit multiplies speed by roughly 2.2. At shorter times or smaller steps this can resemble weak physical acceleration. Reducing \(h\) at fixed physical duration reduces the growth. Testing one step at fixed count, rather than comparing over a fixed physical interval, underestimates the cumulative error.

Solve a centred magnetic step exactly

Use the time-centred velocity in the Lorentz force:

\[\mathbf v_+-\mathbf v=d J(\mathbf v_++\mathbf v)/2.\]

Premultiply by the transpose of \(\mathbf v_++\mathbf v\). The left side is the difference of squared speeds and the right side vanishes, so the update preserves speed for every \(d\) in exact arithmetic. Rearranging yields a two-dimensional linear solve; using \(J^2=-I\) turns it into a rotation,

\[\mathbf v_+=\frac{1-t^2}{1+t^2}\mathbf v+\frac{2t}{1+t^2}J\mathbf v,\]
\[t=d/2.\]

These coefficients are \(\cos\alpha\) and \(\sin\alpha\) for \(\alpha=2\arctan(d/2)\). This is the magnetic rotation used in the nonrelativistic Boris integrator. For simultaneous electric fields, the usual split step adds two half electric impulses around the magnetic rotation. The current experiment isolates the magnetic part; it makes no claim about a nonuniform electric field or the accuracy of interpolation from a field mesh to particles.

Open the live experiment

Speed conservation does not fix arrival time

At small \(d\),

\[\alpha=d-d^3/12+O(d^5).\]

A speed-preserving orbit lags in gyrophase, even when its energy plot stays perfectly flat. After four true orbits at 24 steps per orbit the predicted lag is about 8.14 degrees. The browser advances the Euler and rotation velocities and reports both norm and lag. The y-axis rescales to fit both curves, so a sharply growing Euler error can flatten the appearance of the well-behaved curve. Inspect numerical values as well as the picture.

Suppose an optical pickup observes the time at which a dilute ion tracer first crosses a known line of sight after several orbits. A phase error can be an important measurement error even when the speed is exact. To use the trajectory as a reference, refine the step until the crossing time changes by less than the experimental timing resolution. If the required integration spans many revolutions, a uniform field permits exact substeps. In realistic fields one can retain a structure-preserving rotation while resolving spatial variation independently.

A separate diagnostic is time reversal. Starting from the final velocity and applying the rotation with \(-d\) exactly inverts a centred step, up to rounding. Forward Euler with \(-d\) after \(+d\) gives

\[(I-dJ)(I+dJ)=(1+d^2)I.\]

A forward/backward test detects the lost reversibility without knowing a single experimental constant.

Tutorial

Given an orbit with 48 steps per period, estimate the gyrophase error after ten periods using the leading asymptotic term.

Work through the solution

There are 480 steps with \(d=2\pi/48\). The accumulated lag is about \(480d^3/12\simeq0.0897\) rad, or 5.14 degrees. Doubling the number of steps per period reduces the leading accumulated error fourfold.

A computation preserves speed but reverses the sign of its orbit relative to observation. Would the magnetic work test detect the error?

Work through the solution

Both field signs give zero work. Check a signed initial acceleration component \(a_y=-qBv_x/m\) for a positive initial \(v_x\), or observe the oriented trajectory. A scalar invariant cannot detect all vector-sign errors.

What changes in the relativistic case with appreciable kinetic energy?

Work through the solution

Evolve momentum \(\mathbf p=\gamma m\mathbf v\) and infer velocity from momentum. In a uniform magnetic field without electric work \(\gamma\) stays constant, and the orbital frequency is reduced to \(|q|B/(\gamma m)\). An energy-preserving nonrelativistic integration at the wrong frequency would still give the wrong measured period.

How a turning point supplies the missing semiclassical phase

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Far from a turning point the wavelength follows the classical momentum. At the turning point that approximation becomes singular. Derive the Airy connection, quantise a smooth well, distinguish a hard wall and estimate a tunnelling exponent with a defensible error check.

Expand the action before interpreting a local wavelength

For a stationary one-dimensional state with smooth potential \(V(x)\), write \(\psi=e^{iS(x)/\hbar}\). Substitution into Schrödinger's equation gives

\[S'^2-i\hbar S''=2m(E-V)=p^2(x).\]

Expand \(S=S_0+(\hbar/i)S_1+\cdots\). The leading terms give \(S_0'=\pm p\) and \(S_1'=-p'/(2p)\) in an allowed region where \(p>0\). Thus travelling waves have form \(C p^{-1/2}\exp[\pm i\int^x p\,dx/\hbar]\). The amplitude factor is physically essential: for a single such branch the current to leading order is \(\pm|C|^2/m\), independent of \(x\), as required for steady flux in a real potential. A constant amplitude with variable wavenumber would predict current piling up in a stationary region.

A local condition for this expansion is \(\hbar|p'|/p^2\ll1\) together with control over higher derivatives on the wavelength scale. Away from a barrier the phase can remain accurate even if absolute amplitude normalisation must be fixed globally. Where \(E<V\), define \(\kappa=\sqrt{2m(V-E)}\) with units of momentum. The local solutions become \(\kappa^{-1/2}\exp[\pm\int\kappa\,dx/\hbar]\). A single real decaying branch carries zero probability current by itself. Stationary tunnelling flux through a finite barrier involves a complex combination satisfying both exterior matching conditions, even though the leading size of transmission can often be inferred from the decaying exponential.

Resolve the apparent divergence near a simple turning point

Let \(x_t\) satisfy

\[V(x_t)=E,\]

with slope \(F=V'(x_t)>0\). Linearise \(V-E\simeq F(x-x_t)\). The local Schrödinger equation becomes

\[\psi''=(2mF/\hbar^2)(x-x_t)\psi.\]

Introduce length \(\ell=(\hbar^2/(2mF))^{1/3}\) and \(\xi=(x-x_t)/\ell\), leaving \(d^2\psi/d\xi^2=\xi\psi\). Its two independent solutions are \(\operatorname{Ai}\) and \(\operatorname{Bi}\). On the positive side \(\operatorname{Ai}(\xi)\) decays, whereas \(\operatorname{Bi}(\xi)\) grows. A bound state extending into a semi-infinite forbidden region selects the former. At \(\xi=0\) the Airy function is finite; the apparent divergence \(p^{-1/2}\) came from extending the outer approximation beyond its range.

The asymptotic forms for \(s>0\) large are

\[\operatorname{Ai}(s)\sim\frac{e^{-2s^{3/2}/3}}{2\sqrt\pi\,s^{1/4}},\qquad \operatorname{Ai}(-s)\sim\frac{\sin(2s^{3/2}/3+\pi/4)}{\sqrt\pi\,s^{1/4}} .\]

For a linear slope, \(2s^{3/2}/3\) equals \(|\int_{x_t}^x p\,dx|/\hbar\) on the allowed side or \(\int_{x_t}^x\kappa\,dx/\hbar\) on the forbidden side. Matching in an interval where \(|x-x_t|\gg\ell\) but the potential remains effectively linear gives the connection from a decaying tail of coefficient \(C\) to \(2C\,p^{-1/2}\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_x^{x_t}p\,dx+\pi/4]\). The coefficient two and phase \(\pi/4\) both come from the same local solution. A turning point with zero slope, a sharp step or two turning points closer than their Airy lengths requires a different local treatment.

Join two decaying tails to a standing wave

For a smooth well with two well-separated simple turning points \(x_1,x_2\), the left tail matches to \(\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_{x_1}^xp\,dx+\pi/4]\). The right tail matches to \(\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_x^{x_2}p\,dx+\pi/4]\). Their zeros and logarithmic derivatives must coincide throughout the overlap, up to an overall sign. The sum of the two phase arguments is the full one-way action divided by \(\hbar\) plus \(\pi/2\), which must be an integer multiple of \(\pi\). Taking \(n\ge0\) gives

\[\int_{x_1}^{x_2}p(x,E_n)\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+1/2).\]

For a harmonic oscillator the turning point magnitude is \(a=\sqrt{2E/(m\omega^2)}\) and \(p=m\omega\sqrt{a^2-x^2}\). The action is the area of a semicircle times \(m\omega\), equal to \(\pi E/\omega\). The rule reproduces \(E_n=\hbar\omega(n+1/2)\) exactly for this special spectrum, even though its outer WKB wavefunction is still inaccurate near each turning point and globally for low quantum number. Equality of a spectral result does not imply equality of spatial densities or agreement of all observables.

Different boundaries change the phase budget. Take a hard wall at \(x=0\) and a linearly rising potential \(Fx\) for \(x>0\). The solution decaying to the right is \(\operatorname{Ai}[(x-E/F)/\ell]\). Its exact energies follow from \(\operatorname{Ai}(-E/F\ell)=0\). The wall imposes a node without an Airy phase at the left. Setting the right-matched standing wave to zero at the wall gives

\[\int_0^{E/F}p\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+3/4).\]

Thus \(E/(F\ell)\simeq[3\pi(n+3/4)/2]^{2/3}\). For \(n=0\) this is about 2.320 versus the first exact zero 2.338; with \(n=4\) the approximation is about 7.94. Replacing the wall by a second smooth turning point would instead introduce an erroneous half offset.

Separate a credible exponential estimate from its prefactor

For a barrier with two simple turning points \(x_a,x_b\), a large forbidden action \(I=\int_{x_a}^{x_b}\kappa\,dx/\hbar\) suppresses the transmitted flux approximately as \(T\simeq e^{-2I}\), up to prefactors and corrections that depend on geometry and matching. For a triangular forbidden region with height \(V_0-E=\Delta\) at \(x=0\) falling as \(\Delta-Fx\) to zero at \(\Delta/F\), its action is \(I=2\sqrt{2m}\Delta^{3/2}/(3\hbar F)\). A discontinuous incident-side boundary may affect the prefactor substantially. It does not justify assigning a second smooth-turning-point phase to the discontinuity. For a rectangular barrier the exact thick-barrier prefactor is \(16E(V_0-E)/V_0^2\) times the decaying exponential at equal exterior potentials. It can exceed one while total transmission remains small.

Suppose two measurements change only the forbidden width of a flat barrier from \(d\) to \(d+\Delta d\), keeping both interfaces and asymptotic energies the same. In the thick limit the ratio \(T(d+\Delta d)/T(d)\simeq e^{-2\kappa\Delta d/\hbar}\) cancels the common prefactor. If \(\kappa/\hbar=2.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) and \(\Delta d=0.50\) nm, this ratio is

\[e^{-2}=0.1353.\]

An offset error in barrier width that leaves this ratio unchanged can still cause a large error in absolute transmission. Near the top two turning points can merge, \(I\) becomes small, and the raw exponential lacks a unitary connection to the over-barrier reflection regime. Use a suitable uniform approximation or a direct scattering solution there.

Predict a high-energy spectrum without a closed-form wavefunction

Consider a pure quartic trap \(V(x)=\lambda x^4\) with \(\lambda>0\). Classical turning points lie at \(x=\pm a,\ a=(E/\lambda)^{1/4}\). Unlike the harmonic oscillator its period changes with energy, so its levels cannot be equally spaced. The semiclassical action can be evaluated once in dimensionless coordinates and then rescaled to any \(m,\lambda,E\):

\[A(E)=\int_{-a}^a\sqrt{2m(E-\lambda x^4)}\,dx=2\sqrt{2m}\,\lambda^{-1/4}E^{3/4} I_4,\]
\[I_4=\int_0^1\sqrt{1-y^4}\,dy .\]

With \(s=y^4\),

\[I_4=\frac14B(1/4,3/2)\simeq0.8740.\]

Quantising \(A(E_n)=\pi\hbar(n+1/2)\) predicts \(E_n\simeq[\pi\hbar(n+1/2)\lambda^{1/4}/(2\sqrt{2m}I_4)]^{4/3}\). The dependence \(E\propto n^{4/3}\) and the dimensional factor \(\hbar^{4/3}\lambda^{1/3}m^{-2/3}\) can be checked independently from scaling the full Schrödinger equation. That dimensional argument does not supply the coefficient or the half-integer offset. The action and boundary phases supply the additional physics.

Choose units such that \(H=-d^2/dx^2+x^4\). Then \(2m=\hbar=1\) in the scaled equation, and the leading energies are \([\pi(n+1/2)/(2I_4)]^{4/3}\). For \(n=0\) this gives about 0.868, below the accurately computed ground value around 1.060. An independent variational Gaussian \(\psi=(\beta/\pi)^{1/4}e^{-\beta x^2/2}\) has energy \(\beta/2+3/(4\beta^2)\), minimised at \(\beta^3=3\) and giving 1.082. Its proximity to the true ground energy has a different status: the Gaussian energy is an upper bound by the variational principle, while leading WKB provides no universal bound for this potential. Using agreement between two approximate numbers as proof of a rigorous interval would require actual lower and upper estimates.

At larger \(n\) the period relation provides an additional prediction. Since \(A\propto E^{3/4}\),

\[T_{\rm cl}=2\,dA/dE\propto E^{-1/4};\]

the frequency increases at larger amplitude, and the local spacing should grow as \(E^{1/4}\). Numerically compute a handful of high levels with a direct finite-difference or spectral Hamiltonian, then plot \(E_n^{3/4}\) against \(n+1/2\). A nearly linear trend is a more sensitive diagnostic of the predicted law than comparing a single apparent power from a log plot at low \(n\). Increase domain size and basis cutoff independently. A truncation that confines high-energy tails can falsely steepen the spacing, while a coarse grid can flatten the kinetic-energy dispersion. Both errors become more visible for rapidly oscillating states.

Relate a barrier action to a coherent population oscillation

Take two identical minima separated by a smooth barrier. At energies below the barrier top but above each isolated-well ground, there are two classically allowed intervals and a central forbidden region. If the barrier is sufficiently opaque, construct approximate localised states \(|L\rangle,|R\rangle\) with equal one-well energy \(E_0\). In their span the simplest symmetric Hamiltonian is \(H=E_0 I-J(|L\rangle\langle R|+|R\rangle\langle L|)\) with \(J>0\) in a suitable phase convention. The even and odd combinations have energies \(E_0-J,E_0+J\). The tunnel splitting is \(2J\), set to leading exponential order by the overlap of amplitudes through the central forbidden interval.

A single under-barrier wavefunction amplitude carries \(e^{-I}\),

\[I=\int\kappa dx/\hbar.\]

For an open barrier the transmission probability is proportional to \(e^{-2I}\). For a symmetric double-well splitting the relevant quantity is a coherent off-diagonal energy amplitude, so its leading exponential is \(J\propto e^{-I}\), times a prefactor involving the oscillation scales and matching in the two wells. Confusing these exponents can lead to a severe error when using a calculated spatial barrier to predict a coherent tunnel frequency. The exact prefactor depends on the potential and turning-point normalisations. A direct symmetric numerical eigenproblem giving the difference of two nearly equal energies requires enough precision to resolve an exponentially small splitting.

Prepare \(|L\rangle\) at time zero. Its evolution under the two-state Hamiltonian gives

\[P_R(t)=\sin^2(Jt/\hbar);\]

the first full transfer time is \(\pi\hbar/(2J)\). If the observed energy splitting expressed as ordinary frequency is \(2J/h=20\) Hz, the first transfer occurs at 25 ms. Changing only the barrier action by \(+0.70\) at fixed prefactor multiplies the splitting by \(e^{-0.70}=0.497\) and approximately doubles the transfer time. An open-barrier transmission experiment over the same change instead sees factor \(e^{-1.4}=0.247\). Both tests depend exponentially on barrier shape but interrogate different quantities.

Now add a calibrated tilt \(\epsilon=E_L-E_R\). The \(2\times2\) Hamiltonian after trace subtraction is \((\epsilon/2)\sigma_z-J\sigma_x\), and the maximum transfer falls to \(4J^2/(\epsilon^2+4J^2)\). At \(\epsilon=2J\) it is one half while the oscillation angular frequency increases from \(2J/\hbar\) to \(2\sqrt2J/\hbar\). A loss of transfer contrast need not be environmental decoherence; static tilt alone yields this combination of increased frequency and reduced amplitude. Measuring both quantities and reversing the tilt tests the two-level model. If a higher intrawell level lies within the tilt or excitation range, a single tunnelling doublet no longer spans the relevant motion and a larger basis is required.

Understand the angular correction at a radial turning point

In three dimensions the reduced radial equation on \(r>0\) has a centrifugal term \(\hbar^2l(l+1)/(2mr^2)\). Applying the flat-coordinate WKB rule directly to this equation can give incorrect phases at its singular endpoint, especially for low \(l\). A way to see the appropriate angular correction without guessing is to set \(r=r_0 e^x\), mapping the half-line to all real \(x\), and write \(u(r)=e^{x/2}w(x)\). The arbitrary reference \(r_0\) keeps the logarithm dimensionless. Since \(d/dr=r^{-1}d/dx\), differentiating twice yields

\[\frac{d^2u}{dr^2}=\frac{e^{x/2}}{r^2}\left(w''-\frac14 w\right).\]

The resulting equation for \(w\) has squared local action momentum in \(x\) proportional to \(2mr^2(E-V)-\hbar^2(l+1/2)^2\). Thus returning its action \(\int p_xdx\) to \(r\) uses \(l(l+1)\to(l+1/2)^2\) in the radial semiclassical momentum. This is the Langer correction. It does not change the exact angular eigenvalue \(L^2=\hbar^2l(l+1)\); it accounts for the coordinate transformation and endpoint behaviour of the semiclassical representation. Confusing those statements would alter the exact radial equation to repair an approximate integration rule.

An instructive check is the isotropic oscillator. For \(V=m\omega^2r^2/2\) and effective classical angular momentum \(L=(l+1/2)\hbar\), the radial action over its two roots is

\[A_r=(\pi/2)(E/\omega-L).\]

One can obtain this without an explicit contour integral by noting that radial motion repeats at twice the Cartesian frequency, so

\[dA_r/dE=\pi/(2\omega).\]

Fix the integration constant at the circular-orbit threshold \(E=\omega L\), where the two radial turning points meet and \(A_r=0\). Quantising \(A_r=\pi\hbar(n_r+1/2)\) gives \(E=\hbar\omega(2n_r+l+3/2)\), matching the exact three-dimensional oscillator spectrum. For \(l=0\) the naive uncorrected centrifugal term has no inner turning point; transferring a generic two-smooth-turning-point formula into that half-line problem without reconsidering its origin is especially unsafe.

Calculate an exponentially small transmission without subtracting nearly equal numbers

In a classically forbidden interval the local solution contains a growing and a decaying branch. In a long interval numerical transfer matrices can contain elements as large as \(e^I\), while the quantity sought is as small as \(e^{-2I}\). Straight propagation of a decaying boundary condition in the unstable direction can contaminate it with a tiny growing roundoff component which eventually dominates. A plot matching the expected slope over the first few decay lengths gives no guarantee that a final tiny flux is accurate. Numerically stable alternatives include propagating a logarithmic derivative with controlled handling of its poles, matching independently normalised left and right solutions, or composing scattering matrices for short slices while retaining decaying propagation factors.

For a square barrier the exact transmission at \(E<V_0\) in equal exterior regions is \(T=[1+V_0^2\sinh^2(\kappa d/\hbar)/(4E(V_0-E))]^{-1}\). With \(E=V_0/2\) this simplifies to \(\operatorname{sech}^2 I\). At \(I=20\) its value is approximately \(4e^{-40}=1.70\times10^{-17}\). Computing \(T=1-R\) in ordinary double precision can give exactly zero since the reflection probability rounds to one. Compute transmitted amplitude or its logarithm directly and compare \(\log T\simeq\log4-2I\). At a moderate \(I\), where both currents are accurately representable, independently verify \(R+T=1\). Moving the benchmarking point across the barrier-opacity range tests both the physical approximation and the floating-point representation of the desired observable.

Resolve a wave through a classical turning point

Vary the physical length scale in a locally linear potential. Compare the numerical Airy solution and the two semiclassical branches near and away from the turn.

Open this experiment online

Connect semiclassical action to counting states in phase space

The action quantisation rule can also be read as a statement about the number of energy levels below a chosen energy. In a one-dimensional smooth confining well the classically accessible region in \((x,p)\) has area \(\Omega(E)=\int dx\int_{-p(x)}^{p(x)}dp=2A(E)\). Roughly one stationary state fits per phase-space area \(2\pi\hbar\), with an endpoint correction from the turning-point phases. The smoothed density of states follows by differentiating \(\bar N(E)\simeq\Omega(E)/(2\pi\hbar)\), giving \(\bar\rho(E)=T_{\rm cl}/(2\pi\hbar)\). This counts levels after smoothing over individual spacing; the exact count is a staircase and its derivative a sum of delta functions. A formula for a smooth density cannot predict each state in a near-degenerate doublet without additional phase and tunnelling information.

For a particle in a two-dimensional rigid rectangular region, apply the same counting idea in four-dimensional phase space. With area \(\mathcal A=L_xL_y\), accessible momentum disk \(p_x^2+p_y^2<2mE\), the scalar smoothed count is \(\bar N(E)\simeq\mathcal A\pi(2mE)/(2\pi\hbar)^2=\mathcal A mE/(2\pi\hbar^2)\). Its derivative is a constant, unlike the \(E^{-1/2}\) free one-dimensional density and the \(\sqrt E\) three-dimensional density. This leading area law can be checked by directly counting positive integer lattice points satisfying \((n_x\pi/L_x)^2+(n_y\pi/L_y)^2<k^2\) in a Dirichlet rectangle. It works after smoothing when many modes fit in the domain and makes no assumption that the rectangular shape itself models all possible billiards.

A more sensitive check accounts for the excluded axes in that first-quadrant lattice count. For a Dirichlet rectangle subtract half of the positive-axis contributions at each boundary of the quadrant. In terms of perimeter \(\mathcal P=2(L_x+L_y)\), the resulting first two terms are \(\bar N(k)\simeq\mathcal A k^2/(4\pi)-\mathcal P k/(4\pi)+O(1)\) after appropriate smoothing, with fluctuations from discrete modes beyond this local expansion. Neumann boundaries reverse the sign of the perimeter term because modes constant along one coordinate are admitted. Area alone determines the bulk growth, whereas boundary condition affects the next correction. The finite corner term and spectral fluctuations require more care than assigning one universal integer offset to every billiard.

The distinction between smoothing and exact prediction also explains why counting every computed eigenvalue only up to an arbitrarily sharp threshold can produce apparent convergence jumps as the mesh improves. If a true eigenvalue lies near the threshold, a small numerical shift changes an integer count by one. Compare a range of cumulative counts, track nearby eigenvalue residuals and study the smoothed density with a bandwidth large enough to include several levels but small compared with the energy scale of interest. Repeating for two geometries of equal area and different perimeter tests actual geometric information beyond an overall energy scale.

Measure the boundary correction in a square cavity

A scalar mode calculation uses a square of side \(10.0\) length units and reports all Dirichlet modes with \(k<5.00\) inverse length units. Find the leading area estimate, add the perimeter correction, and compare with a direct lattice-point count.

Derive and check the result

The area contribution is \(100(25)/(4\pi)=198.94\); the perimeter subtraction is \(40(5)/(4\pi)=15.92\), giving a smoothed estimate \(183.03\) before corner and oscillatory terms. The exact count uses positive integers \(n_x,n_y\) satisfying \(n_x^2+n_y^2<(50/\pi)^2\simeq253.30\). For each \(n_x=1,\ldots,15\), count \(n_y\le\lfloor\sqrt{253.30-n_x^2}\rfloor\) and sum. The values are \(15,15,15,15,15,14,14,13,13,12,11,10,9,7,5\), giving 183. Agreement at this threshold is close, while the leading area term alone is about sixteen high. Nearby thresholds can have different residual fluctuations, so the close integer agreement at this one wave number should not be treated as an exact two-term counting rule. The same calculation on a different aspect ratio at fixed area would change both the perimeter and the lattice pattern.

Measure a reflection phase separately from the travel action

In a one-dimensional cavity a quantisation condition can be written as a round-trip phase budget. Let an allowed region accumulate one-way phase \(\theta(E)\) and let reflections at its left and right bounding regions have phase shifts \(\phi_L(E),\phi_R(E)\) for unit-modulus reflection. A self-reproducing wave requires

\[2\theta(E)+\phi_L(E)+\phi_R(E)=2\pi n,\]

with an integer depending on the chosen phase origin. A rigid Dirichlet wall reflects with amplitude \(-1\); a smooth decaying turning point contributes a different reflection phase that the Airy matching fixed. Adding \(2\pi\) to one reflection phase simply relabels the integer, but an energy-dependent boundary phase alters the spacing and cannot be removed by a constant relabelling.

A useful boundary that can be solved without approximation is a finite step \(V=0\) for \(x<0,\ V=V_0>E\) for \(x>0\). With incident wave \(e^{ikx}\), reflected amplitude \(r e^{-ikx}\) and exterior evanescent \(t e^{-\kappa x}\), where \(k,\kappa\) here are inverse lengths, continuity of the value and derivative gives

\[1+r=t,\ ik(1-r)=-\kappa t.\]

Solving yields

\[r=(k-i\kappa)/(k+i\kappa).\]

Its magnitude is one and its phase can be written \(-2\arctan(\kappa/k)\). As \(V_0\) tends to infinity with \(E\) fixed, the phase tends to \(-\pi\), giving the rigid-wall limit. Just below the step threshold it approaches zero. Two mirrors with equally complete reflection can therefore produce different cavity eigenfrequencies.

Near the rigid-wall limit expand \(r\simeq-e^{2ik/\kappa}\). To leading order the exterior behaves like a rigid reflecting plane displaced a penetration length \(1/\kappa\) beyond the interface. For a deep symmetric finite well of width \(L\), the phase condition approximately replaces \(L\) by \(L+2/\kappa(E)\). This shift lowers levels relative to a rigid well of the same geometric width. It is only an approximation because penetration depends on energy. Matching logarithmic derivatives independently on the two sides is preferable near threshold or when the barrier shape varies over the penetration length.

Infer a boundary penetration from a measured free spectral interval

A long one-dimensional cavity with identical deep finite barriers has a measured low-mode wavenumber interval 2% smaller than \(\pi/L\). Under the constant penetration approximation infer \(1/\kappa\) relative to \(L\) and identify a check on the approximation.

Derive and check the result

Set \(\pi/(L+2/\kappa)=0.98\pi/L\). Then \(2/\kappa L=1/0.98-1=0.0204\), or a penetration distance \(1/\kappa=0.0102L\) at each wall. For \(L=100\) nm this is about 1.02 nm. Measure several successive intervals to see whether one penetration length describes them. An increasing penetration with energy changes the reflection-phase derivative and therefore the spacing; the physical group delay then contains travel time in the allowed region plus boundary delay. A geometrically longer rigid cavity would produce a similar single interval but a different energy dependence, so an isolated observation does not uniquely identify the cause.

Tutorial · determine what a semiclassical estimate can certify

Recover the period and level spacing from an action curve

For \(A(E)=\int_{x_1}^{x_2}p\,dx\) in a smooth bound well, derive \(dA/dE\) and infer the local energy spacing. Apply it to a 100 kHz oscillator and express spacing in both joules and electronvolts.

Work through the solution

The endpoint derivative terms vanish because \(p=0\) at both ends. Inside, \(\partial p/\partial E=m/p=1/v\), so \(dA/dE\) equals the classical one-way travel time \(T_{\rm cl}/2\). From the quantisation rule increasing \(n\) by one gives local spacing \(\Delta E\simeq2\pi\hbar/T_{\rm cl}=hf_{\rm cl}\). At 100 kHz this is \(6.626\times10^{-29}\) J or \(4.136\times10^{-10}\) eV. In an anharmonic potential the classical period generally depends on energy; use the local slope or integrate between adjacent solutions rather than assuming a universal ladder spacing.

Check whether the Airy overlap interval exists

For a model atom with mass \(1.44\times10^{-25}\) kg let \(F=1.0\times10^{-21}\) N at the turning point. Find the Airy length. If \(|V''|=1.0\times10^{-16}\,\mathrm{N/m}\), estimate the relative quadratic correction at \(5\ell\).

Work through the solution

The length is \((\hbar^2/2mF)^{1/3}\simeq3.38\times10^{-8}\) m. The ratio of quadratic potential term to linear term at \(5\ell\) is \(|V''|5\ell/(2F)\simeq0.00845\). Thus several Airy lengths can fit in a region with small curvature correction for these numbers. The parameter \(|x-x_t|/\ell=5\) should still be checked against the desired asymptotic precision; the result supports an overlap interval but does not guarantee a prescribed decimal accuracy. Bringing a second turning point within a few such lengths changes the matching problem qualitatively.

Compare a triangular action with a rectangular shortcut

A linearly falling barrier has initial forbidden wavenumber \(\kappa_0/\hbar=3.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) and width \(d=2.0\) nm. Compare its exponential estimate with that of a rectangle using initial height throughout. What would a rectangle at the average potential height predict?

Work through the solution

For the triangle, \(\kappa(x)=\kappa_0\sqrt{1-x/d}\), so \(I=(2/3)(\kappa_0/\hbar)d=4.0\), giving \(e^{-8}=3.35\times10^{-4}\). The initial-height rectangle gives \(I=6\) or \(e^{-12}=6.14\times10^{-6}\). Averaging the potential height first gives wavenumber \(\kappa_0/\sqrt2\), \(I=4.24\) and \(e^{-8.49}\simeq2.06\times10^{-4}\). The action averages the square root of the local energy gap, not the gap itself. Agreement in mean barrier height is insufficient for predicting an exponential observable. Exact matching or a direct numerical probability current would be needed to determine the prefactor.
